FOREWORD
We are proud to present the vigorous efforts of scientific research fellows and teachers of the academic institutes and universities of Mongolia in this collective work entitled “Xiongnu Encyclopedia.” This encyclopedia consists of short articles on Xiongnu related historical records, especially ancient Chinese documents, as well as archaeological sources from Mongolia and neighboring territories. This historical research of the above mentioned records includes articles on general Xiongnu history, the main historical figures of the Xiongnu and neighboring Chinese states, and the important historical-geographical locations related to Xiongnu history. This encyclopedia also emphasizes significant archaeological findings that demonstrate the particular and distinctive aspects of Xiongnu culture. Larger articles dedicated to Xiongnu material culture, religious beliefs, rites and rituals, and economic activities are not only informative of steppe society but also of scientific importance.
Scientific fellows of the Institute of Archaeology and the Institute of History of the Mongolian Academy of Sciences along with the Department of Archaeology and Anthropology of the National University of Mongolia and the National Museum of Mongolia have contributed to this work. This encyclopedia represents the largest ever comprehensive collection of studies on Xiongnu Civilization and the first work in which a complete millennia of the Mongolian History is centered on the Xiongnu. Thus, this work is not merely limited to discussions of history, culture, and imperial expansion of the Xiongnu political entity during the 3rd century BCE to 2nd century CE; it also covers the subsequent periods and cultures, such as the Xianbei, Rouran (Nirun) and ancient Türks, whose nomadic polities drew significantly upon the Xiongnu heritage. In particular, this work touches upon the important post-Xiongnu period of nomadic states and historical figures of the 2nd-4th centuries CE, which has rarely been directly addressed until now. This work, though compiled by the efforts of scholars within Mongolia, represents growing efforts to expand the chronological and geographical scopes of Xiongnu historical and archaeological investigations beyond previous limitations of the formal imperial period and boundaries of the modern nation of Mongolia in order to more fully understand the Xiongnu historical phenomenon.
This encyclopedia is the third book in a series of publications, following the significant works of the Xiongnu Treasures catalog and the research compilation entitled Some Issues of Xiongnu Archaeology and History which was edited by the Institute of Archaeology, MAS. These works demonstrate the development of Xiongnu studies in Mongolia, the core homeland of the Xiongnu.
My special thanks go to the team of authors and translators and to the initiator of this project, Associate Professor Dr.Tsagaan Turbat, and his collaborators. At the end of this long project, I wish to express my deep hope that this work will further our cause to popularize and more broadly disseminate interest in the history and civilization of the Xiongnu, the ancestors of modern-day Mongolians.
Director of the Institute of Archaeology,
Honorary Scientific Fellow of Mongolia,
Professor, Doctor (Sc.D)
Dandinsuren Tseveendorj

A
BAKAN. Xiongnu period settlement in Khakasia, Russian Federation. An ancient ruin of a place is located in the confluence of the Tasheba and Abakan Rivers, close to the capital of the Khakasia Republic of the Russian Federation. The site was discovered in 1940 and continuously
studied by different researchers. Even though some parts of the site was destroyed by road construction, main part is still in in-tact and giving the possibilities to reconstruct the structure and construction. There are a number of construction ruins within the outer rampart; including the outer basic wall in height of 0.5 m, in thickness of 1.8 m, The rampart has 35 m from the north to the south, as well as 45 m from the west to the east. Nevertheless the Xiongnu period datation of the site and the Chinese character of the architecture has any more doubt, the attribution of it to particular historic figure has far more than unique opinions. Most accepted attributions are the Li Lin, the general of Han and Xiongnu, Yimo, the Xiongnu Xiubu Dang wang, and the Yun, the daughter of Huhanye Chanyu and his Chinese spouse Wang Zhaojun. (Ts.E.)
AFUJILO (492-496). One of the rulers of Tele. A subject of the Rouran Empire initially. Even though he frequently asked Deulun, an emperor of Rouran, not to make a fight against Tabgach Wei dynasty, he did ignore his warning. So Afujilo mounted a campaign and moved to the Irtysh River
leading his tribes. He ruled 100.000 households. He broke down the Yueban tribe of Xiongnu origin and occupied the Semirechiye region. Afujilo divided his state into two parts, ruled the North personally and then granted himself a title “The Great Emperor”. In 496, his state was destroyed by the Ephthalits. (Ts.T.)
AGRICULTURE. In the chapter 110 of the Shiji, writes about Xiongnu that they moved in pursuit of best pastures and water and they had no any walled cities or permanent settlement. Even though they did not practice agriculture, each of them had separate domains. Some ancient Chinese sources note that they did not build permanent or city and did not run any agriculture too. As a result of these facts, most scholars and researchers have been aware of the same thing just like above mentioned. Kh. Perlee, as a discoverer and researcher of the Xiongnu cities and settlements, emphasizes about the Xiongnu those Chinese historians, and researchers especially some modern orientalists, wrote that the Xiongnu had had no city and collected reserves and goods by the second half of 3rd c. BCE, they moved following water for all the year and firstly proposed a dogmatics about – “Asian pure nomads”. One of the Mongolian senior archeologists, Ts.Dorjsuren suggested that above mentioned theory of absolute nomadism of Xiongnu is likely based from a note in Chinese sources that: “The Xiongnu has been considered to be merely
Abakan settlement reconstruction
Agricultural Tools of Xiongnu



A
6
herdsmen. They always moved in pursued of water and pasture, with their livestock animal”. However, Soviet scholar S.I.Kiselev underlined “The unlimited dominance of this doctrine caused many researchers and scholars to ignore the facts and evidences of the existences of settlements in the land of present day Mongolia”. Another Soviet scholar, A.V.Davidova drew a conclusion on the materials of the Ivolga settlement, dated to the Xiongnu period that “According to the research work, the inhabitants had run out the agriculture since ancient times, so that it is impossible to say that in Central Asia only nomads were pastured the land”. Most researchers and scholars say that these few notes “The Xiongnu were a merely nomadic tribe” in Chinese sources, must be dated to the early period of Xiongnu-China relationship. In other words, it can be reflect the period of the pre-settlement in Xiongnu. Kh.Perlee noted that new discoveries of cities or settlements in Mongolia does not fit with these information recorded in Chinese sources, and emphasized the new discoveries of settlements which is mentioned in these narratives. Also a Mongolian scholar, G.Sukhbaatar said “The saying in Shiji, that the Xiongnu do not run out agriculture, is to say that they mainly run out animal husbandry but not to say they never practiced out agriculture”. Most of the Xiongnu scholarship agree with these point of view. The Chinese source indicates that in 119 BCE, Wei Qin, a Han general reached Zhao Xin cheng city in the Tianyan Mountain, ate the grain, stored by Xiongnu there and burned rest of them all. This stored grain noted in the source is bristlegrass, a sort of millet. A fact dated to the year of 100 BCE says about there was a big hole to store grain in Chanyu’s palace and in the note of 90 BCE says about livestock animal were perished, people were ill in mass and the grain did not grow because of heavy snowfall for months. We have some other direct and indirect information concerning Xiongnu agriculture. For example: in around 83 BCE or in early
period of the reign of Huyanti Chanyu, there were revolts of local noblemen and attacks from Han Dynasty, as the Chanyu was too young and was illegally throned as an Chanyu and his mother yanzhi was not reputable. At that time, the advisor of the palace, Wei Liu gave an advice to build a city with wells and wall surrounding it, to erect a tower for storing grains and to protect the city together with the Qin people. In this case, the soldiers of Han Dynasty could do nothing. In addition to it, Zhao Xin cheng city, destroyed by the soldiers of Han in 119 BCE, can be noted. However, the agriculture was developed sufficiently, and eventually not able to cover all food consumption in Xiongnu. Xiongnu took many measures to meet demand for the agriculture products. Judging from the facts above mentioned, at first, they tried to develop the local agriculture, secondly, they tried to control some south-western city-states which complex agricultural-pastoral economy for the purpose of supplying their needs. A fact related to it, is noted in Han shu, that since the allied troops of many citystates in the west, mounted an attack on Cheshi city state-an ally of Xiongnu in 66 BCE, the Xiongnu were in disgust and decided to send both of east and west Da Qians with over 10.000 soldiers for each, to attack Wusun and its domains and to guard themselves from them or to take up a quarter and to run agriculture there too. According to the source, the west was a main place, where the Xiongnu wanted to extend its domains and according to the words of a military commander of Han, Xiyu (West region) originally depended on Xiongnu. Zhichu prince of the west wing of Xiongnu generally controlled the states in the West region and appointed a governor titled Dunpu Duwei, to take the products from agriculture as a tax or duty. We have no adequate materials to prove that the agricultural products, imported from the West regions played a major role in the Xiongnu economy. An interesting and uncommon objective fact concerning with the agriculture of Pre-Xiongnu is a mattock which was found from Xigoupan
Ceramic steamer of Boroo settlement



A
7
burial place in Inner Mongolia, dated to the period of the Warring States. This shows the agriculture was influential in the economy since the time of the Xiongnu culture formation. G.P.Sosnovsky and A.V.Davidova discovered and researched the main material of Xiongnu agricultural culture, in the Ivolga settlement. Also they found spade-2, ploughshare-1, reaper-1, bone mattock-1, mill stone-5 and some holes which may have stored grains. The tools are the findings from only 7 of 54 dwellings in Ivolga settlement, that means there were at least 7 families run agriculture. Two grain-thresher stones like above mentioned were found from the 105th dwelling in the Boroo settlement, in the North Mongolia, in 2007. In addition to them all, there are numbers of the tools used for agriculture such as 1 iron share of shovel just similar to one of the Ivolga settlement, found from 28th grave, Derestuy, Transbaikalia; 26 iron ploughshares and 1 iron reaper like the Ivo
lga’s, from the settlement of Xiongnu, Dureny, the South of Transbaikalia. G.P.Sosnovsky found seed of millet from 40th and 123rd graves, in the Ilmovaya Pad’ and one of “mongol amu” or millet (Panicium miliaceleum) from the graves of the Noyon Uul. Apart from the millet seed, found from the Ivolga settlement, discovery of two sorts of grain those are barley (Hordeum vulgare L.) and wheat (Triticum comactum, Triticum aestivum) is significant to advance the knowledge of sorts of grain. Of the findings-animal bones found from the Ivolga settlement, 92.5 per cent is of domestic animals and 7.5 per cent is of wild animals. The most interesting thing is the bones of pigs, which consisting 15 per cent of the bones of the domestic animals. It shows the residents of the Ivolga settle
ment had run sedentary animal husbandry. As a result, a question “Which people ran the sedentary agriculture in Xiongnu?” is regularly asked. There are two hypotheses that some of native Xiongnu or some settlers in Xiongnu in a certain reason ran the agriculture. But we have not had any fact to answer this disputable question today, although many Mongolian and foreign scholars and researchers say much about it. According to the present level of studies, some of the Xiongnu might have settled down in a place and become peasants and craftsmen. Of course, the main economy of the Xiongnu was a pastoral animal husbandry. But it can be said that the agriculture played an important part in their life and they tried to develop the agriculture for their own needs. As a model, the cities and settlements of Xiongnu helped for the development of sedentary civilization in Inner Asia. The Xiongnu did not prefer merely nomadic lifestyle but they, as a sedentary lifestyle, developed a complex enterprise
including the agriculture and craft which is considered to be the first step to supply their needs. Although we can not answer the question regarding how successful their attempt was, and which role the settlers played on the Xiongnu society, we can say that the Xiongnu were the initial nomadic tribe who develop the city with the purpose and plan. Being mostly incompatible between the information about the cities and settlement of Xiongnu, in written sources, and the settlements or sacrificial places, which have been discovered, as geographical location, shows that the cities and settlements were built in the land of Xiongnu at that time. But nowadays, some archeological research denies both of the view that the Xiongnu were really nomadic and Chinese source information, compared with their sedentary lifestyle, and
Grain-tresher stones

requires we should do research work in classification, in further. (Ts.T.)
AMARTUVSHIN Chunag. Mongolian archaeologist. Born in 1974 in Ulaanbaatar. He graduated in history and archaeology at the National University of Mongolia in 1995. In the same year he started to working at the Institute of History and since 2002 has been working as a senior research fellow, head of a Department at the Institute of Archaeology, Mongolian Academy of Sciences. In 2004, he received a PhD title on his dissertation titled “Elaborating the archaeological survey method” (on the example of the Egyin Gol area). Then in 2007 became an assistant professor of the Ulaanbaatar University. He published several monographs including “Archaeological monuments of the Egyin Gol basin” (with Ts.Turbat and U.Erdenebat, 2003), “Archaeological study conducted on the political structure of ancient nomads” (with W.Honeychurch, 2006), “Archaeological study conducted in Dundgovi province: Baga Gazryn Chuluu” (with W.Honeychurch et al., 2010). Moreover he published over 50 scientific articles and over 20 papers for conferences. During archaeological investigations conducted at Egyin Gol (1996-2000), Khutag-Undur soum, Bulgan province, at Baga Gazryn Chuluu (2003-2008), Dundgovi province, in the Umnugovi province territory he discovered and studied many burials and settlements of the Xiongnu period. (Ch.Ye.)
ANAHUAN (520 or 522-552). Last emperor of Rouran. Also named Amgai. He was ascended the throne as an Emperor in 520, but descended very soon due to a rebellion. Then he was reigned as an Emperor again with the honored title “Tengert mendtei (that is “(A person who) has a destiny to make a greeting to the Heaven” as meaning)”. He defeated the rebels and made the state pacify. The disintegration of Wei Dynasty in 534, caused by an uprising, brought an advantageous situation to his empire. However, in 552, the fact that Turkic vassals of Rouran called to ask a marriage and heard a negative answer, was the reason of their revolt and his suicide. (Ts.T.)
“ANCESTORS OF THE MONGOLS” (Social structure, Culture and Ethogenesis of the Xiongnu 4th century BCE- 2nd century CE), Author G.Sukhbaatar, Ulaanbaatar, 1980, 298 pages. A monographe on Xiongnu study. Structure: Introduction, 5 chapters, conclusion, and appendix. The author used the historical and archaeological sources and scientific works widely for his monograph.
He considers that Xiongnu society was the whole organism and he attempted to describe whole problems of socio-economic, cultural, and ethnogenesis of the Xiongnu. Also studied Xiongnu economic basis animal husbandry and its methodology and he wrote that: Xiongnu had classic nomadic economy and they loaded some cultural elements from neighboring countries and besides they extended their culture. The main achievement of this monograph, author studied Xiongnu ethnogenesis and clarified the ethnic linkage between Xiongnu and later Xianbei, Rouran, Turk, Kidan, Uighur and the Mongols. He confirmed that Xiongnu, Rouran and Xianbei have ethnic linkage of Mongol nation and have relationship of socioeconomic and cultural development. G.Sukhbaatar confirmed by historical facts that, Xiongnu believe in shamanism and live in Mongolian nomadic lifestyle, thus, Mongolia have been developed by separate socio-economic formation way since Xiongnu period. (Na.B.)
ANHOU River. The name of a river noted in the historical records in connection with Xiongnu. De Groot and G. Sukhbaatar say that the Anhou River is the present day Orkhon River, while L.A.Borovkova says that was an another small river. This name was firstly noted in history from the period of Han-Xiongnu war in 90 BCE. The Xiongnu Chanyu moved the subjects to the north of the Qi Jiu River in the north of Zhao Xin cheng city and came back together with his elite cavaliers to wait the Han army crossing the Guce River, in the east of the Anhou River. Later, the Northern Xiongnu Chanyu was settled down in the west part of the Anhou River for a while in end of 80s CE. (B.B.)
ANIMAL OFFERING CUSTOM. Historical records and archeological materials providing plenty of information that Central Asian nomads not only constructed the burial structures for their deceased but also built up particular and separate structures for offering and sacrifices. The Mongol word “Khoilogo” designating the meanings “to offer, to sacrifice and to let accompany the death” is explained in different ways. Of them, the explanation making the interposing with a word “Yavuulakh” (to let someone go) could be nearer to the truth. According to the research, they made an offering and let the dead accompanied by offering animals since the Bronze Age. Consequently, it is a proof that the animal husbandry became a main economic activity of the ancient nomads in Mongolia. Also all the different type and time period graves like



A
9
Kereksur, Slab burial, Pazyryk, Xiongnu elite and ordinary people graves, Turkic, Uighur and Middle Age tombs, have been followed by the animal offering rituals all the ages through keeping the tradition on one hand but being renewed on the other hand. In some degrees the animal offering custom has been handed down to the present Mongolian burial custom. Nevertheless, the Xiongnu people launched to put some parts of the horse or other domestic animals as the representation of a whole animal. They put away a horse, flayed its skin, and wrapped its bones including head, neck, back, four legs, and enfolded up the five entrails such as heart, lung, gullet and liver in the skin. This is named “Zuld”. They put the zuld in the grave considering that it was a representation of the whole animal. Also non-domestic animals such as bird, antelope, deer and wild-ass were put to accompany the deceased. An interesting custom was the dog offering. The Xiongnu Graves accompanied by a dog have been found at the Egyin Gol, Baruun Khairkhan and Derestuy. (Ts.T.; No.B.)
ANSI River. The name of a river noted in the historical records in connection with Xiongnu. In Han shu, written in 82 CE by Ban Gu, tells us the fact that Gegun (Kyrgyz) was located in 7000 li in distance, in the west of the West Palace of Xiongnu Chanyu but 5000 li in the north of the Cheshi. Nevertheless, Wei shi, copied in The Chronicle of Three Dynasties reveals that there was Gegun located in distance of 7000 miles from the Ansi River where the Palace of Xiongnu Chanyu was in, that means the Palace of Xiongnu Chanyu was in the Ansi River. Some Inner Mongolian researchers say this is the Ertysh River, actually in the North Xinjiang, but has not been proved yet today. (B.B.)
AR TSAIDAM tombs. Xiongnu period tombs in China. In 1972, the local peasants accidentally found some findings such as a gold diadem and a gold belt decoration, in a place named Ar Tsaidam, Hyangan banner, Ikh Zuu aimag, Inner Mongolia. On the basis of these findings, this area was surveyed and two more graves were discovered, which were later excavated. 218 gold pieces were discovered from the excavation of these tombs and surface chance findings in the vicinity, including a gold diadem with a bird figure, gold belt buckles set with jade, a gold ring, and a gold decoration with an animal head image of unknown purpose. These graves had been damaged by sand moving and lost their initial burial structure. According to the comparative analysis of the burial objects, they
have been proved to be dated to the Xiongnu in later period of Warring States or 4-3rd c. BCE. (Ch.S.)
“ARCHAEOLOGICAL STUDY OF NORTHERN STEPPE-BRONZE AGE TO EARLY IRON AGE”. Author Wu En (Unen-Yosutu). Beijing, 2007. A monograph about the nomadic culture of Bronze Age to Early Iron Age in the Northern Steppe and neighboring regions. The author studied systematically archeological cultures of Lower layer culture of Xiajiadian, culture of Datoutou, culture of Zhukaigou, culture of Weiyingzi, Upper layer culture of Xiajiadian, culture of Maochingou which founded in the Northern steppe, and cleared the relationship between cultures of Mongolia, Transbaikalia and Central Asia. Moreover origin of nomadic culture, development and varying, and influence between nomadic and settled civilization terminated in this work. (Ch.S.)
ARCHAEOLOGICAL RESEARCH OF THE XIONGNU. Archaeological research of the Xiongnu was commenced by Yu.D.Taliko-Grintsevich, doctor of Kiakhta, a Mongolian-Russian border town, during late 19th century. As an amateur archaeologist, he has dug approximately 100 graves of the Xiongnu period in Dzida, Selenge, Chikoi, and Khilga river basins. He attempted to classify the graves he dug into the tombs with “log chamber” and the tombs with “wooden coffin” and he concluded them to be referred to different periods. As his view, the “log chamber” graves might belong to the Xiongnu, whereas the “wooden coffin” tombs to be referred to the Kirghiz and the Tungus peoples. However, later, all those were revealed to be of the Xiongnu tombs. He has found out a number of the Xiongnu graves distributed along Transbaikalia and published extensive excavation reports. These are including 33 “log chamber” graves excavated in Ilmovaya Pad’, the 26 “wooden coffin” graves excavated in Derestuyski Kultuk by the left bank of Dzida river, and the different tombs excavated in the Khar-Us, Gudjirmyge, Orgoiton, Urgun Khundii, Tsaram, Usti-Kiakhta (Bor, Uval, Lipovski), Sudji, Burdun, Edui, and Khuurai bulag. Although his researches were not evaluated properly at his time and could not draw attention of the scientific public, researchers were interested in his works following the great discovery of the Noyon Uul Xiongnu elite tombs. This influenced for further researches on Xiongnu archaeological monuments by BuryatMongolian archaeological expedition (19281929), program led by an archaeologist of Irkutsk, G.P.Sosnovski. His expedition has dug 11 graves



A
10
in the Ilmovaya Pad’ (which has more than 300 graves), the biggest cemetery of the Xiongnu in the Transbaikalia, and started to research settlemet at Nijniy Ivolga near Ulan-Ude city. He proved researchers’ thesis and hypothesis on the common origin of the Mongolian and Transbaikalian graves after conducting comparative study on the materials of his own excavations at Ilmovaya Pad’ and Ivolga and the excavation materials of Derestuy by Yu.D.Tal’ko-Gryntsevitch. In 1949, the 2nd Buryat-Mongolian archaeological expedition led by academician A.P.Okladnikov started to work in Transbaikalia. Within this expedition the following Xiongnu graves were found in such places as Khujir Dev by the right side of Dzida river, and Cheremukhovay Pad’ nearby Ilmovaya Pad’ etc. Later in 1957, A.P.Okladnikov also led the excavation in the Ilmovaya Pad’. An influential contribution from RussianSoviet scholarship to the Xiongnu archaeological research was a book titled “The Xiongnu culture and the Noyon Uul graves”, published in 1962 by professor S.I.Rudenko. The fact that the almost whole excavation materials of P.K.Kozlov were included and analyzed in the book was essential contribution for the research of the Xiongnu archaeology and culture. Buryat archaeologist P.B.Konovalov significantly contributed to our understanding of the Xiongnu archaeological culture. Since 1960s, P.B.Konovalov conducted the research of Xiongnu common people and some elite graves in Transbaikalia and published a fundamental monograph
based on his research and excavation materials. Between 1965-1971, P.B.Konovalov dug 41 graves in total in Transbaikalia (one of them was square shaped elite tomb). His work was the first attempt to make syntheses issue the Xiongnu establishment, social structure, origination on grave sites in comprehensive ways. His scientific excavation techniques, methods, cohesive determinations, high qualified freehand sketch, drawings, and appendices are highly appreciated by researchers. Between 1949-1950, a cemetery-settlement complex, “Nijnyi Ivolga” was being researched by V.P.Shilov and since 1956, it was carried on by A.V.Davidova who has finished excavation the complex. As for the grave which is considered one of the biggest ones excavated completely (has 216 graves with earthen cover), it is invaluable significant material for the study of Xiongnu economy and social structure. Thousands of artifacts found from the Ivolga settlement became an important resource to the study of Xiongnu ecomony, culture and art related history. Since 1980, Petersburg researcher, S.S.Minyaev, has been researching the Xiongnu graves in Transbaikalia. S.S.Minyaev has excavated and researched in many different sites of the Xiongnu along Dureny settlement, Derestuy and Tsaram grave sites. In 1998 S.S.Minyaev published a monograph of the excavation to Deresuy common graves. His thorough research into practices of circular graves of the Xiongnu, and his innovations and implications or conclusion is appreciated. S.S.Minyaev started to excavate a big terrace tomb
Xiongnu bone arrow-heads



A
11
in Tsaram cemetery near the Kiakhta town since late 1990 and finished recently. According to P.B.Konovalov, as there are 866 more graves of the Xiongnu in Transbaikalia, 369 of them have been excavated during 18961971. 4 of the biggest ones were excavated. In 1983, this figure rose up to 377. According 1996’s registration, 30 cemetery, over 470 excavated graves were registered. Recently approximately 5000 graves of the Xiongnu over 100 sites across Mongolia were registered and 500 out of them were excavated. It is undeniable that this number accounts for a few percent of the total graves of the Xiongnu. With the P.K. Kozlov excavation of elite tombs of the Xiongnu at Noyon Uul, Batsumber soum, Tuv aimag during 1924/25, the research of Xiongnu tombs started in Mongolia. The result of Kozlov’s excavation of 1924 drew public attention so that S.A.Teploukhov, G.I.Borovka, professional archaeologists of the Soviet Academy of Sciences were mobilized to work on the site. They have excavated the tomb No. 24 with the respect of scientific methods, which allows us until recently to understand the construction, structure and meaning of Xiongnu terrace elite tombs. In 1924-1925, when 6 elite tombs, 4 small graves were excavated, hundreds of considerably significant research findings were found, which drew the world’s scientific public attention and highly influenced Mongolian archaeology and the Xiongnu archaeology development. The result of excavation of the Noyon Uul tombs turned into a number of articles and books, while, S.Umehara, Ts.Dorjsuren, S.I.Rudenko’s works have not still lost the importance.
In 1926, after G.I.Borovka get returned some of the findings of early years to Mongolian Commission of Science in accordance with the contract, he made independent excavation at Noyon Uul. As he excavated a grave nearby Sujigtiin Am, it was not an elite burial as he expected, but was a small grave with wooden coffin. Although the grave was looted early, it gave interesting information about interior structure of the Xiongnu grave. Findings from this excavation consists of 3 types of ceramic potteries, bone made bowl, lacquer box, horse shaped bronze plaques, 3 bronze bridles, and horse bit etc. A.D.Simukov, a former research fellow of the Mongolian Commission of Science, excavated an elite tomb in Sujigtiin Am, and Juramtyn Am valley at Noyon Uul in 1927. Among number of interesting findings, lacquered wine glass with Chi
Excavation of Gol Mod site



A
12
nese characters, similar to the finding of the Tomb No. 6 was found, which gave an opportunity to determine the chronology of graves of Noyon Uul. Since 1950s, Mongolian national archaeological specialists were begin to conduct independent researches of the Xiongnu graves. In 1953, O.Namnandorj excavated some Xiongnu graves at Gashuun Gol, Khashaat soum, Arkhangai aimag and at Inget Tolgoi, Selenge aimag, where he found human bones and pottery. Since 1954, Ts. Dorjsuren has conducted excavations of the Xiongnu grave site in different major Xiongnu burial sites. During 1954-1957, he excavated an elite grave of the Xiongnu at Noyon uul and 15 small graves. Moreover he excavated an elite tomb at Gol Mod, Erdenemandal soum, Arkhangai aimag, and 26 small graves. During these excavations, hundreds of interesting findings were found such as weapons, labor instruments, tools, horse equipments, metal decorations, ceramic and bronze utensils, which allowed him to clarify some issues regarding with the Xiongnu grave structure and practices. Later, he has published a monograph titled “The Northern Xiongnu” (UB.,1961), which is considered as a must to read book on Xiongnu society, economy, culture, and other historical issues, written on the basis of the Xiongnu archaeological research resources conducted in Mongolia and Transbaikalia. Since 1960s, Ts.Dorjsuren carried out researches of the Xiongnu grave sites in cooperation with national and foreign researchers. In 1961,
during his expedition with V.V.Volkov in Khovd aimag, he found a big cemetery of the Xiongnu in the locality named Khaalgatyn Zavsar or Takhiltyn Khotgor in Mankhan soum, where they excavated 2 graves. Furthermore, he excavated and researched 2 graves with Hungarian archaeologist, I.Erdelyi in Khujirtyn Am of Noyon Uul and published an article. In 1961, Mongol-Hungarian joint team headed by N.Ser-Odjav, Ts.Dorjsuren and T.Horovat excavated 2 graves in Selbe Youth camp, near Ulaanbaatar. In 1970s N.Ser-Odjav excavated a grave belongs to the Xiongnu period in Khutag Uul mountain, Khashaat soum, Arkhangai aimag. Between 1963-1964, Mongol-Hungarian joint expedition headed by I.Erdelyi and D.Navaan
k
r
Gol Mod-II site, Tomb 1, plan.



A
13
published preliminary results of their research on 4 graves at Naimaa Tolgoi, Erdenemandal soum, Arkhangai aimag. In 1969 Soviet academician A.P.Okladnikov visited several disturbed graves near Darkhan city, on his way to Ulaanbaatar. The same year remaining 6 graves were excavated and researched by P.Kherlee, and Yu.S.Grishin. In 1969 Mongolian archaeologists excavated a grave at Tsagaan Chuluut, Gurvanzagal soum, Dornod aimag and 5 Xiongnu graves at Duulga Uul, Khentii aimag. In 1971, V.V.Volkov and D.Navaan excavated 2 of 21 graves at Tevsh Uul mountain, Uvurkhangai aimag and a grave at Khutag Uul mountain, Khashaat soum, Arkhangai aimag. In 1977, while excavation nearby Salkhit railway station, 5 graves were found and excavated by D.Navaan and others.
Between 1980-1982, the Bronze and Iron age research team of the Mongol-Soviet Joint Historical and Cultural Expedition (MSJHCE) excavated over 40 graves of the Xiongnu period in Arkhangai, Bulgan, Selenge, Tuv aimags, however, the results have not been published yet. Between 1989-1990, D.Navaan excavated 2 elite tombs of the Xiongnu in Mankhan soum, Khovd aimag. Since 1970s, Mongolian archaeologist D.Tseveendorj conducted excavations of Xiongnu graves in different sites throughout Mongolia. In 1972, and 1977 he excavated 8 graves at Tevsh Uul, in 1973, 1981; 4 graves at Chandmani Uul, Uvs aimag; in 1974, 5 graves with I.Erdelyi at Naimaa Tolgoi, Erdenemandal soum, Arkhangai aimag; in 1987, 11 graves, in 1975, 3 graves at Nukhtiin Am, Galt soum, Khuvsgul aimag; in 1986, 5 graves at Khirgist Khooloi, Tsogt soum, Gobi-Altai aimag, a grave at Onitolt, Uyanga soum, Uvurkhangai aimag; in 1987, with I.Erdelyi, 2 graves at Khudagyn Tolgoi, Battsengel soum, Arkhangai aimag, 2 at Solbi Uul, 11 graves at Naimaa Tolgoi; in 1988, with I.Erdelyi, 5 graves in Duulga Uul, 5 graves at Zaraa Tolgoi, Bayan soum, Tuv aimag; in 1989, with P.K.Konovalov and I.Erdelyi, 2 graves in Morin Tolgoi, Altanbulag soum, Tuv aimag, a grave at Baga Gazryn Chuluu, Delgertsogt soum, Dundgobi aimag, a grave at Khuushiin Khotol, Uvurkhangai aimag; and in 1990, 2 graves of the Xiongnu in Altanbulag soum, Tuv aimag. The result of these excavations were published systematically. He came to various interesting conclusions on the Xiongnu archaeological research issues, including the thesis of the similitude of funeral rites of Xiongnu common people throughout whole Mongolia. Among hundreds of findings found from these excavation, we could emphasize some graved vases and pots, bronze mirrors and coins, bronze cauldrons, ancient musical instrument – Jew’s harp. He published a research paper with Z.Batsaikhan on the analysis for the Xiongnu 110 ceramic pots archived at the Archaeological laboratory of the Institute of History. Since early 1990s, Z.Batsaihan started to research the Xiongnu grave
Duurlig nars site, Tomb 51, plan.



A
14
sites. In 1991 he excavated a grave in Delgerkhaan Uul, Tuvshinshiree soum, Sukhbaatar aimag; between 1992-1996, 18 graves at Burkhan tolgoi, Khutag Undur soum, Bulgan aimag; and late 1990s some graves of the Xiongnu in Delgerkhaan Uul, Tuvshinshiree soum, Sukhbaatar aimag and in Duulga Uul, Jargaltkhaan soum, Khentii aimag. He defended a dissertation entitled “The Northern Xiongnu and the Development of the Nomadic Civilization” in 2000. In following years he conducted excavations of Xiongnu burials in Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu, Ugiinuur soum, Arkhangai aimag, and some graves in Emeel Tolgoi, Battsengel soum. In 2003 he published a monograph “The Xiongnu (archaeology, ethnography, history)” presenting current results and achievements of Xiongnu study in Mongolia. Between 1994-1999, Mongol-French joint archaeological expedition excavated 78 tombs out of 96 (which have 106 graves in total) at Burkhan Tolgoi, Bulgan aimag. It was the first case in which such a big cemetery of the Xiongnu has been excavated completely in Mongolia. As a result of the excavation, considerable amount of interesting and new information about funeral practices of the Xiongnu were accumulated and hundreds of findings were revealed. It should be emphasized that among the findings there were golden adornments with jewels and precious stones, bronze and iron pots, tens of vases and pots, horse outfits, bone made chopsticks, and iron and bone arrowheads. Ts.Turbat, Ch.Amartuvshin, U.Erdenebat published the results of the study of the Burkhan Tolgoi, Kholtost Nuga, Khahan Khad, Khunkheriin Am cemeteries of Xiongnu period in 2003. Furthermore, leader of the French party of this joint expedition, Prof. P.-H.Giscard published a mono
graph on funeral practices of the Xiongnu people based on the excavations of 58 grave materials. In 2000 Mongol-Korean joint archaeological expedition conducted excavation on a Xiongnu grave at the Morin Tolgoi site in Tuv aimag and published their report as a research book. In 2001 Mongol-Belgium joint expedition excavated 4 graves with circular stone cover at the Xiongnu cemetery of Uvgunt in Buregkhangai soum of Bulgan aimag. In same year Ts.Turbat exacavated and published his report on excavation of two small graves out of 370 at Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu in Ugiinuur soum of Arkhangai aimag. These tombs have delivered a ceramic object in shape of a cup, a big pottery which is comparable with large vessels from elite tombs at Noyon Uul, with narrow brim and hole near bottom, a complete bronze mirror and other interesting objects. Since 2000 Mongolian-French joint archaeological expedition started working on the research which Ts.Dorjsuren commenced in 1950s in the territory of Gol Mod, in Hunui river valley, Arkhangai aimag. Here they identified 420 graves, and excavated 3 big elite tombs and 13 satellite graves. Although these graves were all looted in the antiquity, hundreds of interesting and new materials were revealed, including complete bronze and silver equipments of ceremonial chariot, gold and silver tournament for coffin, felt rug, silk, bronze and iron pots and pans, and crystal made items and belongings. In 2001, Mongolian-American joint expedition discovered a big Xiongnu cemetery of elites at God Mod, Undur-Ulaan soum, Arkhangai aimag. As there were 452 graves, 28 satellite graves were excavated and researched. In 2003-2009, Mongolian-American joint expedition excavated 30 graves of the Xiongnu



A
15
people in Baga gazaryn chuluu, Delgertsogt soum, Dundgobi aimag, and published the results in 2011. Many objects, including unique items from far western glass amulets were unearthed from this excavation. Since 2006, within the framework of Mongolian-Korean joint project “Monsol”, large scale excavation has been carried out in a big cemetery of the Xiongnu in Duurlig Nars, Bayan-Adraga soum, Khentiy aimag. An elite tomb, 2 small graves were excavated. From the tomb No. 2 of Duurlig Nars, some interesting objects such as ceramic bowl, platter, tea-pot, mirror, bronze items, jade made objects, precious stones, golden bead necklace, small and big potteries, iron spoon, horse equipments made of metal, a chariot, sacrificed animal’s bones. TLV shaped bronze mirror which was found from the grave, has become important tool for determining the chronology of this cemetery. In 2009 an elite tomb, and 4 small graves were excavated. In 2010-2011 one of the biggest elite tombs has been excavated. In 2006 Mongol-Russian joint expedition has begun research on tombs of the Xiongnu elites at Noyon Uul. In 2006 they excavated an elite tomb of the Xiongnu in Sujigtyn am. The burial chamber has been found in 18.3 meters depth. Amazing number of unique and high class artistic objects were found, including bronze and silver equipments of a chariot with parasol, felt rug, bowl with Chinese characters, and bronze utensils. Among those objects was a embossed silver disc decoration on which depicted European mythic characters that is considered as a peculiar finding regarding with the Xiongnu trade and cultural relationships with war western regions. In 2009, Mongol-Russian joint archaeological team excavated
another big elite tomb numbered No.31. Such findings as precious woolen knitted carpet, lots of gold, silver, bronze made items were found from the excavation. Since 2007, Mongolian National Museum and Pennsylvania University of the joint research team excavated at Takhiltyn Khotgor an elite tomb and 7 satellite graves and published the result in series of papers. (D.Ts.; Ts.T.)
“ARCHEOLOGICAL RESEARCH ON THE ORIGIN OF XIONGNU, IN MONGOLIAN PEOPLE’S REPUBLIC”. Scientific article on Xiongnu study in Chinese. Published in the Menggu kaogu yu wenwu. 1993, No. 1, 2. (A publication in two series). Author Lin Yuan. He did comparative research on historical and cultural monuments of ancient nomads in north part of Hebei province, China, in the south part of the Yingshan Mountain, Ordos and Ningxia autonomous region, Inner Mongolia and in east part of Gansu province. Consequently, he drew a different conclusion from most researchers, that many nomadic tribes recorded in history before ancient Qin Dynasty, were not the ancestors of Xiongnu. Judging from the research on the skulls, found from the Xiongnu graves of the Noyon Uul and Shansunjizhai, Qinghai province, the Xiongnu were Mongoloid. However, a comparative research on the Xigoupan graves, Daodunzi graves, found in Ordos and Xiongnu graves of the Noyon Uul, reveals about a same custom, followed by the Xiongnu to bury someone together with their own horse’s head as well as a bow and arrow. (Ch.S.)
“ARCHAEOLOGICAL STUDY ON THE STATE STRUCTURE OF ANCIENT NOMADIC STATES.” Scientific work on Xiongnu
Khudgiin tolgoi site



A
16
studies in Mongolian. Authors W.Honeychurch and Ch. Amartuvshin. Published in Ulaanbaatar, 2006. Consisted of the Introduction, 8 chapters and annexes in 262 pages. The monograph explaining political, social and cultural history of ancient nomadic states based on the archaeological datas discovered in Egiin Gol river valley, North Mongolia. Through studies on the artifacts discovered from Xiongnu burials and settlements from this area the authors reconstructed the model explaining how to ordinary nomads living in remote places far from the center of empire had specific social status and contributed in the political stability to some extent. In addition, nomads from generation to generation relied on their internal forces to participate freely in the political and social relationship of much wider region, which in some cases, might had been main factor to accelerate the political processes (Na.B.)
ARROWHEAD. For the Xiongnu people the bows and arrows was the main weapon of longrange combats. As an evidence of that there are many arrowheads have been uncovered from the Xiongnu sites as well as kept at the most of the state and local museums. Though iron was widely used in the Xiongnu period, bronze continued to be used as well. The Xiongnu made arrowheads of bone, iron and bronze. The bronze arrowheads had two to three cutting edges, a tang, and a socket. Bronze and iron were often used together, and many arrows with bronze heads and iron shafts have been excavated from the Xiongnu settlement Bayanbulag in Nomgon soum territory, Umnugovi province.
An arrowhead made entirely of iron could have 2 to 4 cutting edges, a greater range, and more force and killing power than a bronze arrowhead. Such arrowheads were commonly from 8 to 10 cm in length. The Xiongnu seem to have been the first to make a whistling arrowhead. It was made by inserting a piece of bone with a hole in it into the arrowhead’s shaft socket. The wind passing through the hole generated the whistling sound, which served as a battle cry, and to panic the enemy and frighten his horses. Also the Xiongnu people designed arrowheads with barb in order to slay the enemy with snake poison and fire, and chopper arrowheads for chopping, cut off and/or in order to considerably injure and to pierce in a big area. Excavations have yielded bone arrowheads with two cutting edges and two sides, with tang, socket and clamp (to hold to the by shaft). Bows and arrows have not only been uncovered from burials of men but also have been recovered from burials of children and women. Recent studies on the common Xiongnu people burials show that 87% of arrowheads have been unearthed from men burials, 10% from women burials and 3% from children burials. Bronze and bone arrowheads were designed to be used against unarmored enemies, whereas iron arrowheads had greater penetrating
Iron arrowheads, Duurlig nars, Tomb 2
Bone arrowhead, Delgerkhaan uul



A
17
power and could be used against armored troops. Each arrowhead has its own distinctive characteristics and each had different functions and the ability to cause different types of injuries to humans and animals. Compared with arrowheads used by neighboring groups, the Xiongnu arrowheads are marked by greater size. (N.E.)
ART OF WAR AND MILITARY TACTICS. Chinese historical sources dated back to this period, reveal the records concerning the battles with the Xiongnu but not deliver any comprehensive accounts on their military art and tactics. Numbers of sources have different informations on the attack of the nomads to the small tribes settled down within the north border of China, the construction of signal towers and fortified walls to protect against them by Yan, Qin and Zhao States during 4th c. BCE, as well as the attack of a Chinese commander, Li Mu against the nomads in mid of 3rd c. BCE. On the other hand, no information on nomads military tactics has been found yet. However, the attack of the nomadic cavalry to China posed a great threat and caused to complete the construction of The Great Wall extended 4000 km from Liaodong to Gansu in 212 BCE. The beginning of the Xiongnu art of war and set of weaponry based to the Bronze Age and they developed through getting individual features. They handled three main types of military weapons: long range, short range and defensive weapons. They used the main weapons like long range curve bow, arrow, arrow-head in long distant fight but sword, spear and knife and wore iron or wooden shield and leather, felt and iron armor in short range fight (or melee). Other supplementary defense means are belt and guards for forearm and arm. The advanced tools and weapons dated to this period are whistling signal arrow, composite bow, plate armor and arm-guard. And the construction to securing and defense began to be built by the nomads at that time. Some information on their art of war and military tactics is briefly written in some sources dated back to the period after the establishment of Xiongnu Empire. After the Shiji, “As Tumen Chanyu did not want to raise his eldest son, Maodun as an Emperor; he pawned him to Yuezhi State. Then he launched a surprise attack on Yuezhi in order to get them to kill his eldest son. But Maodun managed to escape and met with his father. His father evaluated his quick wittedness and courage and granted a right to command the 10.000 cavalry unit”. These few sentences express that the Xiongnu had already the system of the tens as well as
the vigor and intelligence of Maodun, the next Chanyu. Since he commanded his soldiers under the strict discipline, trained them to follow his orders and renewed the weapons, he established the first Nomadic Empire Xiongnu, which would be raise in equal with Chinese Han dynasty. His strict character and great ability of management were the basis of the rapid development of the Xiongnu art of war and military tactics. Judging from the sources, Xiongnu army comprised of the light cavalry and they tried to conduct a combat through the way of retreatment, attack, ambush, feint and besieging with a show of retreating in united and scattered locations/positions as his military tactics. In addition to them, they investigated well the landscape before the battle, waged a sudden attack, broke up the enemy’s force and fell hail of arrows down. A clear example of these tactics is the battle of Baideng near Pincheng city in 200 BCE. According to Shiji, when Xiongnu firstly mounted an attack on China in 201 BC, Xiongnu had unified all the nomads “owning felt ger” of the East Eurasian steppe. At that time, Liu Bang (256-195 BCE) ruled Han Dynasty, newly established (in 206 BC) and commanded his army to fight against Xiongnu. The Xiongnu followed the tactics of combating, attacking, escaping and being chased in order to break up the enemy’s force, and besieged the leading troops together with Liu Bang near Pincheng city for a week. This was to let them understand there to be another empire which was more powerful than Han Dynasty. Other sources tell about Xiongnu that they were never ashamed of retreatment, scattered like splitting and disappeared like a cloud when they were defeated. Xiongnu Chanyu had a right to decide all the state and military affairs and commanded the army by himself. There was a military council consisted of high officers who give advices to Chanyu and commanded the army under the order of Chanyu. Maodun besieged Liu Bang army together with his 400.000 cavaliers, who were quartered with four different coat-colored horses in four directions: the soldiers on white horses were in the west, ones on horses with white patch on the forehead were in the east, ones on black horses were in the north and ones on grey horses were in the south. Identifying soldiers’ horses just like this, during the war or combat was either a kind of recognition signs or an expression of art of war and military power. From a note written by a historian of Han



A
18
Dynasty, there was one soldier for each five heads of Xiongnu population. Xiongnu men had to come on time when they were enlisted as soldiers. If not, they would be strictly sentenced to death. The Xiongnu soldiers were divided into the units of ten, hundred, thousand and ten thousand and regularly made a combat training. This unit was helpful for not only increasing the number of soldiers also arranging a regular army. There are some notes about the campaign in Shiji, that: “Xiongnu levied a tax from some states near Kangyu and Fergana (Dawan) in the west and had a relationship with Qian in the south... All the states of the West were shattered down and were dominated by Xiongnu. Xiongnu captured many countries and became more powerful”. In his letter sent to Han court in 174 BC, Maodun Chanyu noted that “We punished the West Tuqi Wang and sent him to the west against Yuezhi. By the virtue of the Heaven’s protection, all the commandants are healthy and the horses are strong. So we have met an opportunity to defeat them. I dominated all 26 nations including Loufan, Wusun, Huzhe and Yuezhi and they all joined Xiongnu. As a result, all the archer nations have united under the roof of one family.” Like these above mentioned, we can find out much more about Xiongnu art of war, used for unifying all the nomadic nations in Eurasian steppe, under one standard. (N.E.)
ATTILA (?-453 CE). The King of Western Xiongnu or Hun. Attila ruled from 434 until his death in 453, shared the power with his brother till 445. Most famous nomadic leader among the nomadic peoples invaded the Roman Empire. He and his brother Bleda inherited a large area from Alps and Baltic Sea to Black sea from his ancestors to govern. Attila started his invasion to Danube River in 441 and 443 CE because the Romans did not give the promised tributes. He killed his brother in 445. After successful invasion in Danube river and in states at the Balkan Peninsula he established a peace treaty and agreed to take high tributes from the Eastern Romans. He also attempted to
conquer Roman Gaul in 451 but he defeated by Roman general Flavius Aëtius and the Visigoths. His invasion of Italy in 452 was ended up with famine and epidemic. For some people the destruction made Attila was like a punishment of a god hence they called him as “God’s knout”. Probably Attila was killed by his bride on his wedding night. After his death his sons took the throne but collapsed in a short time. (Ts.T.)
ATTILA’S HUNNIC EMPIRE (Greek Hounnoi, Lat. Hunni). Hunnic Empire consisited of Xiongnu people came from Central Asia, and the local peoples of Ugr and Sarmatian origins between the 2nd and 4th century CE. In the 70s of the 4th century CE the Xiongnu people all together moved into west that caused to start the “The Great Migration of People”, as noted in the history. The Huns who led by Balamber defeated the Alan tribes in the northern Caucasia and in 375 overpowered the Goths in northern steppes of Black sea, they taking in their hand Ostrogoths and chased Visigoths into west. Then they centered in Pannoni, present-day Hungarian territory. During the reign of Attila the western Hun reached to the highest point of prosperity. At this time the Western Hunnic Empire stretched from the Ural River to the Rhine River and from the Danube River to the Baltic Sea. They not only took tributes from the conquered tribes but also received high tributes from the Eastern Roman Empire. In 451, by the leadership of Attila the Hunnic army crossed the border of the Roman Gaul but they were defeated at the Battle of the Catalaunian Plains. They were divided and defeated in 455 in the Battle of Nedao. The invasion to Balkans in 469 did not accomplish the goal and very soon the empire was collapsed. (Ts.T.)



B
19
B
AGA GAZARYN CHULUU tombs. Xiongnu period cemeteries of common people in Mongolia. Located in the Baga Gazaryn Chuluu mountain area, in Delgertsogt soum of Dundgobi aimag. During archaeological field survey there were found 220 Xiongnu burials in 11 cemeteries, such as Alag Tolgoi, Baga Mongol, Dund Shandyn Am, Duraal, Ikheriin Am, Khanan, Khukh Khad, Khuren Khond, Undur Khond, Tsagaan Ukhaa and Chandmani. Between 2003-2008, Mongol-American joint archaeological expedition conducted the excavation of 32 Xiongnu burials, including 9 at Alag Tolgoi, 2 at Dund Shandyn Am, 1 at Baga Mongol, 4 at Duraal, 1 at Ikheriin Am, 3 at Khanan, 2 at Khukh Khad, 5 at Khuren Khond, 2 at Undur Khond, 1 at Tsagaan Ukhaa and 2 at Chandmani Uul. The Tomb No.1 of Baga Mongol and the Tombs Nos.1 and 2 of Undur Khond are particulary
interesting by their peculiar structure of tomb as well as funeral practice from other Xiongnu burials. For example, the burials were located separately from other tombs and inside the tombs the deceased were placed in pits that are shallow than 100 cm and in stone boxes made of thin flat stones, on natural rock or flat rock floor. Also these burials did not contain some common elements of the Xiongnu funeral practice. These excavated burials delivered over 500 items, including horse equipment, bronze decorations, ceramics, birch bark vessels, bone
chopsticks, parts of lacquer cups, iron decorations of coffin, golden earrings, various stone beads, bone pendants, stone objects, silver ornaments, bronze, iron and bone arrowheads, iron knife, spear, axe, bone parts of a compound bow, and birch bark objects. These have thoroughly described in special chapter dedicated to Xiongnu period study in the book “Archaeological study conducted in the territory of Dundgovi aimag: Baga Gazaryn Chuluu” by Ch.Amartuvshin and P.Khatanbaatar. (Ch.A.)
Baga gazariin chuluu site
Baga gazariin chuluu site



B
20
BAIYAN Tribe. Historical tribe related to Xiongnu mentioned in Chinese chronicles. Judging from Chinese sources, an official, named Liu Jing paid a visit to Xiongnu as a messenger and reported to Gaozu “Baiyan and Loufan tribes of Xiongnu are inhabited in the south part of the Yellow River or in distance of 700 miles apart from the capital. It takes one day to reach Qinzhong on unarmored horses”. This report can be said to give some clear information or fact on their location. Some researchers say that Baiyan could be the ancestor of Bayud tribe who inhabited in the period of the Great Mongol Empire. (G.E.)
BALAMIR (in 360-378). A well-known ruler of Western Xiongnu who is recorded in history. His name is noted like Valamir or Balamber. In 370, the western Xiongnu crossed the Volga River, broke up the Alans in the North Caucasus and entered to Eastern Europe. In 371, they defeated the Gots on the north coast of the Black Sea and attacked to the west. In 378, he died after the defeat the Ostrogots. (Ts.T.)
BAN CHAO (32-102 CE). A state figure of the East Han dynasty and a famous Chinese general. He was a younger brother of a famous historian, Ban Gu. He participated in several battles against Xiongnu. Thanks to his command, the East Han managed to control the Tarim basin, Loulan, Hotan and Kashgar and protected the regions along the Silk Road, after the fight against Xiongnu for 31 years. He was a skillful politician who was able to be supported by the Kushan in order to mount an alliance against the Kangju for the Kashkaria. Afterwards, he won the combat caused by a conflict with the Kushan or Yuezhi in 90 CE. He was officially promoted as a governor in the Western region and stationed in Qiuci or Kucha city. In 91 CE, over 50 city-states joined Han Dynasty after conquering Yanqi or Kashgar. In 97 CE, he sent a messenger named Gan Yin to Parthian Empire, but the messenger reached only Persian Gulf. He retired due to illness in 102 CE. Since then, the Xiongnu dominated the Western region again. (Ts.T.)
BAN GU (32-92 CE). Chinese prominent historian and poet of 1st c. CE. The compiler of the Han Shu- The History of Han dynasty. Ban Gu was famous historian in the West Han imperial court but did not advanced well in official hierarchy. He served under Dou Xian, the General major and War minster and acted as a counselor on Xiongnu fighting committee. Although Dou
won prestige for two successful campaigns against the Xiongnu, in 92 CE he was suspected by Hedi Emperor of plotting a rebellion and forced to commit suicide. Immediately thereafter, Ban was dismissed from office and arrested by an old rival who was serving as the prefect of Luoyang. Ban died in prison that same year at 61 years old. His major work- Han Shu, especially the Chapter 94 contains large amount of important informations on Xiongnu culture and history. Although Ban Gu largely used Sima Qian works to compile Xiongnu chapter, it contains also fresh materials on the customs, social and political life of Xiongnu. (Ch.Ye.)
BARUUN DURUU (Bars Khot-II). A walled settlement of Xiongnu period, located in a place named Baruun Duruu, in distances of 7 km in the east of the Bars Khot-I and of 5 km from the Kherulen River, Tsagaan Ovoo soum, Dornod aimag. This wall was first discovered by Kh. Perlee in 1951 and has been explored and partially excavated then. The rampart was built with hardened earth. The dimension of the wall is in length of 345 m in north, 335 m in west, 345 m in south and 335 m in east. And now it is 1-2 m in height and 4-5 m in width. In the north part of the wall, there are four earthen mount apparently the rest from the construction. Small area, on both sides of the earthen mount has been excavated. Some little fragments of charcoal, fraction of rotted wood in the sites as well as a pottery found in 1.25 m in depth from the east mound respectively. As this pottery is quite similar to what found from the grave of the Noyon Uul, this wall has been determined to date to the Xiongnu period. (Ts.T.)
BARUUN KHAIRKHAN tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. The site located in the Songino mountain which lies close to the north bank of the Tuul River, in distance of 30 km in the west of Ulaanbaatar, Altanbulag soum, Tuv aimag. Western part of this mountain is named Baruun Khairkhan. This mountain has kept some square graves and ten Xiongnu graves, put close to each other, on its southern lower slope. The Xiongnu graves in the Baruun Khairkhan were discovered by an expedition for Bronze and Iron Age research team in 1982, and four of them were excavated in the same year. Afterwards, an expedition for Xiongnu studies excavated and researched two graves in 1990. An iron sword is found in one of the Xiongnu burials. (G.E.)



B
21
BATBOLD Natsag. Mongolian archaeologist. Research fellow of the Department of Xiongnu Archaeology, Institute of Archaeology, MAS, Ph.D. Born in 1968 in Bugant khoroo of Selenge province. In 2000, graduated Ulaanbaatar State University in major of history and archaeology. In 2003, he received a master’s degree with his work “Some issues of the Xiongnu funeral ritual”. In 2011, he defended a Ph.D with his dissertation “Rock Art Study of Mongolia (according to the research materials of the Gobi region)”. N.Batbold conducts studies on ancient history of Mongolia, especially on the petroglyphs of the Bornze Age and the Xiongnu period. He wrote 11 joint scientific reports and is a coauthor of monographs, including “Petroglyphs of Molor Tolgoi” (UB.,2004), “Petroglyphs of Biluu at Del Uul, Dundgovi province” (UB., 2005), “Petroglyphs of Shaakhar Tolgoi” (UB., 2008), “Xiongnu burial sites in Mongolia” (UB-Seoul, 2010), and wrote over 20 articles and papers, which published in domestic and foreign (USA, Japan, South Korea) scientific reviews. In 1999, 2003 and 2005 he participated in “Nomadic Empire” project and since 2006 he has been working in joint projects such as Mongolian-Russian joint research “Archaeological study at Noyon Uul in Mongolia” and Mongolia-Korean joint project “Mon-Sol” on the study of Xiongnu archaeological remains. (G.E.)
BATSAIKHAN Zagd. Mongolian archaeologist and Xiongnu scholar. Born in 1954 in the city of Ulaanbaatar. Graduated National University of Mongolia as a historian-history teacher in 1981. Between 1987-1997 worked as a research fellow at the Institute of History, MAS, and since 1997 have been working as lecturer at the Department of Archaeology and Anthropology of National University of Mongolia. In 2001 he defended his Ph.D dissertation on the theme “The Northern Xiongnu and the development of nomadic civilization”. In 2007, he nominated as associate professor of the National University of Mongolia. Z.Batsaikhan published two monographs, over 40 articles, and over 10 papers for conferences
in the theme of ancient Mongolian history and archaeology. He has been working on the Xiongnu study since 1990 and played a significant role in studying Xiongnu burial sites at Delgerkhaan mountain, at Burkhan Tolgoi of Egyin Gol, at Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu of Uguu-Nuur soum, as well as settlements including the Khermen Tal fortresses. His monograph “The Xiongnu” (history, archaeology, ethnography), published in 2002, is a skillful work that described many issues of the Xiongnu civilization basing on the research materials of the small circular burials and settlements. This monograph was translated into Hungarian and published in Hungary. (Ts.T.)
BATTLE OF BAIDENG. Military conflict between Xiongnu and Han dynasty. Held in the territory of today’s Shanxi province, in 200 BCE. The main combat was held in the Baideng Mountain, near Datong. In this combat, the Xiongnu was commanded by Maodun Chanyu and the Han by Gaozu emperor. Xiongnu had 400.000 cavalries and the Han had 320.000 soldiers. The main reason of the war is the policies intended by Han, those are to invade the Xiongnu with a power of soldiers and to make them a subject of Han. Basically, Han Xin or Han wang of the Han made a fight against Emperor, Gaodi and he wanted a support of the Xiongnu. At the time of the establishment of Han in 202 BCE, Han Xin, an aristocrat, was granted an estate of Henan province. Nevertheless, it was changed for where is now in Shanxi province under the pretext of protecting the boundary. Actually, he wanted to make Mayi city the capital of his planned empire, as being much closer to the boundary. At the same time, the Xiongnu launched an attack and besieged Mayi city. So Han Xin had no way to conduct a negotiation with Xiongnu. In contrary, this situation caused Gaodi Emperor to suspect that Han Xin would betray to Xiongnu. Falling in ambiguous situation, Han Xin joined Xiongnu and decided to destroy Han. In 200 BCE, the Xiongnu army, commanded by Maodun Chanyu invaded China and attacked to Zhiyan through Mayi city, surrendered by Han Xin. Emperor Gaodi sent 600.000 soldiers against the Xiongnu, so that, the Xiongnu thinned the main force out and besieged the leading troops together with the emperor of Han near the Baideng Mountain, in the north of Shanxi, after the disruption of their force through the way of dispersing the Chinese soldiers in hundreds of kilometers in distance. There was a non-stop fight lasted a week in winter, with the



B
22
Chinese soldier who finished all the food and were not able to pull the bear bow because of starvation and chilly coldness. So, the Xiongnu soldiers on four coat-colors of horses made an attack and shot in four turns. Emperor Gaodi in such a difficult situation, was advised by a dignitary named Chen Ping and decided to send a large scale of present to Yanzhi, wife of Maodun in order to persuade him to stop to besiege and if so, they would be ready to compromise multilaterally. Maodun received their proposal due to his wife’s word “There is no need to upset each other. Even though you captured Han, we cannot adapt to the new land” she added “The Emperor of Han is accounted for a wise man” and Han Sin’s promise to send his army, what was not fulfilled. After the defeat at the Baideng, Han Dynasty was aware that they could not depress Xiongnu with a force of army and decided to make “A Marriage Treaty/A treaty of Perpetual Peace” and to give a tribute. Since the treaty was drawn in 198 BCE, Han Dynasty agreed to give his princess to Chanyu and also a large scale of tribute to Xiongnu as named a present every year. In addition, the demarcation of the boundary with the Great Wall is considered to have a historic significance. Han Dynasty stopped boasting and officially recognized Xiongnu as the most powerful Empire that would be coincided with Han Dynasty. The basic provisions of the “A Marriage Treaty/A treaty of Perpetual Peace” helped to the peaceful relationship between them both and became a principal model of following treaties between Nomadic Empires and China for hundreds of years. Although the treaty was made after the war at the Baideng, Xiongnu went on supporting Han Xin, so that, there was unrest in the North China for three years. Also Chen Si who was a commander of Zhao and Dai Wang ling’s army betrayed and joined Han Xin and this event was the action like pouring oil on flames. Han Xin was killed at the fight in 196 BCE but it was not rested along the north boundary yet, caused by an uprising in Yang Wang ling’s territory against Han Dynasty and revolts mutinied by Li Guan in some provinces from north to east depended on Xiongnu. All of them were historic events to attempt to solve the domestic problem in relying on Xiongnu who was thought to have the most powerful army at the same time. In fact, the flourish of Xiongnu is directly related to the war at the Baideng and the subsequent events. (Ts.T.)
BATTLE OF JUSHI. Battle between Xiongnu
and Han near Turpan city in modern East Xinjiang in the winter of 67 BCE. Wugui wang led the Xiongnu army and Zheng Ji led the Han forces. Xiongnu army size is not clear, but from Han there were 1500 Chinese soldiers and 10.000 soldiers from allied local city-states. This battle was an aggressive attack from Han to control one of the important part of north route of Silk Road, the actual Turpan. When Jushi capital city Jiaohe was surrounded by a stronger army from Han, Wugui wang surrendered. Small number of support military force came from Xiongnu, but they were defeated and pulled back. Chinese appointed a governor of the city with less number of military forces, but they were afraid from counter attack from Xiongnu and run to Wusun to west. Soon big army of Xiongnu came and appointed Doumo as a new governor of the city and evacuated Jushi inhabitants to the east. (Ts.Т.)
BATTLE OF TALAS River. Fighting between Xiongnu and Han army near the Talas river in autumn and winter of 36 BCE, present Taraz city of Kazakhstan. Zhizhi Chanyu headed the Xiongnu army and Han army headed by Gang Yanshou and Cheng Tang. Xiongnu army consisted by 3000 Xiongnu, 10 000 Kangju warriors, and Chinese army consisted by 40.000 land forces, few number of cavalries and support warriors from allies of Tarim basin. Talas river was the core point of Chinese warriors to pass through the northern border. Later, Tang warriors passed over again to the north and fought with Arabian warriors near the Talas river in 751 CE. Zhizhi and Huhanye two brothers disputed Xiongnu throne in 56 BCE, and incited the sedition. Zhizhi Chanyu defeated in this fight and moved to the west. He reached to Semirechiye and continued his political and military activity. First of all he controled Kangju people, and defeated Wusun people allied with Han. Moreover near the Talas river, he built the fortified city which is circled by earthen wall, double wooden abates and watch tower for 2 years by 500 people. In 36 BCE, Chen Tang the deputy governor of the Western Regions claimed that Zhizhi was planning to build up a great empire and proposed a preemptive attack. He rise up an army and marched west on both sides of the Tarim Basin, reunited near Kashgar and moved across Kangju territory reaching the western shore of Lake Balkhash. The Chinese army camped about 30 li from Zhizhi’s fortress and the two sides exchanged rather hypocritical



B
23
messages. They then moved to within 3 li of Zhizhi and fortified themselves. The Xiongnu sent out several hundred cavalry and infantry, but they were driven back into the fort. The Chinese followed and attacked the fort and managed to burn part of the wall. That night several hundred Xiongnu horsemen tried to escape but all were killed. Zhizhi himself thought of escape but decided to remain because he knew that he had too many enemies in the surrounding country. Zhizhi’s queen and concubines shot arrows from the ramparts. Zhizhi was seriously wounded by an arrow. Shortly after midnight the outer walls were breached and the Xiongnu retreated to the inner citadel. At this point several thousand Kangju horsemen appeared and attacked the Chinese in the darkness but were unable to accomplish anything. When dawn broke parts of the inner citadel were on fire. The Chinese piled dirt on the citadel walls and clambered into the citadel. Zhizhi and a hundred or so warriors retreated into the palace. The palace was set on fire and attacked from all directions and Zhizhi was mortally wounded. 1,518 Xiongnu died, including the crown prince and Zhizhi’s wives. 145 were
captured and well over 1,000 surrendered. The soldiers were allowed to keep their booty and the surrendered Xiongnu were distributed to the fifteen Tarim city-kingdoms that participated in the battle. Zhizhi was the only Xiongnu Chanyu killed by the Chinese. (Ts.T.)
BAYAN BULAG settlement. Xiongnu period walled settlement. Situated on the foot of a small hill of the southern slope of the Khorkhiin Nuruu mountain range, the north side of Borzon Gobi, in 30 km in south of the Nomgon sum center, Umnugobi aimag. As the ruin was damaged by rainfall, wind and animal foot, the north wall is comparatively complete, and most part of the west and the entire east wall are blurred. The north part of the wall is 180 m in length, 10-16 m in width, whereas the west part is 110 m in length. The wall is built up directed to the straight north. This site was firstly explored, and described by Kh. Perlee, in 1950’s. As a result of excavating in a small area of the site, Perlee found some fractions of pottery similar to Xiongnu, a bronze arrow with a metal filament, pottery and slag. Later, in 1976, D. Navaan, a Mongolian archeologist studied and found some items including wushu coins, some pieces of bronze articles and pottery sherds, and concluded that is dated to Xiongnu period and 13-14th c. CE. In 1990, Mongolian-Russian archeologists revisited the site, determined its dimension and structure, excavated and found some interesting findings such as 24 kinds of arrow head, a bronze mirror, some fraction of 50 different designs of potteries, a bronze seal, some roof-tiles and wushu coins. Afterwards, D.Tseveendorj, Z.Batsaikhan and Ts.Turbat drew a conclusion on the basis of some written sources, that this settlement could be the Zhao Xin cheng city, which is dated to Xiongnu period and was dedicated to Zhao Xin who was a military commander and joined Han from Xiongnu in 123. (Ts.T.)
Bayanbulag settlement plan



B
24
BAYAN-UNDUR settlement. Xiongnu period walled settlement. Situated on the west bank of the Dzida River, in Republic of Buryatia, the Russian Federation. Near the site located Derestuy burial site. The settlement is enclosed by defensive
rampart or wall sized 70×70 m. There is another different rampart which is 1 m in height and 1.6-1.7 m in width in the north part. Nowadays, it is almost blurred but only 10-20 cm of wall, remained in the surrounding parts. There was a wall around the settlement and its door in the southwest. In 198889 S.V.Danilov conducted small scale excavations on this settlement. (Ts.T.)
BEIHAI Lake. Historical place name recorded in relation with Xiongnu histSince Han Dynasty was able to unify small tribes who were settled down in the south and to extend his dominance, they boasted and sent Go Gi as a messenger to tell their insulting words to Wuwei Chanyu of Xiongnu. Wuwei Chanyu who got annoyed at this event, confined the messenger and then exiled him to the Beihai lake. It is said to be the actual Baikal Lake. (B.B.)
BELT. An important composition of nomadic people costumes was a belt and especially the Xiongnu put the belt on high-waist according to the historical and archeological facts. In addition to them, the belt was a main wear for men and
a recognition sign of their ranks, while women traditionally did not wear any belt. Nevertheless, the archeological survey tells all people, men and women, wore belt. But some material objects dated to the later period of Xiongnu, reveal the women ceased to wear belt and the men were distinguished by their belt for carrying their bow and arrow. Consequently, researchers prove the men are named “bustei” (person who carries the
Bayan-Undur settlement



B
25
belt) and women “busgui” (person who do not carries the belt), what are used now in modern Mongolian. The nomadic Xiongnu surely wore belt that is proved by the belt with a little ring at one end-a kind of belt made of weaved or plaited woolen thread on its frame. But no any leather belt has been found. But of the archaeological findings of Xiongnu, rings and studs are frequently found that can prove they wore a leather belt with metal decorations. In the burial place in Ivolga, some findings like buckle, ring and hook in the material objects buried with the body were found. The most interesting thing is some decoration of belt, found from the grave of not only men also women, and all of them have been sorted. The men hanged down their weapons like dagger, sword, arrow case and bow case, whereas the women hanged down accessories like bag, jeweler, necklace and cowrie shell. On the basis of the decoration, the findings were classified into four groups and they can clarify their ranks, position in the society and wealth. The Xiongnu played a great role on the
tradition of belt and their belts got more delicate in their made and design than belt-buckle or stud, made in the Bronze Age. (U.E.)
BELT DECORATION. Belt was a significant composition of the costume which was used for taking care of tiredness for long time riding with weapons. In the process, the belt has been decorated with gold, silver, bronze and metal fittings. The belt is a kind of ornaments and can indicate the rank of the man who is wearing. The fact that the nomads have worn the belts with beautiful metal fittings since ancient times is proved by some Chinese sources and archeological findings that the Xiongnu wore the belt for carrying bow and arrow. The Xiongnu gold, silver, bronze, metal, bone, wood and stone belt buckle and some fittings have being found from many sites. Their principal feature is a portrayal of a couple of animal standing, looking at or fighting against each other. The portrayal on the belt decorations date to the pre-Bronze and Iron Ages is any strange animals like in legend or myth, whereas one date to the Xiongnu period is domestic animal like ox, horse, camel, sheep, goat and yak as usual and dragon, cart and persons as well. Also some geometrical shapes are displayed. The Xiongnu belt decoration portrayal characterizes trees, plants, mountains and rivers as a background of the picture. (G.E.)
Bronze buckle with a dragon image, Gathered finding
Artefacts of the Gol mod, tomb 20
Golden belt decoration, Burkhan tolgoi site
Bronze belt buckle with image of two horses

26
