BELKHIIN AM tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. A small narrow valley at the beginning of the Selbe River sourced from a high mountain in north of Ulaanbaatar is named Belkhiin Am. This monument is in a place named Ranjamba hollow, at the mouth beginning on the southern slope of the mountain with a conifer forest. There is a children’s summer camp “Selbe” in just east of the monument. The date of the Xiongnu burial place discovery is unknown. The first excavation at the monument was made by a Mongol-Hungary joint expedition in spring, 1961. They uncovered 10 graves in total, drew a map and excavated three of them. Accordance with the map and measurement, three of them are comparatively large, three are middle and four are small. The graves are placed from east to west in file. Although each has a circle stone external construction, two of them in northwest (numbered 9 and 10 on the map) are like square in shape and have a dromus or ceremonial entrance. The dromus of the 10th grave is 9 m in length, 5 m in wide part joining the mound and 3.8 m in point part. But as for the 9th grave, it is 3 m in length and 4 m in width, stuck out. There is a large grave (7th grave) in the east of the burial place, shaped circle and with no dromus. There are three small graves (3rd, 4th, 5th graves) in east of the 7th grave. There is a small one (2nd grave) in east of a large one (10th grave) (See the map). The large and middle sized graves have 1.15-2.8 m depth hollows on the external construction center particularly on the part with no stone (graves-1,6,7,8,9,10). So, the local people called the graves “Khulgaichiin doloon khonkhor”
(that means “Seven hollows of thief”). Of three graves which were excavated by the Mongol-Hungary joint expedition, the 1st grave is middle sized and the rest (2nd, 3rd graves) are small and are placed in east as a satellite. (G.E.)
BERNSHTAM, Alexander Natanowich (19101956). Soviet archeologist and historian. Doctor in History and Professor of University of Leningrad. He wrote approximately 250 scientific works and did research in the regions like Semirechiye, TianShan, Pamir-Altai and Fergana. Also he discovered the ruins of some ancient cities in Kazakstan including Balasugan, Taraz and Saraka and determined the basic periods of history of Central Asia. His some works dedicated to research the role of city and settlement after the periods of the Great Mongol Empire and to use for research on ancient culture formed among the people who inhabited near the middle confluence of the Syrdarya River. A.N. Bernshtam is the first person who discovered the Xiongnu monuments in the Central Asia and did his first research work on it. In addition to them, he published some articles and papers on Xiongnu based on Chinese source materials. His major work on Xiongnu “The Essays on the History of Xiongnu” clear focuses the influence and significance of Xiongnu conquest in history of the Central Asia and South and Western Europe. (M.Ts.)
BICHECI. It is a post name, particular title of Xiongnu. It is an origin of “Bičigeči” in Mongolian language. But today it is used for merely a post name not title in Mongolian. (No.B.)
Plan of the Belhiin am site



B
28
BICHURIN, Nikita Yakovlevic (1777-1853). Russian orientalist. Graduated from the Academy of Religion in Kazan. He worked in Iyonow Church in Kazan and Voznesensk Church in Irkutsk, as a priest and in Beijing as a head of Russian Orthodox Mission in 1807-1821. He collected plentiful Chinese historical materials such as book and other written sources etc. and brought them all to Russia. In 1826, N.Ya.Bichurin started to make a big effort to scientific and translation works. The fundamental field of his research was history and ethnology of the Mongol-Turkic speaking peoples, their history, language and culture based on Chinese written sources. His greatest work is a three volume-book entitled “About the people who settled in the Middle Asia in ancient times”, who concerns largely the history of Xiongnu. (M.Ts.)
BOARD GAME. The only known board game was found from the tomb No.1 of Gol Mod site, Khairkhan soum, Arkhangai aimag. It comprises of a wooden board and three circle stones (token). The surface of the board has rotted away from the soil moisture but it is possible to make clear its general structure and form.
The game board. It was made as the following way that the wood is made thin like a board and little rectangular holes are on it. The board was broken down in eight small parts as it
was found, but after the restoration it constitutes only three big parts. After the restoration, it has 52.7 cm in length, 20.5 cm in width and 0.7-1.2 cm in thick. The rectangular lower long side proves it is an edge of one side. But the upper part and two sides were severely damaged. So it is difficult to estimate the real size of the board. There are 12 vertical and 4 horizontal holes on the board. Each hole is the same in 4.5×6.8 cm. The holes are accurately lined up. The groove space between the holes is 0.5 cm and depth is 0.4 cm. There is a cross on two holes on the two sides of a hole. Then two holes are passed and next one has a cross. It means there are four crosses on the board. The length of the cross is 7.8 cm and width of the groove is 0.2 cm.
The circle stone (token). There are three tokens found together with the board game. They are made of dark grey stone and in the same size. Their diameter is 2.95 cm and width is 0.45 cm. They have no any figure on them.
As its structure, it is the same to Xiangqithe board of Chinese chess, but little different in the cross between the stops. The Mongolians have played numbers of board games and about some of them have been published. G.Sukhbaatar investigated children’s games including some g p board ggames: Monggolian Deer,, Singgingg Deer
Board game, Gol mod, Tomb 1



B
29
and Tangut Deer and published a book on them. N.Namjildorj found out around 100 board games, composed some of them with a modern theme and published. His book tells about the board, other components, and the rule of how to play in detail. Of them, the boards of games: Orgil, Ulzii, Big Chess Deer (Ikh shatriin buga), Nine Horses (Esun mori), Deer Chess (Shatran buga), Shaa and Eighty One Deer (Nayan negtiin buga) are square or rectangular and have the stops. Nevertheless, we have inadequate facts or information and research on history or the origin of the board games. A historian A.Punsag mentioned about the origin of Mongolian chess in his book entitled “Mongolian chess and Mongolian culture” that some researchers viewed chess was originated from India or China. But some oral literatures, tales and epics tell Mongolian chess was handed down from Ancient India via Persia and Arabia and has been changed through Mongolian traditional way since 9-16th c. CE. Etymologically, “shatar” (chess) a kind of the board games is a Sanskrit word “chaturanga” which means four sides. The researchers agree a view the board games were initially originated in India. However, some archeological excavations have found some parts of chess but not full of the board. Some researchers particularly D. Forbess says in a book “History of Chess” (1860), on the basis of some written sources, that the chess was originated in India 5000 years ago. In addition, A.Van der Linde, T.Von der Lasa and
H.Murray researched the history of the chess in certain scale. H.J. Murray published “A History of Chess” in 1913, as a result of his research work for 14 years. This work was re-published with the names of “A History of Board games other than Chess” in 1952 and “A Short History of Chess” in 1963 respectively. He emphasizes we have some obscure information from written sources dated back to 700 BC as the earliest fact. On the contrary, others view the chess was originated in China. We have numbers of myths and legends on how the chess was originated, whereas the question when and where the chess was originated, might be as complicated as playing it. From the point of most researchers’ view, the chess was originated in India and spread in Persia and Arabia. Then it became more popular in Europe via Arabia in 10th century. As for the Mongolians, we have some publications on the board games, but no any detailed research on their history. The Tomb No.1 of the Gol Mod where this board game had been kept in has been accounted to be dated to 20-50 CE with a radiocarbon analysis and a relative date of a bronze mirror in Chinese style. Hence, it helps to conclude that the Xiongnu played board games in 1st c. CE. (Ch.E.)
BONE PROCESSING. According to Z.Batsaikhan, “it was common among Xiongnu to make various objects from horn of dear and antelope and bone of animals. There must have been households and special workshops mastered in bone processing craft. From the 25th,
d d bli h d b k h h d h hi h h i
Carved pattern belt pendent, Baruun mukhlag, Tomb 143
Board game, Gol mod, Tomb 1



B
30
46th, 47th dwelling of ruins of Ivolga settlement, processed bone material, readymade objects and tools were discovered.” Less bone objects in comparison to pottery were found from ruins of Ivolga settlements. Bone objects or materials were found from almost every dwelling. Bone and horn materials were softened and then special saw, knife, grate and rasp was used to make objects. Bone materials were polished, pierced and carved. Bone jews harp is an important artifact to
show the highly developed craft of bone processing of Xiongnu period, discovered from burials of Morin tolgoi. Also, esthetical sense and perception of religion can be deciphered from bone carvings of Xiongnu. Fine lines of the patterns and decorations were made through scratching method. Some bone carvings of Xiongnu period are perfect in their execution, making them real art work. These types of carvings reflect traditional craft of nomads, illustrating images of their legend, domestic animals from their

livelihood. Carving of a tiger on a cattle hip bone found from the ruins of Boroo settlement. (Ts.Т.)
BOROO settlement. Xiongnu settlement located on the east bank of the Boroo River, on the border between Mandal sum, Selenge aimag and Bornuur sum, Tuv aimag. The settlement is extended about 500 m along the first terrace of Boroo river. A Mongolian senior archeologist, Ts.Dorjsuren discovered in 1966 and identified Xiongnu period datation of the site on the basis of some collected surface findings. He investigated some demolished terraces where were plenty of ceramic sherds and other types rests of material culture have been
discovered in, and looked down at some fraction of pottery, stone, bone and coal, stuck out from the earth in 20-30 m in depth from surface. It extends approximately 30 m in length and there is only red strip in some places. So he considered that it must not be a kind of graves but a ruin of a settlement. As a result of them, he wrote “It is the first settlement of Xiongnu where we have found a large number of relics related to material culture and domestic handcraft in Mongolia.” Also he proposed that our archeologists should excavate and investigate this ruin very soon. 20 years later, or in 1987, Mongolian archeologist D.Tseveendorj visited the settlement,
Excavation of Boroo gol site



B
32



B
33
Excavation of Boroo gol site



B
34
found some findings and investigated two destroyed constructions. In 1990, D.Tseveendorj and I.Erdelyi, a Hungarian archeologist, made the first test excavations here. They dag test holes in two places and found many interesting findings such as some sherds of pottery, a pottery mouth, a device made of a shoulder-blade of small male livestock and a mouth or main part of a pottery which had not completely dried and got thinner, and so on. In 2005-2007, a Mongolian-Swiss joint archaeological team directed by Ts. Turbat and N. Pousaz has conducted large scale excavation on this site and discovered 6 half-buried dwellings, hundreds of household structures were unearthed. If most of Xiongnu settlements of Mongolia have temporary characters, Boroo settlement was permanent one and kept many traces of domestic handcraft activities. Therefore, it is evident that it was one of the agriculture and handcraft centers of Xiongnu. (Ts.T.)
BORBULAG tombs. Xiongnu period elite and common people mixed cemetery in Mongolia. Located in the territory of Binder soum of Khentii aimag, in the southern bank of Onon river, approximately 12 km west of the soum center of Bayan-Adraga. It was initially found during the survey conducted by the Mongolian-Japanese researchers of “Gurvan Gol” project and a detailed map was drawn in 1991. The cemetery consisted of 140 tombs both big elite tombs and small circular mounded burials. Seven burials located in the periphery of the cemetery are suggested to be sacrificial features from the Turkic period. Also more nine burials are recognized as medieval burials and another seven burials are with unknown dates. The rest or 136 burials are related to the Xiongnu period. There have not any excavations carried out yet. (Ch.Ye.)
BOW. During the Stone Age period people used to use simply designed wooden bow for hunting wild animals. However there is not any part of this
Boroo settlement plan



B
35
bow kept until today many kinds of stone and bone arrowheads are found from settlements of Bronze Age in Dulaanii Gobi in Dornogobi aimag, Zuuh in Bayanhongor aimag, Bayanzag in Umnugobi aimag in Bronze age period. During the Bronze Age period ancient people started to use bow actively as a weapon for killing enemies and protecting against attacks at the same time they started to breed animals and increasing livestock which creates private property. Archery requires experience which people can gain during the training and battle. Bone and some attached part of bow were found in numbers from slab burials of Bronze Age period located in Mongolia and in Transbaikalia. Even though these findings are not enough to research about arch from all side it is important evidence to approve ancient nomads lived in Mongolia and region of Baikal Lake used to use compounded arch which has hornlike glued pieces. The advantage of Xiongnu army is attacks of cavaliers and it was noted in the historical sources of that time. Xiongnu cavaliers used to prefer shooting with bow and arrow rather than sticking and fighting so their main weapon was bow and arrow. It can be explained that comparing to the swords bows and arrows are more often found from the graves. According to the statistical data of tombs of Xiongnu’s ordinary people, attached piece of bow was found 77% from men, 17%
from women, 6% from children and arrowhead was found 87% from men, 10% women and 3% from children tomb and it shows that weapon was important to nomadic life. Nowadays attached pieces of bows are found in numbers from Xiongnu tombs in Mongolia and neighboring countries and it can be divided into three general parts such as edge piece and middle part piece and handle piece. According to the researchers Xiongnu used to do composite bow with asymmetric form. In other words upper and lower parts of the in position of handling have different lengths. This kind of bow was found by excavation of Xiongnu tomb in Sul tolgoi site, Khuvsgul province and length of the bow considered as more than 150 cm. Also this kind of bow was found in Xiongnu tomb in Transbaikalia and Tuva. Nomads used to make the upper part of bow bigger and lower part of bow smaller which is comfortable for shooting in horse. Stretching this kind of bow makes hand is pulled around 1.5 cm and it has far target. Bow does not suppose to be stretched all the time and it should be kept straight when it is not used. Xiongnu are experienced in shooting with bow during the horse galloping. They used to train youths for archery and pay more attention on improving bow design. Wooden, horn and composite bow which traditionally used among nomads lived in Mongolia since Xiongnu period and transferred to Chinggis khan warriors. It was approved again by archeological researchers and
Restored drawing of the Xiognu bow
Bone part of composite bow, Khirgist khooloi, Tomb 5



B
36
the secret of conquering half of the world in 13th century is this weapon. (N.E.)
BRIDLE BIT AND CHEEK PIECE. Bridles bit and cheek pieces of many types and designs have been abundantly occurred in Xiongnu burials. According to the studies made by Z.Batsaikhan, the Xiongnu bits range in lengths from 19 to 24 cm, and mouth pieces approximately measure at 21 cm in thickness, which made of two adjusted thin iron circle rings at the end for joining. The mouth piece’s irons are straight designed and one is bit shorter and another is bit longer. Rings of mouth pieces are relatively small than the ring for cheek piece. The Xiongnu people used cheek pieces made of horn and iron. However iron cheek pieces used widely than the horn pieces and currently several types of iron pieces have been found. For instance, two different kinds of cheek pieces found in the Xiongnu burials of Buriatia. One has pointed ends and second has S shaped curved body. In Xiongnu burials of Mongolia have been broadly found iron cheek pieces with a straight body and with axe shaped or circular ends. These iron and horn cheek pieces have two holes which bit ring and bridle straps attached into. This is to prevent from coming out the bit from the horse mouth while riding. According to the studies of Z.Batsaikhan, the 3 most common types of cheek pieces such as circular, curvy and cross mark shaped, were used
even before Xiongnu time, and these types of cheek pieces are still being used without any big changes among Mongols. Following the ancient traditions, the Mongols use these cheek pieces depending on the purpose of use. For example, the cross mark
and curvy bits are mainly used in racing horses or horses that are not controlled with rein properly. (Ts.T.)
BRIDLE, CRUPPER AND BREAST-STRAP. There are many leather straps and metal fittings of bridle found from the Xiongnu graves. A gilded bronze stud, found in Tomb No.9 of the Noyon Uul, by Ts. Dorjsuren is one of the common objects. It is generally a Chinese object used in the period of Han Dynasty. From the tomb No.10 of Burkhan Tolgoi, Bulgan aimag, some bronze studs in this design were found. And some bronze studs in the same shape but closed, were found from the Derestuy burial place, the Transbaikalia. In tomb No.10 of the Burkhan Tolgoi, Bulgan aimag found some pieces of long and short leather straps of bridle. The bridle was made of a leather strap in 3 cm wide, of which two ends were sewed by sinew thread and decorated with bronze gilded studs. Another
Gilded bronze knobble of bridle, Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Breast collar silver decoration with an image of a mythic unicorn



B
37
bronze studs similar to above mentioned, have being found from the Noyon Uul and the Ilmovaya Pad’. These studs can be an evidence to confirm the relationship between Xiongnu and China. On the other hand, the Xiongnu craftsmen may have duplicated some articles of south neighbor. A piece of leather strap found from the Tomb No.9 of the Duulga Uul has been examined in a laboratory was made of the tanned cattle raw skin. While using a soft saddle made without hard wooden body, a crupper and a breast-strap are helpful for stable riding. We have found some but few findings of the crupper and breast-strap in Xiongnu burials. One of them was found from the Tomb No.28 of the Derestuy, has 6 bronze studs with semi-round top-each has a small hook to hang down a bell as well as a square ring to thread a strap of leather at the back side. They were discovered in being placed along the legs of the body. As a result of the placement, they can be the decorative studs of a crupper or a breast-strap. Also two bells in big and small size, in the grave, may be hanged down in front of the breast-strap. According to the previous material objects, bronze bells are found from the graves of Russia-Altai Pazyryk and of the Xiongnu period, as well as from Burkhan Tolgoi, Tamiriin Ulaan Khoshuu, Derestuy, and the Ivolga settlement, and the Ordos, Inner Mongolia. The bronze bells have been found today, have several
types like smooth without any pattern, having pattern on two sides and having four holes. (Ts.T.)
“BRIEF HISTORY OFANCIENT NORTHERN NATIONS OF CHINA.” The book was published in 1979 by Department of Study of Mongolian Language, Literature and History of Inner Mongolia and Department of Historical study, University of Inner Mongolia. It is noted in the book that Xiongnu were ancient nation that inhabited vast territory north of the Great Wall and since around 6000 BCE, Xiongnu started to cohabite with ancestors of Han, in 221 BCE Qin Shihuan unified 7 states and at the same time Xiongnu nations strengthened and built Xiongnu Empire in 209 BCE. The Shiji record says “During Maodun Chanyu reign Xiongnu conquered many nomadic tribes. Donghu was conquered and destroyed, Yuezhi was driven away to west, south tribes Loufan and Baiyan were united with Xiongnu and Dingling, Jiangun were subjugated and then Yuezhi was destroyed, Loulan, Wusun, Hujie, and other 26 nations were brought to peace, territory from south of Yingshan mountain to Baikal lake to the north, from east Liaohe river to Chunli Mountain in the west were conquered” and concluded that “Xiongnu state is the first state based on enslavement of northern nations in the history of our country”. Xiongnu state structure is “The first is Chanyu palace. The central Xiongnu was governed from here. The second is Palace of king of East Tuqi wang. The east branch of Xiongnu was governed from here. The third is the palace of the West Tuqi wang. The west branch of Xiongnu was governed from here. Chanyu is Xiongnu state supreme ruler. In Xiongnu language, he is “Chanyu, son of heaven”, according to the publication. The birthplace of Xiongnu was in the south of Gobi, Ordos and Yingshan Mountain. The political center of Xiongnu, Tumen city is a place near present day Yiyuan xian on the border of Inner Mongolia. Economy of Xiongnu was based on animal husbandry mainly, but handicraft was well developed and had farming as well. In battles, for long range Xiongnu used bow and arrow, in close combat they used a spear and a dagger; they had a treasure sword called “Jin Lu”. Xiongnu had its language, but no writing alphabet. But it is noted
Reconstruction of a Xiongnu Bridle, Crupper and Breast-Strap



B
38
that Xiongnu had rich folklore, musical talent, sang
long songs, played pipe and small drum. (А.P.)
BRONZE CADDY. One of the artifacts that is commonly found in Xiongnu burials are bronze caddys for containing liquids, including alcoholic. The principal forms of these was basically the
same, but the decorations on them were different. Specifically, bronze kettle with vertical handle found in the tomb No.2 of Takhiltyn Khotgor, Mankhan soum, Khovd aimag has bloated round body with spout on one side and hand on the other side. There is a round lid attached to the body with hinge and the lid and two sides of the caddy are decorated with taote relief motives. Also there are three layers of ribbon relief around the body. Similar caddy remains were found in the tombs Nos.6 and 25 of Noyon Uul burial site and from the tombs Nos. 1 and 25 of Gol Mod burial site. The caddy design and shape found in the tomb No.25 of Noyon Uul burial site and the tomb No.20 of Gol Mod burial site are
similar with the ones found in Takhiltyn Khotgor, however there is taote relief image decorating two sides of the caddy. Also kettles without decoration, but with similar shape were found from the tomb No.2 of Duurlig nars and from the tomb No.1 of Khukh uzuur dugui, Bulgan soum, and Khovd aimag. A small bronze goblet was discovered with kettle from the burial of Khukh Uzuur Dugui. (Ch.Е.)
“BRONZE MIRRORS IN XIONGNU GRAVES AND THE ORIGIN OF PRACTICES FOR SHATTERING A MIRROR”. Article published in “Central University of Nationalities Research Journal”, 2009, Vol. 6. Author - Ma Liqing. Bronze mirrors are often found broken or shattered from the graves and these are from Qin and Han Dynasty periods. In the article, bronze mirrors of Qin, Han and bronze mirrors found from the Xiongnu graves were compared as research, he claims, rituals of the Xiongnu for breaking a mirror on funeral could be associated somehow with ancient Qin period culture and the rituals which Xianbei had, originated or inherited from the Xiongnu. Since the southern Xiongnu subdued to Han Dynasty and the Xianbei established its state and government in territory of China, the rituals for shattering a mirror on funeral disappeared. (Ch.S.)
BRONZE PROCESSING ART. Metal objects, specifically iron and bronze artifacts are found from Xiongnu burials in large quantities. Also artifacts made of gold and silver are found along with artifacts produced from iron and bronze with
Examples of Bronze melting of belt open-work plaques



B
39
gold and silver parts. Bronze artifacts were the most commonly excavated findings in the biggest quantity apart from pottery. The art of processing bronze during Xiongnu period was highly developed. Its origin can be traced back to the Central Asian bronze production tradition of Bronze Age. Historical artifacts of the exploration and processing of bronze in Northern Mongolia from ancient time are still being discovered. According to the research carried out by S.S.Minyaev, during Bronze Age of Transbaikalia, tin bearing bronze was processed. During early Iron Age, mainly arsenic and tin bearing bronze and lead bearing bronze were processed. The resemblance in composition of bronze artifacts found in square burials and Xiongnu burials is evidence for the cultural continuity. S.I.Rudenko concludes that similarities of the chemical composition of artifacts discovered in burials of Noyon Uul tombs and in Transbaikalian graves demonstrates the local origin of bronze production. However, S.S.Minyaev criticized S.I.Rudenko’s above mentioned conclusion as being poorly grounded, since the conclusion was only based on the study of 16 artifacts out of several hundred bronze artifacts excavated from Noyon Uul site. Based on the comparative study of bronze artifacts from 6 main Xiongnu sites and chance finding bronze artifacts, S.S.Minyaev concluded that Xiongnu bronze manufacturers mainly produced a) plumbiferous bronze, b) arsenic bronze, c) tin bearing-plumbiferous bronze, d) plumbiferous-arsenic bronze, e) plumbiferous-tin bearing-arsenic bronze products.But he stated that burials can be grouped as following based on the types of smelt of bronze artifacts found from historical places: 1. Noyon Uul burial site in Ilmovaya Pad’ 2. Settlement and burial site of Ivolga and burials of Derestuy, 3. Settlement of Dureny. According to his typology, artifacts
discovered from the 2nd group of burials, i.e. habitation and burial sites of Ivolga and burial site of Derestuy can be classified into the 3rd group. These are 1) arsenic bronze, 2) plumbiferousarsenic bronze, 3) multi-composition bronze (copper, plumbiferous-tin bearing-arsenic bronze). Tin bearing bronze and tin bearing-plumbiferous bronze as well as plumbiferous bronze dominate in artifacts discovered in the first group of burials, i.e. Noyon Uul and burial site in Ilmovaya Pad’, but there is no trace of arsenic bronze. Based on the above mentioned observation, it can be concluded that bronze artifacts from local origin found in the end group of burials totally different in chemical composition from bronze artifacts discovered from the first groups of burials. Artifacts such as mirror, coin and accessories of ceremonial chariots, valuable rare kitchen utensils of foreign origin are found in Xiongnu burials and Xiongnu settlement ruins. It should be noted that rare exclusive presents from Han Empire were placed with dead especially in large burials of Xiongnu elite. Thus there is no ground to be surprised that bronze artifacts discovered in Noyon Uul and burial site in Ilmovaya Pad’ were mostly of foreign origin. Composition of the bronze artifacts found in above mentioned sites differ significantly from other artifacts discovered in other Xiongnu burials and confirm that artisans of Xiongnu period established and developed bronze manufacturing centers independently from metal processing centers of East Asia. It is even possible to conclude Southern Transbaikalia as independent and original bronze production region. Big number of bronze artifacts from China and several articles from Baktria are found from Xiongnu burials of Noyon Uul. Specifically, 4 pear shaped belt rings were made of brass with high quantity of zinc, which is common in North Baktria. These artifacts are similar to the artifacts commonly discovered from North Baktria nomadic burials. Few artifacts made from Batkria brass
Bronze cauldron, Burkhan tolgoi, Tomb 95



B
40
were found in other Xiongnu monuments. According to theA.V.Davydova’s research, settlement of Ivolga was an important center of bronze processing of Xiongnu period. Clay funnel and drop shaped bronze waste were discovered in the dwellings Nos. 32 and 37 of this settlement. Even though mold for bronze articles have not yet found, smelting traces are clearly noticeable on the bronze artifacts. According to the result of spectral analysis on the bronze artifacts from Ivolga settlement ruin, this settlement was one of three bronze manufacturing centers of Xiongnu period in Transbaikalia. The bronze products of this center had equal amount of lead and tin (23%). Three bronze manufacturing centers of Xiongnu period in the Transbaikalia are the
following: 1. Metal processing center of Ivolga is defined by copper slag, bronze smelting trace, 3 types of casting vessel found in the ruins of settlement and burial site of Ivolga. 2. Dzida metal processing center is defined by artifacts excavated in Derestuy burials in the mid stream valley of Dzida river. This center is determined by the discovery of copper slag and trace of copper extraction from the area. Indium is found in the composition of bronze artifacts excavated from Derestuy burial which is not found in other places. The original name of Dzida is Zed and it is highly credible that it derived from Mongolian world “Zes/Zed”, which means copper. 3. Chikoi center is the entrance of the
Dureny settlement ruin in the middle river stream valley. Even though remains from smelting of copper and bronze have not been found in this area, the bronze artifacts discovered have distinct chemical composition and therefore Chikoi center is defined as the third metal processing center. It is imagined that the articles produced in the above mentioned centers were distributed all over the Transbaikalia and Northern Mongolia. Although there is neither artifacts discovered to establish the existence of metal processing centers in Ordos and Mongolia, no prove to deny it. According the study of the chemical composition of some bronze artifacts from Ordos, there is difference in composition, thus pointing at the possible existence of metal processing centers
in this area. There is high amount of zinc in the composition of bronze artifacts from Ordos. Another issue that is related to production and distribution of bronze articles is the identical shaped articles are being found in places distant from each other and the difference in composition of these identical articles. Particularly, Xiongnu style belt buckles with perforated design discovered in significant number from Minusinsk lowland along mid stream of Yenisei River were casted mainly in local traditional arsenic bronze. These belt buckles were casted in local materials with a mold made from the original Xiongnu style belt buckle. Thus, due to the repetitious creation of prototypes for molds the original image were blurred and sometimes image became unrecognizable. Very
Bronze cauldron, Khirgist khooloi, Tomb 5 Bronze cauldron, Shombuuzyn belchir, Tomb 7



B
41
few perfect shaped belt buckles were found from these areas, which may had been the original image of these casted artifacts. It can be concluded from above that there were several bronze production centers in the Mongolia-Transkaikalia, Ordos and Minusinsk lowland during Xiongnu period, where used their traditional local materials in production, however there were typical types of articles produced in these areas. Very few molds of bronze products were found in Xiongnu sites, however from the discovered few small molds, such as a ring mold, it is clear that the mold consists of symmetrical two parts. This type of molds have one or three opening (holes for liquid to be poured). Also molds that consist of two half parts with asymmetrical shape were used. According to research it is clear that one sided mold was used as well. Three different vessels for casting liquid were discovered from the dwelling No. 37 from Ivolga settlement, revealing that a blacksmith lived in this dwelling and used three different types of molds. No clay mold so far discovered during archeological study, thus indicating the use of only stone mold. The molds found in Mongolia and Transbaikalia are mostly stone molds. Bronze was mainly used in making detailed decoration articles for clothing, such as belt buckles and rings, whereas iron was used for production household utensils and articles which would require mass materials (Ts.Т.)
“BRONZE TOOLS OF ORDOS STYLE”. Authors Tian Guangjin and Guo Suxin. Published in Beijing, 1986, in 2 vols. Authors tried to clear the chronology and styles of Ordos bronze tools by archaeological data and revealed their view for the origine of Ordos style of bronze tools and manufacturers in the first volume of this work. The second volume includes archaeological data of ancient nomads and their counterpart Chinese states as Spring-Autumn period (770-476 BCE), Warring states (476-221 BCE), Qin (221-206 BCE) and Han (202 BCE-220 CE) and funeral types, structure and custom of ancient nomads. (Ch.S.)
BUDUGEN. Xianbei leader. After the dead of Tanshihuai, there was a revolt in Xianbei and many people came to be lords in the same time. In order to save the independence of Xianbei, some people like Budugen paid tributes to Cao Cao. In 205, he established a relationship with Kebineng, a component of him indeed, and tried to fight against Cao Wei Dynasty, but it failed and he was killed by Kebineng. (Ts.T.)
BUMAN (died in 552). A founder of Turkic Empire and the leader of Ashina tribe. When they were a subject of Rouran, he established a relationship with the Western Wei in 545 then became related by a marriage and was able to carry out their independent policies. He won the uprising of Tele tribes against Rouran and unified them under his leadership. In 552, he rised the revolt against Rouran. He promoted himself as Il Qagan and founded Turkic Empire but died a few months later. His son, Kul’-Issyq Qagan ruled the east part while his younger brother, Istemi governed the west part and newly conquered countries of Central Asia. (Ts.T.)
BURDUN tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Buriatia. Located in 14 km from Kyachta city, in the Kiran region. This cemetery covers an area of one square km in the Kiran village close to Ust’-Kiran village. In 1901 this cemetery was discovered by Russian anthropologist Yu.D.Tal’ko-Gryntsevich. He found 45 tombs: the size of the tombs differed, the biggest one has 12-15 m, and the smallest one has 7-9 m in diameter. The first excavation was undertaken by him. Yu.D.Tal’ko-Gryntsevich excavated 4 medium size tombs. Outside of the coffin he found charcoal, pottery, sheep and cow bones. A large number of artifacts were excavated in the graves, including knives, iron fragments, different size pottery, broken mirror pieces, gold fragment with decoration, rings, button and arrowheads. Nowadays these artifacts are all exhibited in the Regional Museum in Kyachta. (Ts.E.)
BURKHAN TOLGOI tombs. Xiongnu period burial site. Located in east bank of Egyin Gol River, Khutag-Undur soum, Bulgan aimag. The site is in distance of 40 km in the east of Khantai bag center, and over 60 km in the northwest of Selenge soum. A small hill in the southeast end of a high terrace on the east bank of the Egyin Gol River is named “Burkhan tolgoi” and it keeps a Xiongnu burial place with over 100 graves on its northwest terrace. The external feature of the graves is characterized by a stone circle. Totally, there are 95 graves having such kind of feature and more 8 graves with unrecognizable signs on the surface, were discovered. The dimension of the graves’ stone external construction ranges 3-11 m. Of them, the grave-46a is the smallest and the grave-27 is the largest, whereas the diameter of most graves is 5-7 m. The graves were placed from northeast to southwest, and can be divided into 3 sectors:



B
42
Northeast, West and South. During the excavation and exploration, these sectors have been clearly distinguished from each other by their chronology and burial custom. For example, the graves in the south sector mostly have had stone constructions
with twin burial pits. And the head of the dead is put to the direction of east or southeast. The graves are confirmed to date to more ancient time than others, according to the result of radiocarbon analyses. This sector is considered to be the richest
Burkhan tolgoi site
Burkhan tolgoi site plan



B
43
in material objects and bones of accompanied animals while the west sector is considered most recent and poor in material culture. This monument was firstly discovered by the “Selenge expedition” of the Institute of History, Mongolian Academy of Sciences, in 1991. As this area might be flooded by the water of reservoir of the hydroelectric power station, the site was excavated and researched entirely to for preventive term in 1992-1999. “Egyin Gol” expedition from the Institute of History, Mongolian Academy of Sciences, launched the excavation of 4 graves (Nos. 1-4) in 1992 and 6 graves (Nos. 5-10) in 1993. Afterwards, a Mongol-France joint archeological expedition conducted the excavation of 1 grave (No. 15) in 1994; 2 graves (Nos. 16 and 17) in 1995; 19 graves (Nos. 18, 18b, 19-23, 29b, 30, 31, 31a, 33, 33b, 42) in 1996; 36 graves (Nos. 32, 32a, 34-37, 37a, 38-41, 46-54, 56-71) in 1998 and 27 graves (Nos. 72-95) in 1999, and a MongolAmerican joint expedition did the excavation of 4 graves (Nos. 11-14) in 1994 and another 4 graves (Nos. 42-45) in 1996 respectively. In 2003, Ts.Turbat, Ch.Amartuvshin and U.Erdenebat published the report of the excavated graves with some illustrations and drawings of the Burkhan tolgoi graves in the book entitled “Archeological monuments in the Egyin Gol River basin”. (G.E.)
BURIAL RITUALS OF XIONGNU ELITE. Burial rituals of Xiongnu nobles, ancestors of Mongols differ significantly from burial rituals of ordinary Xiongnu people in many aspects. Some researchers conclude that from the 1st century BCE, Xiongnu elites erected tombs that imitated to some extend the tombs of aristocrats of China. On the other hand, based on the similarity in burial shape, size, structure and arrangement with burials of elites of Early Iron Age nomadic tribes of Altai Pazyryk and Minusinsk valley Tashtyk cultures,
there are researchers who argue that burial rituals of Xiongnu period present typical funeral tradition of nomadic elites. In any case, burials of Xiongnu nobles were the biggest burial complex in the ancient Mongolian history. Actually 10 burial sites of Xiongnu elites were discovered in Mongolia and in neighboring regions as Buryatia and Tuva. The Xiongnu elite burial sites were located in Noyon Uul, Batsumber soum, Tuv aimag; Selbe, near Ulaanbaatar city; Gol Mod, Khairkhan soum and Gol Mod-2, Undur-Ulaan soum, Arkhangai aimag (in total 4 sites in Central Mongolia); Duurlig Nars of Bayan-Adraga soum and Borbulag in Binder soum, Khentii aimag (in total 2 sites in Eastern Mongolia); Ilmovaya Pad’, Tsaram and
Lacguered wooden bowl, Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Ceramic pot, Gurvan modot uul, Tomb 5



B
44
Orgoiton sites (in total 3 sites in Transbaikalia); Takhiltyn Khotgor, in Mankhan soum of Khovd aimag and in Baidag, in Tuva (in total 2 sites in Western Mongolia). Burial sites of Xiongnu elites discovered firstly in Noyon Uul in the 1920s and excavated since then, with exception to Borbulag cemetery which stays intact until now. Within 400 tombs, Gol Mod burial site is the biggest among these cemeteries. It is noted that a burial site in Gol Mod-2 has the biggest tomb. There are in total around 1800 tombs in these burial sites out of which 1500 are found in the territory of Mongolia. Approximately 600 of these burials are belonging to the large terrace burials of elites. Burial sites with Xiongnu elite burials were sacred places of nomads, located in mountainous, hilly places. Most historical sites are in Khangai, Khentii forest steppe region, though in areas with sandy soil. Namely, Gol Mod, Gol Mod-2, Tsaram burial sites and many others are in places with
sandy soil. This particular type of soil was chosen to prevent from burial-robbers; it was easier to have deep pits for tombs in sandy soil. Larger burials with terrace and smaller burials with circular stone cover were all mixed with small offering places with dirt or stone cover in a burial site. However, these burials were arranged and assembled in strict order. Smaller burials are lined in semi-circular pattern on right and left site from north to south of the larger tombs. These smaller burials considered to be of those who were sacrificed after death of their chefs. Square or in some few cases trapezoidal square stone terrace of larger burials of nobles are visible on the ground surface. Wall height of tomb is determined by burial size. At the moment, the biggest known tomb is the No. 1 of Gol Mod-2. Its size is 46×46 m, wall height is 3.7 m. However, there are very few round small burials with entrance. There is a trapezoidal stone pavement by the south side of terrace named dromus. Attachment of the dromus to the south wall is usually wider, which gets narrower and smaller to the south. The entrance is located underground, with its end located deeper in the ground. This part is called entrance. The longest entrance-dromus of Xiongnu terrace tomb is registered to be the entrance of the Tomb No.1 of Gol Mod-2 cemetery, with its 37.5 m long entrance-dromus. There is pit under stone cover in a burial. Pit has an upside down pyramid shape, i.e. it gets smaller from top to down. Pit depth depends on burial size. The tomb No.20 of Gol Mod has
Bronze decoration of parasol, Noyon Uul



B
45
covered of 30×30m area in surface and its pit depth has 18 m. According to the Chinese historical records, “the deceased was buried in external and internal ... tomb”. This source is confirmed with archeological evidence. Corps was placed in wooden coffins. Wooden coffin of the nobles were placed in a single and/or double chamber tomb. There is a double layered log tomb in lager burials
and there is a single log tomb in smaller tombs of elite. According to the research by Ch.EruulErdene, main four footwalls joined with log, floor and ceiling were made from timber and plastered with clay. There is a well made wooden coffin in a tomb. Timber cover and base of coffin were attached in dovetail join, walls secured with iron nail and stick. Coffin outer wall was decorated with iron or sheet gold ribbon in a pattern as yurt wall. Spaces in the pattern are decorated with iron or gold quadrifoils with four petals. There were decorations of the shape of sun and moon made of gold, silver, bronze, iron on the head side of corps. The orientations of the excavated tombs of Gol
Mod are directed towards north. This is not the case only in Gol Mod, but also common in other burial sites. Bayanzurkh is the biggest mountain in the north of Gol Mod site. It may give the impression that all the burials are directed towards this mountain, because nomads considered tall and big mountains as sacred places. Researchers concluded that the orientation of burials correspond to the understanding and notion of the other world,
country of dead. G.Menes concluded that “the country of the dead is in the mother land, specifically in the mountain worshipped by the tribe, so a head of a deceased buried in the sunny side of the mountain would be directed to the north without doubt. Depending on the season, this direction can be tilted a bit either to the west or east. This
Golden decoration with embossed pattern, Gol mod, Tomb 20



B
46
notion was evolved into an underestanding that the deceased are to be buried in direction towards north, but with little tilt to west or east symbolizing the transfer of the deceased to the other world”. Ts.Turbat concluded that Mongolians believed in dark world and/or other world, located north or northeast based on their tradition, religious belief and perception of the universe; distinctions
between different directions were defined and symbolized in relation to specific worlds. In the excavated Xiongnu elite tombs, no single intact skeleton is found, however it is thought that as in burials of nomads, deceased noble persons were put head towards north, arms along the body, legs stretched and facing up. It is common not only for Xiongnu nomads, but
Artefacts of Gol mod, Tomb 20



B
47
also among nomads of Central Asia to place the diseased face up to the sky. Xiongnu had a special burial ritual for their chefs and nobles, namely specific articles were placed in the coffin according in strict arrangement. The quality of artifacts found in burials increases with change in size, depth of a tomb as well as the complexity and sophistication of the structure and craftsmanship. Unfortunately, most of the excavated burials were subjected to the ancient plunder and the order of burial objects is either cluttered or obliterated. Nonetheless, it is possible to define original location and differences among artifacts. Also in most of the cases, according to the special mortuary ritual, burial accessories and artifacts were intentionally broken partially or randomly. The placement of funeral accessories and artifacts in the burials were defined by religious belief and the notion of afterlife. According to the shamanisms, not only living organisms, but also objects have spirits. If an object gets broken, then it turns into spiritual state. So the belongings of deceased are intentionally broken and placed in a
Gilded bronze handle of a coffin, Gol mod, Tomb 20
coffin believing the deceased would use them in their afterlife. Specifically, mirrors were broken, potteries were chipped, and complex things were separated believing this act would turn these objects into spiritual state. Consequently Mongolians despise eating and drinking from chipped cups, bowls and looking in a broken mirror. Some researchers categorize Xiongnu elite tombs into the following two types depending on tomb size, depth of pit, similarity of artifacts: The first type: Larger tombs with similar funeral accessories, such as the No.20 of Gol Mod, No.7 of Tsaram, No.20 of Noyon Uul. Chinese style chariot, silver decorations of saddle strap were found from this type of tombs. Also there were lacquer ware, Chinese bronze kettle with high raised stand, cudgel shaped bronze coffin handle, jade artifacts and bronze mirrors in these burials. Coffins were decorated with golden ribbons and floral decoration.
The second type: Small in size with shallow pit, burials Nos.64, 83 of Takhiltyn Khotgor, No.70 of Gol Mod. The main difference from the first type of burial sites is the lack
Sacrifice section of burial, Tamiryn Ulaan khioshuu



B
48
of silver decorations of saddle strap and also there were fewer Chinese style bronze artifacts. Artifacts found from this type of burial are similar to circular tomb (of ordinary persons) in some cases. It is known that from these burials, one can find bronze mirrors, lacquer ware, Chinese bronze kettle with high raised stand, coffin floral decorations. It is worth noting that less weaponry and its accessory are found from the burials of nobles. It is rare to find specifically bow and arrow, sword and spear. Further studies on the location of artifacts found in the tombs are important for the classification of burials. In most of the burials excavated, pottery, kitchen utensils were placed near head of deceased. The accessories of horse and other domestic animals were usually placed on the east walkway of the tomb. (Ch.E.)
Plan of the Burkhiin dorvoljin settlement
BURKHIIN DORVOLJIN Settlement. Xiongnu walled settlement located on the west bank of the East Burkh River, effluent of Kherlen River, Mungunmorit soum, Tuv aimag. The local people named the ruin “Kharaalchiin kherem” (The wall of Witch), in which shamans dwelt in. Kh.Perlee firstly discovered the ruin and explored briefly. The rampart is four sided, sized 180×180m. There are two heaped earth, like remnants from some constructions measured 18×30 m and 28×29 m. The south part of the wall has been flooded by Burkh River water. From the excavation, some fraction of roof-tiles and a square brick with a special pattern were found. All the findings were the similar to findings from Gua Dov and Tereljiin Durvuljin. As a result, there is an evidence that both of the monuments date to the same period in other words, Xiongnu period. (Ts.T.)



C
49
C
AI WENJI. Born in Kaifeng, modern Henan in 177 CE. Poet and composer of the Han period. South Xiongnu attacked China in 195 CE and captured Cai Wenji. She was given to be married to Liu Bao and spent many years of her life among Xiongnu, gave birth to two sons. Cai Wenji’s poem “18 verse of fife melody of Hu” is an interesting evidence about music of Xiongnu. In 207 CE military leader of Han Cao Cao bring back her from Xiongnu by paying large sum of money. (Ch.Е.)
CAP (hat). 3 types of a cap were found in elite tombs of Noyon Uul. Two of them were of a men, 1 was of a woman. The man’s cap had a pointed top whcih was from ancient times commonly used among Eurasian nomads. Its style and design still has been kept among Bashkir people in the West and Kirgiz people in the East who are Turkic speaking people. It was made of 2 similar pieces of fabric attached together (sewn together). The lower brim can cover ears. Main part of the cap was made of felt and woolen cloth. Outer and inner layers were made of beige silk. 2 silk ribbons were sown to the 2 sides of lower part for tight under a chin. Another cap for a man has unique, interesting style and design. Though, it is round shaped and top-hated. It had 2 parts dangling from both sides for covering ears. It was made of thick, soft, black brown felt. 2 lines of thick, woolen fabric with triangular and twisted ornament embroidery of thread on the front part of it. This cap also had 2 ribbons for tight under the chin or behind the head. A scarf made of felt covered with silk for wearing over a head was also found. This is a long piece of material rectangular shape with 4 strings (ribbons)
sewed to 4 angles. In the middle of it, there was a slight curve. Compared to these caps of a man, the woman’s cap found from Noyon Uul was top-hated and pot shaped. It had an inner layer of cotton and an outer layer of beige silk. In 1972, two tombs were excavated in the dune basin of the area Ar Tsaidam of Khangin banner
of Inner Mongolia, and found more than 200 artifacts made of gold, silver and bronze. It became a unique phenomenon in the history of archaeological studies related to Xiongnu. Among these findings, a gold throne and a gold belt of the gold crown of a Xiongnu nobleman were a unique. The gold crown (so called of Chanyu) with a figure of a falcon on it was 7.1 cm high. It weighed 191.1 grams. Diameter of the gold belt was 16.5 cm, and weighed 1020 grams. A figure of a wolf biting a wild mountain sheep was raised above the surface of the front part of the crown. The edge of the crown was cut wavy and was decorated with ornaments. At the upper part of the crown, a falcon with its wings spread was depicted. The bird’s head with a beak, neck, body, tail and claws were
Artefact of the Noyon Uul excavation in 1924
Artefact of the Noyon Uul excavation in 1923
Artefacts of the Noyon Uul excavation in 1924



C
50
intricately designed. The bird’s neck and head were made of azure and a pearl necklace made of gold was worn around its neck. Surprisingly, from the tip of the beak of the falcon through its head, neck and body and tail, gold thread was run. Thus, every time the holder of the gold crown moves to the right and the tail also does accordingly. The lower part of the Xiongnu Chanyu crown- a gold belt had figures of a tiger, a horse and a wild mountain ship raised on the surface it is on and was decorated with twisted ornaments. Due to the fact that this tomb was related to the period of 2000 years ago, other items made of wood, skin, fur, fabric and silk decayed. Therefore it was impossible to figure out what kind of a cap this crown was attached to. It might have been attached the cap made of felt, animals fur or skin. As Chinese researchers concluded, these findings revealed at Ar Tsaidam were related to Xiongnu people of Warring states period. This kind of crown cap became popular in China from the 3rd c. BCE and the upper class people, noblemen, generals had a crown similar to it. It reflected their rank and title with different figures on it. A plenty of artifacts such as pots made of gold, silver, copper, brass and jasper, hair decorations of a woman, earrings were unearthed from the tomb No. 4 of the Xiongnu burial site excavated in the area named Xigoupan of Ordos region of Inner Mongolia. A cap decoration of a woman was made of gold, jade, mussel, metal and brown stone, a necklace was made by threading different kinds of pearls on a string. Chinese archaeologists proposed that the 4th tomb of Xigoupan was the Xiongnu tomb related to the early period of Han Dynasty. The way, how the head and hair decorations were restored was identical with that of many Mongolian national women head dresses. Besides that Japanese researcher Sueji Umehara determined the remaining of a sack shaped item made of birch bark to be one type of a cap. The study of Xiongnu tomb in some places of Mongolian has demonstrated that his hypothesis had some grounds. Big pieces of birch bark with some traces of seams on it, which was preserved well, were found
at Baga Gazaryn Chuluu of Dundgobi aimag. (U.E.)
CARVED IMAGES ON THE BIRCH BARK. One of the basic type of Xiongnu drawings was knife carving on the birch bark. Chariot images on birch bark plate of Tomb No.79 of Burkhan Tolgoi, the human head image of Tomb No.7 of Tsaram site of Transbaikalia, and the chariot image with ger
and flag are carved by above mentioned method. In order to imagine, projected by 2 sides and carved in 2D platter is the important fact of drawings and development of graphic design of Xiongnu. For example, 2 birch bark plates of Tomb No.79 of Burkhan Tolgoy site: first one imagined the
Drawing of a image on the birch-bark, Tomb 79, Burkhan tolgoi.
Artefact of Burkhan Tolgoi, Tomb 79



C
51
two covered chariots, first chariot is imagined with wheel with 8 naves, circle umbrella on the square root, 18 bias line on the umbrella, edge of circle brim is wrecked, and unknown image in front of this chariot. Second chariot imagined with wheel with 12 naves, also unknown image with 9 lines in front of this chariot. Second one also imagined 2 chariots. Third chariot is imagined without naves, 7 vertical lines in the lower brim of the chariot. Fourth chariot is also imagined without naves, similar with others by circle umbrella and square seat and window screen imagined by rooked lines. (Ts.T.; Ch.Ye.)
CATTLE-BREEDING. Xiongnu had a classic nomadic cattle-breeding in the aggregate of the nature of cattle-breeding, and its constitution, maintenance practices, equipments, product types, sub-economic function. After the archaeological data, sheep-breeding occupied major role in Xiongnu cattle-breeding, which is best adapted with nomadic pastoral way of life. Xiongnu had private cattle property, but land and pasture was under the state property. Some scholars who studied Xiongnu cattle number estimating that there were around 19 cattle per person, or more precisely 19±4 per person. This date is based on the calculation of number of arrested or died people and robbed cattle during Han invasion in 72 BCE, which was then calibrated with ethnographic data. The cattle structure is important to study Xiongnu cattle-breeding practice. G.Sukhbaatar wrote that “the small cattle or sheep and goats are playing major role in Xiongnu cattle, second importance is given to horse and cattle/yak, and last place is for camel. There is no discussion on the places of small cattle and camel status in Xiongnu cattle-breeding. But the number of horse and cattle/ yak is doubtful ... we can see such as Xiongnu cattle structure by archaeological data”. After the excavation of Mongolian archaeologist Ts.Dorjsuren at Gol Mod and Noyon Uul sites in 1954-1957, domestic animal bones discovered from all small and bigger 44 tombs and ox, horse and small cattle bones were occupying 90 percent of these bones. Horse bones were most abundant among these bones. Ts.Dorjsuren considered that “horse, ox and small cattle were major cattles for Xiongnu cattle-breeding and it shows that camels were unusual in that time”. The excavation of common burials at Egyin Gol site shows yak and yak-cow hybrid was principal among ox herd. There is several information about Xiongnu cattle-breeding in Chinese historical sources. For example, according to Shi Ji, by Sima
Qian: “The animals they raise consist mainly of horses, cows, and sheep, but include such rare beasts as camels, asses, mules, and the wild horses known as taotu and tuoji”.
Ts.Dorjsuren studied Xiongnu cattlebreeding on the basis of Xiongnu archaeological materials and wrote that: “The bones of cattle, wild animals, fishes and birds from Xiongnu tombs contain mainly domestic cattle bones, say us nomadic cattlebreeding was a major part of economy of Xiongnu”. Mongolian historian G.Sukhbaatar compared archaeological and historical sources and concluded that Xiongnu had 6 kind of cattle as sheep, goat, horse, cow, camel and yak, which are basic cattle of the Mongols until now. The historical and archaeological data about Xiongnu cattle as follows: Horse. Horse breeding had leading role for Xiongnu cattle-breeding. Horse is served simultaneously used as mean of transportation and battle; moreover it is useful with its nutritious meat and milk, and its encolure and tails are serving as primary material for handicrafting many kinds of domestic needs. Horse is not appreciated only for domestic use; it is a main animal of nomadic worshipping. Main part of Xiongnu horses considered to be the steppe breed, perfectly adapted to Central Asian harsh continental climate. According to Russian archaeologist S.I.Rudenko, horses from Xiongnu tomb was low-rise, stumpy, and chunky and rangy strong Mongolian species of horse. Horse bone form Xiongnu tomb of Ivolga was also belonged to Mongolian species of horse by A.V.Davydova. Chinese historians and commentators assessed highly Xiongnu horses. For example, Chinese courtman Chao Tso (200-154 BCE) said about Xiongnu horse: “Xiongnu horses are stronger to climb or cross over the river than Chinese horses”. Also, when officer Liu Jing of Han sent to Xiongnu as a envoy, reported to Gaozong emperor: “Baiyan and Loufang tribes of Xiongnu in the north of Yellow river, locate 700 li from Chang’an. The light cavalry spend one day and one night from there to Qingzhong”. After V.S.Taskin`s analyses, it is mean 160-210 km per day, quite similar with modern day Mongolian horse. During a discussion in Han court in 135 BCE, the Chief Inspector Hang Anguo said against battle to Xiongnu: “No success from a battle in thousand miles far away. Xiongnu are strong by their horses speedy hoofs, they have wild animal hearts, move like birds, in consequence it is trouble to stop them”. Moreover, he repeated his warning again in 134 BCE “Xiongnu warriors are strong and



C
52
speedy like the wind, and appear like a typhoon and disappear like a thunderbolt”. There is an exceptional information about Xiongnu horse color in 110th paragraph of Shi Ji: “West side of Xiongnu all ride white horses, east side ride grayish horses (with white shout), north side ride black horses and south side ride reddish horses”. There were 400 thousand horses of four sides in this battle. Also there is mention about “good breed horse” of Xiongnu which can speed 1000 li (about 500 km) a day on the historical records. Ts.Dorjsuren said that “Xiongnu were herd superior breeds of horses, but these horses used for Xiongnu forces authorities and aristocrats, could not play major role in economic system of Xiongnu”. Dr. Z.Batsaikhan studied this topic by archaeological and historical sources. On the basis of the research made by Russian archaeologist S.P.Nesterov, he said: “It is certain that there were existed famous speedy horses, but galloping 1000 li per day seems to be doubtful... Xiongnu were in close relation with the countries of Middle Asia, and breed this species of horses and even presented in Xiongnu art. For example, horse image on Xiongnu carpet from tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul site, “Horses with carriage” on Yamaan Us petroglyph, “Horse with thin neck” on the bone adornment from tomb No.4 of Burkhan Tolgoi cemetery, are say us that “however Xiongnu were breed good horses independently, but used for elite use and in sacrificing ceremonoes”. Unfortunately, that “good species of horse” still could not been confirmed by paleozoological studies until now. Cattle. There are quite rare mention on Xiongnu cattle in Chinese chronicles. Consequently, we can take some understanding mainly from archaeological data. S.I.Rudenko and A.V.Davydova said that number of cow bones have discovered from Xiongnu tombs, which were similar with steppe breed cows, especially more similar with Kalmyk cow. But the origin of Kalmyk cow is considered to be from Mongolian. Yak. The economic role of yak is same as Mongol cow. Yaks have same economic importance as cow, but yak hair and more fat milks distinguish it. According to French scholar S.Lepetz, some yak bones found among cattle bones from common burials of Egyin Gol cemetery. The image similar to yak on the silver plaque found from the tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul site considered as mythic animal “Khonin goroos-Muskox”, but not exactly the yak image. G.Sukhbaatar noted that: “When Xiongnu lost Qilian mountain, they sing the song telling that Once we lost Qilian mountain, would not breed our 6 kind of cattle, and the sixth cattle must be the yak”.
Sheep. When aggressive armies of Han Dynasty overcame to Xiongnu, many thousands of sheep robbed away. Also sheep bone found in large numbers from Xiongnu tomb excavation attesting that the sheep was the most numerous cattle in Xiongnu. Sheep had been important cattle in nomadic livelihood, which can provide many kinds of important products as meat, milk, wool, felt, woolen cloth and leather. S.I.Rudenko analyzed Xiongnu felt and woolen goods from Xiongnu tombs and wrote that “Xiongnu had a Mongolian species of sheep with 10 micron sized fineness wool of sheep”. Goat. Goat is one of the main cattle for nomads, which also have a high economic importance with leather and cashmere except for meat and milk. There is no especial information about goat in the historical records, but it could be imagine that they very associated with sheep. Researchers consider that goat number might not been most numerous, citing for the reference modern day Mongol nomads cattle structure. The proportion of sheep and goat bones among the whole number of small cattle bones discovered from Ivolga settlement was 22%:3%, is fitting with above mentioned references, but same proportion of bones from Xiongnu tombs is equal to 13%:27% is far more different from previous proportion. Anyway, quantity of goat was not such small among the Xiongnu cattle. Camel. Camels were used for wool, leather, meat and milk, except for transportation. According to Chinese source, camel accounted to strange cattle. The first information about camel of Xiongnu related to 265 BCE. Xiongnu Chanyu sometimes made gift to Han emperor some 2-3 camels, among the huge number of looted animals by Han armies only few camels were mentioned and as belonging to strange cattle, camel was quite rare in Xiongnu. Only one camel bone found from Ivolga settlement, and no camel bone found yet except from Tomb No.23 of Noyon Uul site. Nevertheless, these archaeological sites usually include in non camel breeding region. The reason why Chinese historians comprised the camel in strange cattle, is that the camel might not been spread in China at that time and not widely used for transportation. Strange cattles. In Chinese historical texts a category of animals named Strange cattle was in Xiongnu. Ts. Dorjsuren wrote that “According to the sources, Xiongnu had good species of horses, donkey and mule, but these cattle had no important role to economy. Donkey and mule are not pasture cattle, these are semi or full sedentary cattle. There are many sources about sedentary people who followed Xiongnu or captured by Xiongnu, these people might be used donkey and mule in their livelihood”.



C
53
Donkey and mule are considered as strange cattle. These cattle might be not many, but increased in numbers in Xiongnu cattle structure. For example, according to the source related to 80 BCE, “their donkeys, mules and camels are numerous”. They might be used to use them for transportation. Three animals mentioned among the strange animals of Xiongnu: “kuaiti”, “tansi” and “taotu” have been interested and discussed by many scholars. These scholars have been tried to reconstruct the original pronunciation of these words and determine the exact species of these animals. Nevertheless, it is clear that these cattle have been mentioned along with domesticated animals as donkey, mule and camel. G.Sukhbaatar explained “kuaiti” as a) spotted horse of northern alien tribe, b) foal of stallion and mule, c) hybrid between mare and donkey. Japanese scholar N.Egami explained kuaiti as “sweating”, and this is the name of the horse, which spread from the west into Xiongnu. By E.Pullyblank`s view, “kut” is “horse” by Ket language. V.S.Taskin said, it means “katir” (mule) by Turkish root of language. “Dictionary of Five Languages” of Manchu period noted as “koti mule”. Accordingly, kuaiti means “mule” by Mongolian language, and root of this word is Turkish. “Tansi” explaned as a) wild horse, b) good species of horse, c) spotted wild horse, d) wild horse like as crocodile. Japanese scholar K.Siratori considered as “takhi” (wild horse) by its meaning and pronunciation of Mongolian language. V.S.Taskin analyzed pronunciation “dekhi”, “tekhi” and meaning of “wild horse” and said that this terminology is comparable with “takhi” (wild horse) by Mongolian language and “kulan” by contemporary Turkish language. The pronunciation and meaning of this word is completely analogous with “takhi” by Mongolian language. Traditionally “Taotu” explained as а) beautiful horse of northern land, b) grey wild animal similar with northern sea horse, c) bluish wild horse. V.S.Taskin compared this word with “turti”, “totu” (pony) by Turkish language, and N.Egami read it as “tao-yu” (takhi). G.Sukhbaatar agreed with some scholars as Fang Chuang-yu, it is possible that “taotu” is “khulan” (wild horse) by Mongolian language, “chikhtii” by early pronunciation. Moreover there is an idea “these strange cattle as wild horses occasionally joined with domestic horses and remained sprouts”. By Z.Batsaikhans` view “these steppe horses entered to China by trade or fighting ways and assessed these horses` strong feature mush”. By the archaeological data, there excavated 2 wild horses skulls from Xiongnu tomb
near Darkhan city and about 10 bones from Xiongnu tomb of Transbaikalia. (Ts.T.; Ch.Ye.)
CHANDMANI UUL tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. It is a lonely mountain with big red rocks 2 km south west away from Ulaangom city, Uvs aimag, surrounded by flat valley with short grass. There are many burials and steles from different historical periods. Chandmani burial site is known for its burials with log chambers from Early Iron Age discovered accidentally during quarrying activity for construction. During excavation of these burials, there were fewer Xiongnu burials were discovered. Mongolian-Soviet Archeaological expedition for Xiongnu period and Iron age carried out excavation on two burials (Nos. 29 and 30) in 1973 and four burials in 1974 (Nos. 44, 50, 51) (D.Ts., G.E.)
CHANYU. Term used to name the highest leader of Xiongnu Empire. Luandi clan leaders get this title when they sit on a throne. Chinese translation of this word is “vast”. There are many hypotheses, the most probable one is that this word might mean “good” (sain – in Mongolia) in Mongolian and Chanyu might be the ancient pronunciation of this word. There were 60 well-known Chanyu. The first Chanyu is Tumen, Father of Maodun. After extinction of Xiongnu Empire, Wuhuan, Xianbei were used Chanyu title widely. Later in 9th c. CE the
king of Kidan was named Chanyu. (Ts.Т.)
CHANYU ADDITIONAL SUB-TITLES. Xiongnu called their Chanyu “chenli gutu”, in Xiongnu language “chenli” means “heaven” and “gutu” means “son”. In historical records, “Chenli Gutu Chanyu” meaning “Son of Heaven”, i.e. title of a king. (No.B.)
“CHAPTER OF XIONGNU” IN BOOK OF LATE HAN HISTORY. A special chapter dedicated to Xiongnu in Hou Han Shu. This work was complied by Fan Ye lived in the period of the Southern Dynasties (420-589 CE). He complied the book using some historical records written by previous scholars and oral recitations, even though he lived more than 200 years later, after the Eastern Han. The chapter contains some detailed information on the Xiongnu in the period of the Eastern Han (25-220 CE) those are not recorded in Han Shu. But other historians say that this two books may have some abstractions because both writers lived comparatively later and they did not see the events as witnesses. (Ch.S.)



C
54
“CHAPTER OF XIONGNU” in “HAN SHU”. The work compiled by a dignitary named Ban Gu, who joined several years the army of Han and attended the battle against Xiongnu. This work records history of Xiongnu and political, economical, military and cultural relation between Han and Xiongnu within 230 years from the early period of the Western Han to the Eastern Han. The first half or the part on the previous time of Wudi Emperor, the Western Han might have been copied from other records and notes and some of them might have been added and removed. But the last half is considered to be a valuable source as contained the history of Xiongnu after Wudi Emperor. (Ch.S.)
CHARIOT. Xiongnu were used different kind of chariots widely, for many kinds of functions. The sources related to chariots are consisted artifacts from burials, chariot drawings on petroglyphs and Chinese sources. Burial artifacts mainly discovered from elite tombs. The covered chariot figures on Yamaan Us pethropglyph, Yench soum of Khovd aimag, and Baruun Ovoot pethroglyph of Del mountain, Ulziit soum of Dundgobi aimag, and covered chariot figures on the birch bark articles of Tomb No.79 of Burkhan Tolgoy, and Tomb No.7 of Tsaram are discovered. According to Chinese source, there presented the chariot for Chanyu of Xiongnu from Han dynasty, but no source about Xiongnu chariots` structure and design. But in according to
some unclear sources, Xiongnu had many chariots which made by themselves. We have found remains of chariots from all aristocrats` tombs (19 tombs), which have been excavated until the present. There are many historical facts in Chinese sources as “When Chinese requires a part of land of Chanyu in 8 BCE, but Xiongnu were disagreed to give their woodland for chariot”, “Xiongnu had carriage for 4-6 horses”, “Ichise Chanyu was escaped by mule carriage, when Wei Qing general of Han attacked to him in 109 BCE”, “Han dynasty seized about 1000 chariots from Southern Xiongnu in 109 BCE”, ”... seized about 1000 chariots from Northern Xiongnu” shows that Xiongnu not only used chariots but had their own chariots. Meanwhile only Chinese design
Bronze part of a chariot, Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Chariot image of Yamaan Us



C
55
chariots discovered from Xiongnu elite tombs. There remains of the chariots are similar to Han period Chinese chariots. According to the sources Han court delivered “chariot with seat” and “chariot with drum” to Huhanye Chanyu in 51 BCE, “4 horses` seat chariot with ornament and feather umbrella” gifted to Bi Chanyu in 50 CE, “chariot with black ceiling”, “drum chariot” and “seat chariot” were gifted to Hulanjo Chanyu of South Xiongnu. It must be consider that these notes belongs to later period of Xiongnu-Han relations, after the separation to
Southern and Northern Xiongnu. The chariots, which discovered from Xiongnu tombs can be divided into “Umbrella chariot” and “Cabin chariot” by the design. Also can be divided by practice into horse chariot and ox chariot. The ox chariot figured once in the Del Uul mountain petroglyph. The umbrella chariot mostly discovered from Xiongnu aristocrats` tombs, and cabin chariot only figured in the pethroglyphs and on the birch bark artifacts. Thus for the usage, umbrella chariot was used for the royalty and cabin chariot was used for long journeys. The “chariot with drum”, which is recorded many times in the sources is Chinese “guche chariot”. It included in the type of light chariot and similar to “yaoche chariot”, but set the drum instead of umbrella. This kind of chariot did not discovered yet from Han and Xiongnu tombs, it might not been used for funeral custom. (Ch.Ye., J.G.)
CHIEF OF TEN THOUSAND WARRIORS. There were 14 chiefs of then thousand warriors besides ”10 horned wang”. But all chiefs had not ten thousand warriors, 4 horned wangs had own biggest “private domain” of palace and a thousand warriors. (No.B.)
CHENLI GUTU. Tittle of Xiongnu King. Chanyu was named chenli gutu, in Xiongnu language “chenli” means “sky” and “gutu” means son. In historical records, “Chenli Gutu Chanyu” meaning “Son of Heaven”, i.e. title of a king. (No.B.)
CHEDIHOU CHANYU (101-97 BCE). Great Chanyu of Xiongnu. Grandgrandson of Maodun Chanyu. Son of Ichise Chanyu. The 8th great Chanyu after Modun Chanyu. His brother, Guilihu, the 7th Chanyu during his only one year reign, killed in a battle to seize back the land with Shoufan fortress in Ordos from Han state. During Chedihou Chanyu, aggressive attacks from Han state activated and Xiongnu was facing political difficulties. After the throning Chedihou Chanyu wanting peace, declared that “I am a child. How I can consider war, when Han Emperor is older in age?” and he released and sent back imprisoned Han envoys. Han Wudi thought the above mentioned declaration could be used to destroy the independence of Xiongnu and make Xiongnu dependent on Han. At that time Han dynasty himself was also impoverished due to long war years. Envoy lead Su Wu was sent with presents from Han in 101 BCE. The situation was far more different than thought Wudi, when Chedihou did not give chance to raise discussion about their submission. Su Wu with his deputy Zhang Sheng thought of a plan to collaborate with Chinese nativs in Chanyu court, take queen mother of Chanyu as a hostage and kill Wei Liu, Chanyu advisor with Xiongnu-Chinese mixed blood. They also tried to bribe Gou wang and Yu Chan, close nobles to Chanyu. However, this conspiracy was discovered and naturally angered Chanyu. He executed those
Part of an anclosed chariot (restored drawing)



C
56
who betrayed and offered prisoners to submit to him accepting their guilt. Su Wu denied to submit to Chanyu and was sent on exile to Baikal Lake. After 19 years in exile he returned to his homeland. Because of Chinese conspirators, the relationship between two powers worsened and in 99 BCE great army of Han entered Xiongnu in three fronts. Han general Li Guangli attacked Western Tuqi wang domain and killed and captured more than ten thousand Xiongnu and on the way back he was caught in blockade, lost most of his warriors and barely survived. Second Han army did not encounter Xiongnu army and later returned back. General Li Lin of Eastern army of Han was surrounded by Xiongnu and fought with Chedihou Chanyu, was defeated and captured. Only 400 men from his army got back. According to Han state law, if Chinese ministers, noblemen, generals were captured and surrendered due to stronger opponent’s force, he would be executed for betrayal and/or one of his family members were to bear this punishment. Captured Li Lin had no way to return back, so he submitted to Xiongnu and agreed to serve them. Chedihou gave his daughter to marry, made him a governor and sent him to govern remote Qiangun tribe land in the north-western periphery of Xiongnu. Li Lin loyally served to Xiongnu. Archeologists discovered ruin of his palace in lowland of Minusinsk and carried out excavation. Submission of General Li Lin to Xiongnu left deep impression in China. It was unimaginable for great General of Han to surrender to enemy and was considered to be a great sin. His mother was executed. Ancient historian and well known scholar Sima Qian tried to save his mother’s life saying that general Li Lin must have been in intricate situation, but was claimed to be guilty in wrongdoing himself. He was castrated and thrown into prison. Two months later he was released and promoted to a head of Royal chancellery by Wudi. This was a big victory over aggressive attacks from Han in history of Xiongnu. Two years later, in 97 BCE Han decided to take revenge and sent an army led by General Li Guangli, but was defeated again. Chedihou Chanyu was a skillful military leader, talented organizer and saved his homeland in difficult periods. (Ts.Т.)
CHEREMUKHOVAYA PAD’ tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Buriatia. Covers totally 65575 square metres, where identified 84 tombs. Cemetery was discovered in 1950 by Russian scholar A.P.Okladnikov, A.D.Zaporojskii and R.P.Tugutov. In 1955 A.V.Davydova make plan of the cemetery. In 1957 R.P.Tugutov and N.N.Mamonov carried
out first excavation, and in 1961 R.P.Tugutov and E.A.Khamzina excavated 1 burial, after that in 19651966 P.B.Konovalov excavated 18 burials. They were able to discover a large quantity of artifacts, including different kind of pottery, iron arrowheads, knives, belt decoration and hooks, horse equipments, bone and horn artifacts: chopsticks, bow plates. This collection now in Kyachta regional museum. (Ts.E.)
CHOUNU Khan (508-520 CE). Rouran khan. Also known also as Chunu. After his father’s death by High Carts-Tele, he inherited the throne and took “Strong state” name and “Prosperous” tittle. In 516 he attacked High Carts-Tele tribal federation and had them under his control. He had friendly relationship with Tabgach-Wei state. Chounu Khan relied too much on fortune-teller Shidouhun Diwani, who later organized his assassination and started rebellion against state. Chounu Khan was defeated by Afujilo, a leader of High Carts-Tele in 520 and later was killed in the hands of their own people. Anahuan Khan became his successor. (Ts.Т.)
