“CHRONOLOGY OF XIONGNU HISTORY”. The book was compiled by a well known Chinese scholar Lin Gan in 1982 and published in Beijing. Official historical records of China, “Shiji-Historical records” by Sima Qian,”Han shu-History of Han Dynasty” by Ban Gu were used to extract the records presenting chronology of Xiongnu history to write this book. The book is divided into three chapters. The first chapter contains records on Warring States, Qin Dynasty and Han Dynasty period Xiongnu activities. The second chapter contains records on Wei and Jing Dynasty periods Xiongnu activities. The Third chapter contains materials related to Xiongnu history that are complicated to date to certain period of history. Apart from this, there are annexes containing historical short records and articles on the order of succession to the throne of Chanyu of Xiongnu, History of Xiongnu ancestry. Author considered Xiongnu as a specific nation and recorded as “Hu” or “Xiongnu”, thus chronological records related to other nations are not included in this book. For instance, word “Hu” is encountered many times in the records from Warring States, Qin and Han Dynasty period. This word refers to Xiongnu, but it is not the case all the time. Particularly, the records of Wuling wang of Zhao principality, 307 BCE that says “Wear clothes of Hu, ride a horse and shot with bow and arrow in Hu manner”. Hu mentioned in this record is not Xiongnu, Linghu and Loufan are different nations and not included in this book. Records on Xiongnu start from 318 BCE in Chinese narratives. For example, in “Records



C
57
of Qin state”, the 5th volume of Shiji, Han, Zhao, Wei, Yan, Chi sates relied on Xiongnu force to fight against Xiongnu”. This is an important reference for Xiongnu studies, since the historical records on Xiongnu are arranged in their order of occurrence in time. (А.P.)
CLOTHES. Archeological excavations discovered remains of some Xiongnu clothes. But they mostly
are scraps of fabric or silk and buttons or ornamental studs on clothes. For example, a scrap of green silk which is proposed a remain from the clothes found from the grave of the Tevsh Uul aimag as well as some findings purposed for any decoration on the clothes such as fade toggles found from the Xiongnu grave of the Baga Gazriin Chuluu, the Tomb No.2 of the Duurlig Nars and the Tomb No.20 of the Gol Mod; gilded bronze horse figures found from the Tomb No.2 of the Takhiltyn Khotgor, Tomb No.9 of the Noyon Uul and Duurlig nars; and flower shaped gold decoration with a turquoise set found from the Tomb No.20 of the Gol Mod etc. There is no any other grave except for one of the Noyon Uul, which has comparatively well kept two kinds of gowns, hat and pants. These findings found in 1924, have been considered to be the basic materials for researching Xiongnu clothes. However, they are in doubt to be the traditional costumes of Xiongnu as their design, made and purpose. The hat
with earflaps has no top, is fastened with a couple of ribbons on the back and has a pattern like eye quilted with thread on the front. Consequently, this can feature it a ceremonial hat but not a headdress to protect from wind in winter time. So some researchers viewed it was a shaman hat. This explanation could by i accordance with the truth. As for the silk gown, its wide sleeves prove it is not a costume of the nomads. Archeological excavations have found
Gown, Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Pants, Noyon Uul, Tomb 20



C
58
many findings from the elite tombs, which can be accounted to be gifts from Han and other foreigner countries. There is a fact in written sources that Han Dynasty gave some clothes to Xiongnu Chanyu as a present. As a result, these findings found from the Xiongnu grave of the Noyon Uul can be proved the presents from the foreigners. (G.E.)
“COLLECTION OF SCIENTIFIC ARTICLES ON XIONGNU”. The collection was compiled by Lin Gan, Xiongnu scholar of China and published in Beijing, in 1993. He selected and compiled 38 scientific articles and abstracts of 50 scientific articles published in Chinese during the 60 years period from 1919-1979. Succession order of Chanyu of Xiongnu, maps of Xiongnu were published as annex. 38 scientific articles included in this collection covers wide range of topics such as Xiongnu origin, social structure, Chanyu palace, ethnic connections between Mongols and Xiongnu, language, culture and tradition of Xiongnu, archaeological research carried out in Inner Mongolia. On the origin of Xiongnu, Hong Weibi wrote in an article “Xiongnu origin” that tribal names, namely “Guifang”, “Hunyu”, “Kunyi”, “Xianyun” did not originate from Xiongnu, they were of Qian origin. But “Linhu”, “Loufan” and others were core of Xiongnu. Furthermore, in his article “Chanyu Palace from South Han period” he wrote that territory of Xiongnu stretched to North from Great Wall towards Baikal Lake and had two palaces in south and north of Gobi. North palace of Chanyu was in the Valley of Orkhon River, near Kharbukhyn Gol ruin east to Khangai Mountain. Ruin of Kharbukhyn Gol was concluded to be a ruin of palace of Kidan period that is Longcheng palace of ancient Chanyu palace. South palace of Chanyu was on the slope of Yingshan Mountain near Ordos, during the reign of Maodun Chanyu of Xiongnu, Xiongnu fought against Qin state of China by Yingshan Mountain. In “Development of Xiongnu society” article, Wu Yansi wrote on the development of social structure and culture of Xiongnu extensively. According to this article, Xiongnu was a nation comprising of many tribal states and the population was 3 million during Maodun Chanyu reign. He concluded that in 3rd c. BCE animal husbandry, farming and handicraft were well developed which became good basis for the establishment of Xiongnu Empire in 209 BCE. He disagreed with many Chinese scholars who thought Xiongnu social structure was based on slavery and said Xiongnu was state with feudal social system. For instance, Chanyu was a supreme ruler, formed a system of political organization with the left, middle and right
branches of Xiongnu and applied multiplicative principle of administration with 24 great governors of detachments of one thousand. There are number of articles on archaeological excavation, such as “Brief introduction to Xiongnu burial”, “Xiongnu burial of Taohongbala”, “Some Xiongnu burials discovered near Zhao village, West Tsahar, North Hoshuu of Inner Mongolia”. According to “Some Xiongnu burials discovered by Zhao village, West Tsahar, North Hoshuu of Inner Mongolia”, Gai Shanlin wrote that the burial was discovered 1 km south of village of Zhao clan of in spring of 1969. The burial is located in lowland surrounded by mountains on 4 sides. The burial site is enclosed by dirt bank 500 m from east to west, 200 m from north to south and there were wooden coffins in some, but no wooden coffin in others. According to the person who discovered the burials, there were an iron dagger and a sword placed along the shoulder; golden jewelry was put in the mouth and pottery behind a head of a deceased. Stone was placed as a pillow in some burials. It was also noted that there were golden, bronze, iron objects, tools, weaponry and
pottery in the burials. (А.P.)
“COMPARATIVE ARCHAEOLOGICAL STUDY OF NORTHERN STEPPE-BRONZE AGE TO BEGINNING OF XIONGNU”. Author Unen-Yosutu (Wu En). Beijing, 2008. Written by whole collected data of author. Used lots of data which published in Mongolian, Chinese, English, Russian, Korean and Japanese languages and attached 200 pictures related to nomadic history. Expressed his view about nomadic relationship on Northern steppe, and compared archaeological cultures of Early Bronze, Early Iron age and Xiongnu period which found from Chinese northern land. (Ch.S.)
“CULTURE OF XIONGNU AND THE TOMBS OF NOIN ULA”. Author S.I.Rudenko. It was published in Leningrad in 1962. Consists of introduction, 11 chapters, conclusion, and illustrations in the appendix. 204 pages, He described the Xiongnu royal tomb at Noyon Uul excavated by the P.K.Kozlov expedition in 19241925 and demonstrated artifacts revealed from the tomb. He studied the Xiongnu economy, husbandry and agriculture, housing, clothes, accessories and decorations, transportation vehicles, weaponry, history of manufacturing, social development, religion, faith and belief, spiritual life, foreign relations precisely using findings, artifacts, historical sources, research reports and documentary books. (Na.B.)



D
59
D
ABAODANG tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. Located near the ruined city Dabaodang of East Han period on the territory of Dabaodan village of Shenmu county, Shaanxi province. Between 1996 and 1998, 26 brick tombs with mausoleum were unearthed. All those were burials with mausoleum which had slope doors directed to the north and from one to several rooms. They had the dome like a Mongolian ger. Despite the fact that the mausoleum organization, most of findings found from the mausoleum and carved stone statue were the same as the mausoleum of the Han dynasty, the burial ritual was revealed by that the findings such as a vase with wavy ornaments on it, bone application to a bow, a spoon made of bone, chopstick made of bone which are similar to those often found from the burial ground related to Xiongnu were but by the shoulders of the buried person and a horse head bone was accompanied the buried person. The people who were buried in the grave were determined to be related to mongoloid of Northern Asia based on the analysis made in the bone of the person found from this burial ground. Scholars concluded that this burial was of the sinisized Xiongnu people who lived from the beginning to the end of the Eastern Han dynasty. (Ch.S.)
DABAODANG town. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. Located on the territory of Dabaodan village of Shenmu county of Shaanxi province. This city had a 5-side rampart. In 1999, there was excavation conducted by the museum of Shaanxi province. Interesting findings such as a vase with wavy ornaments on the upper part of it which is a distinctive feature of Xiongnu culture, wushu coins related to Han Dynasty and a bronze stamp of the Xiongnu official who submitted to Han Dynasty were revealed. Scholars interpreted the script of the stamp in several different ways and determined the name of the owner of this stamp to be Taiji. Based on the artifacts found during the archaeological excavation and historical sources and writings, the city is considered to be the city where the Xiongnu people who submitted to Han Dynasty were inhabited and it was founded in the latter period of the Eastern Han Dynasty. Information
about burial around of the Han dynasty found in the surrounding areas of the city was published in the scholarly works in 2001. (Ch.S.)
DA DANGHU WANG. This is the name of position or title. It is considered that if the old form of the name of position “Danghu” of Xiongnu is restored it appears to be the word of the contemporary Mongolian language “daruga” (No.B.)
DAI State (310-376). The state founded by Tabgach people of Xianbei origine. The capital city Shengle was located near Khoringer, district of Huhhot, Inner Mongolia, China. After Toba Yilu prince was awarded the title “Dai State wang” by the Jin Dynasty in 310, this name became known. Toba Yilu wang was awarded this title in recognition of his help the dynasty with fighting against the Han Zhao state of Xiongnu. Dai State was expanded and reorganized into Dai Empire. Although Dai was invaded by Jin Dynasty in 376, the Tabgach people became more powerful and founded the Toba Wei state in 386. (Ts.T.)
DAILING. One of the places related to the history of Xiongnu is Dailing. According to information of Shiji, Xiongnu people used to hold a big assembly in Dailing in autumn when horses became fatter and put on weight. But, later Chinese commentator Yan Shigu (581-645) explained, “Offering and worshipping around trees is called Dailing”. This is the autumn offering of Xianbei tradition which was inherited from the ancient period of time. Since there was no tree, the willow was stuck. A lot of horsemen go round the willow 3 times. Thus, N.Ya. Bichurin followed this explanation and considered this word to mean “to go round tree”. Mongolian scholar G. Sukhbaatar wrotes, “Kitan people also made offerings in autumn and said it “daila” in Kitan language. Referring to the above mentioned idea and the old version of the pronunciation, it is quite possible to be true words “tailga” or ”takhilga” in Mongolian language.” (Na.B.)
DANILOV, Sergei Vladimirovich (1950-). Russian archaeologist. Wrote more than 50 scientific works. He had worked as the chef of the archaeological group of the Institute of Mongolian, Buddhism and Tibetan Studies of the Siberian branch of the Russian Academy of Sciences. In 1974, he graduated from the State University of Buryatia named after D. Banzarov, Russian Federation by specialization “Historian”. From 1976 to 1980, he worked as a research fellow in the Institute of Archaeology of the Academy of Sciences, Russian Federation.



D
60
In 1988, he defended his doctor’s dissertation in history entitled “Making offerings with livestock is the source of religious beliefs of the nomads”. Since 1987, he has been doing research on “Nomads settlements and towns” based on the findings of artifacts unearthed from the middle age settlement (Narsata, Husutai) and Xiongnu settlements of the Transbaikalia (Bayan-Undur). He wrote more than 10 articles on history of Xiongnu. He made his contributions to the studies of nomads of Inner Asia by publishing the work “Settlements and towns in nomads’ communities of Central Asia. Ulan-Ude, 2004”. (M.Ts.)
DAODONGZI tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. Xiongnu period located in 8 km North East of the center of Wangtuan village, Tongxin county, Ningxia Hui Autonomous region, PRC. Archaeological excavations was done from 1983 to 1985, and within the framework of this research excavated 32 tombs. Out of them, materials concerning 27 tombs materials were published. Most tombs were headed toward the north. Among them, there were 20 ordinary tombs with rectangle shaped burial pit, 6 combs with niche and 1 tomb with a stone fence. Wooden chambers were revealed from some elderly individus tombs. A small niche was made in the north part of pit of most tombs in which a vase, laquer vessels, and other accessories were put in it. In the excavations, a ceramic pot with wavy ornaments around its upper part, bronze decorations
of a belt, a bronze bell, a bronze knife, wushu coins and a laquer cup were found. A horse head, cattle head, sheep head and other bones were found in the tombs with niche. The Tomb No.22 is an infant’s grave with 12 big pieces of stone and a fence around it. There are few artifacts in it. In accordance with historical sources, the Xiongnu people who submitted to Han lived in his area. The Han court founded the administration unit An Dingjung especially for those people. Researchers view this monument as the cemetery of Xiongnu people who lived in this area around the 2-1 c. BCE (Ch.S.)
DARKHAN tombs. Xiongnu period burial site in Mongolia. Located on the right side of a small hill which lays on the
right part of the Tumurt Mountain, in the valley of the Kharaa River near the Old Darkhan city, Darkhan Uul aimag. A.P.Okladnikov first informed
Artefacts of the Darkhan Tomb.
Artefacts of the Darkhan Tomb.



D
61
about this site in 1949. It was damaged during the preparation of construction sand quarry. Therefore, there needed preventive excavation. Researchers of the Mongolian-Soviet joint Historical and Cultural Expedition arrived in the site they found out that the stone of the burial ground exterior part was excavated and damaged by a bulldozer. They identified 6 tombs by discovering slight marks of boundary of tombs on the surface and excavated them. (G.E.)
DATANG KHAN (414-429). The Emperor of Rouran. He is also named Tatar khan. During his reign, there was the outbreak of war between Rurang and Tabgach which lasted for a long time. In 429, Datang Khan was defeated by the outnumbered military forces of Tabgach-Touba and died of illness. He became so well known that some part Rouran people began to be named after him. After the Rouran Empire was collapsed, the rest of its population named themselves as “Tatar people”. This is a common name of all Mongolian speaking tribes of that period of time. In the 12-13th century, Tatar tribe inherited this name. (Ts.T.)
DAVYDOVA, Antonina Vladimirovna (19272000). Russian archaeologist. Worked as a scientific fellow in the Institute of Material Culture of SanktPetersburg, Russian Federation. She wrote more than 100 scientific works. She graduated from the State University of Leningrad in 1954. In 1955, she had been as the chef of the archaeological excavation at the site of the Lower Ivolga. In 1965, she defended her doctor’s dissertation entitled “Settlement of Ivolga – Xiongnu monuments in the Transbaikalia”. This dissertation work covered important issues in the Ivolga settlement and cemetery archaeological research. As a result of A.V. Davidova’s work, a collection of more than 3000 rare artifacts related to the Xiongnu state period of time was known. Now, these findings have been kept in the local museum of Kiakhta named after A.Obruchev, The Historical museum of Buryatia and State Hermitage Museum. A.V.Davidova made a valuable contribution to the Xiongnu research by her works such as “Ivolga settlement. Archalological Monuments related to Xiongnu. Volume 1. SPb., 1995” and “Ivolga Tombs.
Archaeological Monuments related to Xiongnu. Volume 2. SPb., 1996”. (M. Ts.)
DAWAN. Fergana valley located on the territory of present day Uzbekistan was named as Dawan by Chinese ancient narratives. Well known traveler of Han period, Zhang Qian first mentioned about this country. The emperor Wudi was attracted by the information about horse with bloody sweat originally came from the sky. The horse was said to be in the country Dawan with the capital city Ershi. They were at war with Dawan several times in order to catch the horses to use them as military horses when fighting with Xiongnu. The emperor Wudi granted the general Li Guangli the title “Ershi general” and ordered him to conquer Dawan and bring those horses. According to Chinese narratives general Li Guangli tried twice and brought a few of those horses. (Ts.T.)
DECORATION OF CRUPPER AND BREASTSTRAP. The most important item to harness the chariot is horse fittings. Researchers view that since the first primitive saddle (Shookhoi-Yangia), people have used crupper and breast-strap to make it stable on the horse back. They are used for equaling the load when going up and for not making the cart slip when going down. Numbers of decoration on the crupper and breast-strap have been found from the Xiongnu elite graves in Transbaikalia and Mongolia. This kind of decoration was found from the graves which have kept horse-chariots decorated with lacquer and bronze fittings. These decorations
Gol Mod, Tomb 20



D
62
found from the Xiongnu graves are basically the same in shape of circle and oval and in made of embossed silver. Some decorations are made of silver gilded with gold. Also a bronze decoration was found from the Tomb No.7 of the Tsaram. The decorations of the crupper and breast-strap found from the Xiongnu graves mostly have figures of mythic animals, and sometimes deer and ibex on them. We can example some mythic animals like the unicorn antelope (Tomb No.20, Gol Mod, Tomb No.20, Noyon Uul), dragon (Tomb No.20, Noyon Uul), yak (Tomb No.6, Noyon Uul), deer (Tomb No.6, Noyon Uul) and ibex (Tomb No.7, Tsaram). The animal on the silver decoration with a sheet frame, which was found from the Tomb No.6 of the Noyon Uul, is viewed as a yak by some researchers and as a mythic animal by others. The face and appearance standing on a mountain encourage the second view. The clear figure of other animals helps the agreeable view of the researchers. There are many mythic animals displayed on not only crupper and breaststrap also felts and bone objects found from the Xiongnu elite graves. The figures of artificial animals found from the Xiongnu graves of aristocrats like these, express they were more distinguished people than ordinary men. On the other hand, finding them from burial place can be explained concerning with the concept on the other world. (Ch.E.)
DEEL (Clothes). It was written in Chinese resources that Xiongnu people wore clothes made of skin and fur. It was all about ordinary herdsmen who were the most of the population. However, nobles and other elites liked to wear clothes made up of Chinese silk. Silk ware had been popular among nomads through trade and war benefits. Thus, among findings unearthed from the elite tombs of the Xiongnu Chanyu at Noyon Uul, there were mostly clothes made of silk.
Scholars concluded that these were real Xiongnu clothes in terms of their style and design. It was written in Chinese narratives that Xiongnu people ate livestock meat, wore its fur or skin and used skin or a woolen blanket. It indicates that Xiongnu people were nomads-herdsmen. Thus, they used livestock origin products for a household living and wore clothes made of fur and skin well adapted to the Central Asian extreme and severe weather condition. Moreover, their clothing was relatively different from that of settled people in the way that their clothes were suitable to ride a horse for long hours, to raise livestock and do any manual work with much movement with their nomad’s lifestyle and husbandry in which they were engaged. A philosopher Confucius, who lived in 551-479 BCE, noted that northern nomads wore clothes with the front part which was incorrectly cut. It was related to the preXiongnu period and it indicates that the nomad’s style of clothing had been inherited by following generations for a long time. Two complete deel and the part of another one deel were found from the Tomb No.6 at Noyon Uul. One of them was beige colored (initially it was red) made of light silk, 117 cm long, and 194 cm wide from the left edge of one sleeve to the light edge of another sleeves. A collar, the edges of a front part and a lower part were trimmed with sable fur. In addition, a line of fur was attached to the shoulder and the upper back part of it. These pieces of fur were sewed with brown and reddish woolen thread. Next one was a warm deel of ordinary style. It was made of the silk. The difference was in its inner layer made of thin felt. The edge of a collar, the lower part, the edge of sleeves and the front part were trimmed 10 cms of the inner thin felt layer. There were no seams in the rest part of the deel to combine the outer and inner layers together. The
r
d
d
r
Gol Mod, Tomb 20
Noyon Uul, Tomb 6
Noyon Uul, Tomb 20



D
63
remaining of the 3rd deel was similar to the above mentioned 2nd deel in its style. There was another type of clothing among other clothes with unclear usage and function. Researchers concluded that it might have been used as a deel’s collar part or as a gown for wearing on the shoulders. Generally, it was trapezoid shaped clothing made of 4 pieces of felt added by sewing. It had a silk outer layer and 2 strings attached to the lower part of it. (U.E.)
DEFENSIVE STRUCTURES. Actually three ruins of the defensive constructions have been well studied: the Ivolga settlement, on the Selenge River, the Bayan-Undur fortress, on the Dzida River, and the Mangirtuy fortress on the Khilgo River, all in Transbaikalia. As they were all constructed in northern periphery of Xiongnu, they might have frontier defensive feature. Among them, we emphasize the ruin of the Bayan-Undur. It is located on the east bank of the Dzida River, in distance of 15 km in the west of Derestuy village in Dzida county, Buryatia. An expedition led by S.V.Danilov excavated on this site in 1995-1998. It has 70×70 m dimension, rectangular
shape and has double fortified ramparts surrounding it. Outside rampart has 0.5-0.8 m height from the surface and 10 m width with some broken parts. The inside rampart has some connected structures on the south and is 20-25 m in distance from the outside rampart, 40×40 m dimension, 10-15 m width and 1.1-1.15 m height. The both ramparts are tied with a big stone (like chain in shape). The inside damthe higher one, was likely to be built at first and tied the big stone. The inside rampart was built in laying stone with earth and enclosed with a narrow pole with 10-15 cm in diameter. Thus, a narrow channel was dag in 45-50 cm in depth and some big stones were laid and fixed in the channel. On some parts, there was a post with a point on each end, put down. As result from the excavation, this ruin was made with the same way or technique to the Ivolga settlement. The structure of both ruins is the same. They both are far from each other, and the inside ramparts are higher. In the 70 m2 place at the northwest corner of the inside wall, remnants of a dwelling and some sherds of Xiongnu pottery, Chinese bronze mirror, bones from horse, ox and sheep, fish scale were found but not any trace of the agriculture.
Gol Mod, Tomb 20



D
64
In written sources, there is a note about the fortification built by Zhizhi Chanyu that “Chanyu sent the citizens to build a city wall. Every day, 500 people worked and they finished it after 2 years... There were two double wooden palisades in front of the wall”. It may be the defensive construction. Even though the construction was defensive, it was not used during any war or battle. It might only have been used for the soldiers’ temporary stay and a base of military activities like preparing or keeping their weapons. Mongolian scholar, Kh.Perlee notes about two fortified cities that “In 123-119 BCE, the Xiongnu Chanyu had a fortified city built in the Zhan Yan Mountain for a military commander, Zhao Xin, who joined Xiongnu and named the city Zhao Xin cheng after his name. And there was another city for his wife named Fanfujen cheng (in front of the Khunui River) in 90s BCE”. But the ruins of these cities have not been yet identified with certitude. However, the constructions above mentioned, can be an evidence of not only a new form of the military fortification and defense but also a city construction in the nomadic civilization. (N.E.)
De GUIGNES Joseph. French orientalist and sinologist. The author of the “Mémoire historique sur l’origine des Huns et des Turcs”, published in 1748, Paris, and “Histoire générale des Huns, des Mongoles, des Turcs et des autres Tartares occidentaux”, Paris, 1757. He was born on October 17, 1521, at Pontoise of Ile de France district. He became an interpreter of Eastern languages, sinologist and translator at University of London in 1742. His cousin influenced very much to earn this knowledge. His uncle was a highly educated teacher of the university at the time and he took with him young De Guignes to China and in 1742 sat him a student of a Chinese scientist. When his teacher died in 1745 he came back to
France and was appointed as King’s interpreter. In his book “Histoire générale des Huns” he mainly used Chinese historical sources and by this he is the first Western scholar to present eastern historical records in a Western language. He originated the proposal that the Huns who attacked the Roman Empire were the same people as the Xiongnu mentioned in Chinese records. This view was popularised by his contemporary scholars. He became a member of the academy of Belles Lettres of France in 1754 and in 1757 was appointed as head of the department of the College de France. Later he had been working in Louvre museum. He died in Paris in March 22, 1800. (Ch.Ye.)
DERESTUY tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Buriatia. Derestuy cemetery is situated 200 km from Ulan-Ude city, in the Dzida river valley on the opposite side of the Gujirmeg cemetery. The main plan of Derestei cemetery is similiar to the plans of cemeteries of the Xiongnu. This monument was discovered by Yu.D. Tal’ko-Gryntsevich in 1900, who launched an excavation of 26 burials. In 1929 Russian researcher G.P.Sosnovskii visited this site, also in 1949 Russian scholar A.P.Okladnikov carried out a small-scale survey and after that 16 years later in 1965, P.B.Konovalov excavated 6 small tombs. The tombs had a stone circular mound on the surface and the internal construction consisted of a wooden coffin placed into a stone cyst or a wooden chamber. A large number of artifacts were found in the graves, the most common are the belts, bronze plaques and other details of clothing, ceramics, glass and stone beads, etc. The basic collection from Derestuy consists of bronze artifacts. Artistic bronze openwork belt plaques were found on the women’s and men’s tombs. Some of them have simple designs, others were rather elaborate. Small bronze artifacts are buttons, spoon-like clasps, ornamental belt buckles. Iron and bronze horse equipment, mussel pendant, iron gavelock, earrings,
Xiongnu settlement with the wall Drawn by B.Erdenesaikhan



D
65
stone and glass beads, pottery, charcoal, bronze arrowheads, a knife with bone handle, stone and glass beads, pottery and horse, sheep, cow bones. These are all artifacts which are exhibited now in Kyachta Regional Museum. ( Ts.E.)
DEULUN (185-491). Rouran emperor. He was also named Tulun. After his father Ijin Khan died, he came to the throne by being granted the title “Bugdiin” (Of all people) and “The most peaceful” in 485. He maintained the policy to fighting with Tabgach-Wei Dynasty. On the other hand, he had been at war with Afuchjilo of Uighur to defeat their uprising. Nagai emperor was his successor. (Ts.T.)
DIARY PRODUCTS. Xiongnu nomads practiced classical nomadic husbandry, thus there is no doubt
that they perfected the diary milk product processing technologies. Based on the Chinese sources and archeological artifacts one can get an understanding of dairy products of Xiongnu. According to the narratives of Shiji, Chinese eunuch Zhonghang Yue who submitted to Xiongnu once advised to Laoshang Chanyu that “Food from Han should be thrown away and should not be accepted, to show that milk-chi, airag-dun lao are better than their food”. From the above it can be concluded that there were two main types of dairy milk drinks during Xiongnu period, which are milk and airag. The word “dong lao” was translated as cheese by V.S.Taskin, but Mongolian scholar G.Sukhbaatar corrected it and translated as airag - koumys. E.Pullyblank also explained the word “lao” as airag. In one Chinese chronicle it is noted that Wuhuan people that was once part of Xiongnu also drunk airag. This source can be also seen are indirect evidence. Also one important reference to the diary product of Xiongnu mentioned in this source is that General Li Lin of Han, who surrendered to Xiongnu, wrote that former Chinese envoy Su Wu had mutton and drunk laojiang. V.S.Taskin translated “laojiang” as “fermented milk”, which may have been yogurt. In the third reference, it says that in 11 BCE, during Chinese attack to Xiongnu, they broke down “fenwen” - ox driven mobile gers, and “milo” was put on fire. According to Chinese dictionary “milo” is a name for dried airag, which is actually aaruuldried curd. In the chapter about Xiongnu in the Shiji, it says that “those who capture a prisoner or beheaded enemy would be rewarded with a cup of alcohol (jiu)”. Since Xiongnu consumed dairy products and drunk airag, the “jiu” alcohol, they must have been distilled alcohol from dairy products. Larger sized pots with a hole close to the bottom that were discovered several times from Xiongnu burials and Xiongnu settlements are seen as a container to keep wheat by Ts.Dorjsuren, but A.B.Davydova, S.I.Rudenko and Ts.Turbat suggest these vessels could have been for keeping alcohol and airag. Another type of diary product that is mentioned in the archeological references is sour milk cheese. Several pots with holes were found in Xiongnu settlements in Ivolga and Boroo, which is suggested by archeologists to be a utensil to strain this type of cheese. (Ts.Т.)
“DIFFERENCE BETWEEN XIONGNU AND XIANGBEI’S MONUMENTS IN HAN PERIOD”. The Proceeding of Scientific articles of the VI conference organized by the Archeological
Plan of the Dyrestyu cemetery



D
66
Community, 1987. Editor is Unen-Yosutu (Wu En). Like some Inner Mongolian aimags such as Khulunbuir, Ulaankhad and Ulaantsav, have kept a few Xiangbei monuments, Mongolia and the Transbaikalia are places where a large number of Xiongnu monuments have been discovered. The territories near Ordos, Inner Mongolia have Xiongnu graves but a comparatively few. They cannot reveal the character of the pure nomadic civilization. In the work, he emphasizes there is no much difference between Xiongnu and Xiangbei graves but much difficulty to distinguish them due to the similarity of nomadic family, lifestyle and custom. And the fact that the sources have no much more definite information on their funeral customs, is another reason why it is difficult to distinguish them. In addition, the author mentions in the article about the difference of material, design, pattern of pottery, weapons, accessories, burial structure and funeral customs between Xiongnu and Xianbei monuments, adding the possibility to distinguish them if we can do comparative research on archeological findings and source materials. (Ch.S.)
DINLING tribe. Probably ancient Turkic speaking people. The name of this people had been mentioned in historical narratives from the 3rd c. BCE to the 5th c. CE. They inhabited in the today’s mountainous region of Sayan-Altai. During the Maodun Period, they submitted to Xiongnu. During the Xiongnu state weakness, it won its independence and attacked intensely their former suzerain. It is customary to describe Dinling people with red hair and four eyes, as having European race in Chinese sources. Dili and Tele tribes which are mentioned in historical sources related to the beginning of the middle ages are considered to be related to Dinling. (Ts.T.)
DI tribes. This is the name of group of tribes pastured in the north of the China in the 7th-4th centuries BCE as noted in the ancient Chinese sources. Their ethnic and anthropological affiliation are controversial. Researchers believe that there was “Red Di”, “White Di” people when they began to be first mentioned in history since 7th century BCE. In accordance with Sima Qian, in the king of Wen Gong (765-71 BCE) of Jin state, Rong and Di people were forced to migrate driven out and since they began to live in the west of the Huanghe river, Chi Di (Red Di) and Bai Di (White Di) were distinguished and they had wideranging relationships with Jin and Zhou states. In the 6th century BCE, “White Di” people moved to the east and founded Xianyun state at the beginning of the 5th century BCE in the north of Taihangshan. The origin of Di people is unknown. A.Maspero considers
them to be Chinese, G.L.Grum-Grjimailo to be Proto-European, F.Hirt, B.Eberhard, Ma Changshou to be ancient Turkic, Go Mo-jo, M.B.Kryukov to be Skythian, E.A.Borovkova to be Tokharian speaking people, R.W.Vyatkin and L.S.Vasiliyev, based on its geographical location, to be a mixture of the variety of northern tribes. Most Di people who settled in the north of China during the Zhou state period were formed into a relatively independent small state named Zhongshan, during the Warring States period. Although the state was defeated by the Wei state of China in 430 BCE, it had been existed until 295 BCE. Some historians consider that Di people were not horsemen nomads, but they had infantry and a social organization of a settled nation. There is no evidence that they raised livestock and other animals in historical sources. Their economy is determined as follows, “Population of this area were nomads, hunters and fishers and had mixed economy including husbandry”. As a result of their great, continuous movement in the 8-6th centuries BCE, Di people settled in the vast areas from the great bend of the Huanghe river and Ordos to Taiyuan, to the west of Yan state. The attempt to classify a variety of tribes surrounded China was first made at the beginning of the Zhou state. “These were Rong tribes in today’s Gansu and Tibetan region, Yi tribes in the East and North Eastern areas, Man tribes in the south and Di tribes in the north...”, V.S.Taskin wrote. This location of Di people prevented China encountering directly with northern nomads for centuries. White Di people had been reported to be ancestors of Mongolian people by Mongolian historians of Middle ages. They had been reported as ”Bide, Bede or Bete state”. (S.U.)
DOG BURIAL CUSTOM. The dog burial custom spread in Central Asia since Neolithic period. Mongolian archaeologist G.Menes said that “According to the historical and archaeological data Xianbei and Wuhuan used the dog in the funeral custom and the Mongolian ethnographical data tell us the dog burial is the one of the main subject of ancient ethnogenesis of the Mongols. This is the important issue to clarify furthermore”. D.Tseveendorj found a linguistic fact of dog burial of ancient Mongols, and said “Mongolians call the dog “”khoilog” and since early times there were a custom to follow the dog for the funeral with its master and originated “khoilog custom”, likewise a funeral custom with the belongings and goods practiced as “to khoilog”. The dog burial in Wuhuan funeral custom is firstly described in Chinese sources “Hou Han Shu” of Fan Yue, and “Wei Shu” of Yu Huan. According to the commentator Fan Yue “... Put the corps of the



D
67
deceased in the coffin and mourn. But send off by singing and dancing. Feed the deceased masters` dog and horse which tied by motley ribbon and burn up all of belongings and clothes of the deceased master. They said that the dog ensures the safety of the soul on the road to Qishan mountain”. Besides “Wei Shu” commentator Pei Songzhi of Song dynasty said “Previous day of the funeral relatives and friends of deceased one sit on the ground and sing and dance when getting dog and horse and give some meat for the dog. Two men say the requiem for good way to Qishan mountain without obstacle and horrific souls. After that kill the dog and horse and burn up all belongings and clothes of deceased one”. These sources about Wuhuan funeral custom confirmed by the archaeological data too. In some Xianbei tombs with horses discovered near the Dalai Nuur in Chivchin Barga banner of Inner Mongolia found special antechamber with dog, ox and horse skulls found out the north part of the pit. Furthermore, the horse, ox, and dog bones found in the Xianbei tombs belonging to the 2nd century, in Beipiao, Northern China. Also, the big and black dog depicted on the inner wall of the tomb of Northern Yan prince Fen Sufu and his mistress, along with two dogs’ bones found under the press stone and one of dogs` shout was focused to the west. Some Xiongnu tombs contains the dog funeral. Dog bones have been found from Xiongnu tombs in Transbaikalia. For example, 9 dog funeral found in Ilmovaya Pad’ site, and 1 in Derestuy site. In some case dogs were buried on the coffin in stone chest, in some case were buried between the burials. In Mongolian Xiongnu funeral monuments more than 10 dog burials were unearthed. Some of them were presented by few bones, but some of them were complete skeletons of dog. Some of them have had their special stone chambers within the funeral pit, but most of them found in different parts of the pit in dispersed way. Xiongnu were used the dog for the funeral unconcernedly for their sex and age. But it is unclear what funeral principles required to accompany a dog with his master. In most circumstance, the dog head focuses to the same toward with deceased master by left side, in the middle part of the burial or in the separate stone chest. However dog is no economic importance as the domestic animal, but has many important roles as the housekeeping. Mongolian archaeologist Ts.Dorjsuren wrote that “Dog burial is related to the belief or worshipping, but not for food requisite”. May possibly to originate dog burial with its master among various nations caused for dogs` placing in
family and living closely with human. Absolutely, dog must protect its master and cattle in the next life and a guide to the heaven. In other word, there is a myth analyzes that the totemic animal which knows ancestors place usually represents ancestors’ image. In fact that dog burials tell us Wuhuan and Xiongnu had dog worshipping and had a dog totem. That worshipping custom must connect to the wolf worshipping of Medieval Mongols by some scholars point of view. (Ts.T.)
DONGHU tribes. The confederacy of the Mongolian speaking tribes. The name is composed of two Chinese words meaning “Dong-East” and “Hu-Barbar”. Generally “Hu” mean nomads. Donghu people inhabited in the Eastern part of Inner Mongolia and South-western part of Manchuria. First it was recorded in history in the 7th century BCE. It was a powerful confederacy of tribes at the end of the 3rd century BCE. Donghu was defeated by Maodun Chanyu of Xiongnu and was split into two groups Xianbei and Wuhuan. Archaeological relics related to Donghu and Xianbei were found and studied in the east area of Inner Mongolia and on the territory of Liaoning, Jilin counties. As for origins, it is likely related to upper class culture of Xiajiadian. However, classic Xianbei tombs are almost similar to Xiongnu tombs in terms of structure, ritual, artifacts and materials. It is possible to assume that Xiongnu and Donghu nations have the same origins, the same life style and religion. In accordance with the study of Mongolian scholar G. Sukhbaatar, Xiongnu and Xianbei were related to each other in hierarchy and had the same language and culture. (Ts.T.)
DORJSUREN, Tsogdanzan (1926-1994). Mongolian archaeologist. Born in the family of a herdsman in Tosontsengel soum of Zavkhan aimag in 1923. In 1942, he graduated from the School of the Regiment with medal of the Ministry of domestic affairs by specialization “Japanese studies”. In 19421945, he was appointed to the position of a secret agent to Japan occupied Inner Mongolia. In 1945, he was admitted to the National University of Mongolia by specialization “Mongolian



D
68
language and literature studies”. In 1947, he was admitted to the Moscow State University, majoring in history and graduated from it by specialization “Archaeologist” in 1952. He is one of the first national archaeologists of Mongolia. While he was studying in the Moscow State University, he took a correspondence course in Mongolian language and literature of the National University of Mongolia and graduated from it by specialization “Mongolian language-literature teacher”. Ts.Dorjsuren was a well educated researcher, with good knowledge of Japanese and Russian languages well and could use scientific and scholarly works in French, English and Chinese. Being one of Mongolian archaeologists who studied and acquired specialization in the Soviet Union he studied relics and artifacts related to the all periods of Mongolian archaeology and focused on making records and definition of unearthed and revealed materials and artifacts
and publishing articles about them. The relics and artifacts discovered and studied by him during the archaeological excavations conducted on the territory of Central and Western regions of Mongolia included several important discoveries. Main field of his study was archaeological studies related to Xiongnu. He excavated and studied the elite tombs at Noyon Uul in Central Mongolia, tombs at Belkhiin Am near Ulaanbaatar and at Gol mod and Naimaa tolgoi in Arkhangai aimag, at Khaalgatyn Zavsar of Mankhan soum of Khovd aimag related to Xiongnu. The results of his studies were published accordingly. In 1961, he wrote the scientific work “Northern Xiongnu” combining results of his studies and published it. The work has been considered as the first important scientific work written by Mongolian researchers on Xiongnu history and culture. Ts. Dorjsuren compared the artifacts found during the archaeological studies at the burial



D
69
grounds and settlements of Xiongnu with historical sources and materials and studied the economic and social issues of northern Xiongnu as a whole. Based on the results of his research, he scientifically proved that Xiongnu people were the first state to found their own State among other Central Asian nomads. Within a relatively short period of time of his scientific work, he did research on different periods of Mongolian history. Later, in 1990, his work on Xiongnu “Northern Xiongnu” translated into Japanese and published in series. (Ch.A.)
DOUXIAN Mountain. When the military forces of Han Empire attacked the Xiongnu Chanyu in 90 BCE, the Left Tuqi wang crossed over the river Yu-wu with his people and arrived in the mountain Douxian. Other information has not been found written in historical sources and works. (B.B.)
DUAN clan. Tribe of Mongolian speaking people of Xianbei origine. It is unknown why they adopted the Chinese style clan name Duan. Jin state granted Duan Wuwucheng, the leader of Duan tribe the title “Marquise of Liaoxi” in 303. Although Duan tribe with its center in Shandong was defeated by Murun Yan state and Duan people assimilated into it, this tribe had been powerful and influential until the beginning of the 5th century. Many ministries and generals of Yan dynasty were of Duan origins. (Ts.T.)
DUND SHANDYN AM tombs. The rocky mountain consisted of granite, surrounded by plain steppes; located 37 km Northwest from the Delgertsogt soum of Dundgobi is called Baga Gazariin Chuluu. There discovered number of archaeological monuments such as burial grounds, khereksur, and rock paintings. The wide valley behind the mountain is called Olon
Dund shandyn am site



D
70
Ovoot. The small valley located in the middle of it is called Dund Shand by local people. There are 64 Xiongnu tombs registered and located from the right to the east on the right meadow of the valley Dund Shand. These tombs were buried in groups. Round-shaped tombstones are put on the surface. The diameter is 3-8 m. The Xiongnu cemetery of Dund Shand valley was revealed by the Xiongnu relic expedition in 1989. During this excavation, 1 tomb was excavated and D.Tseveendorj who later published the results in an article entitled “The Xiongnu tomb of Baga Gazriin Chuluu”. (G.E.)
DURENY settlement. This settlement stretches 5kms along the Chikoi river bank on the territory of Republic of Buriatia, Russian Federation. This was a big settlement without rampart and citadel. Several half-earthen dwellings and thousands of artifacts related to Xiongnu were found during the small scale excavation in this settlement. (Ts.T.)
DURAAL tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. The West and SouthWest area of the mountain Baga Gazariin Chuluu located on the territory of Delgertsogt soum, Dundgovi aimag is called Duraal. There are 2 small cemeteries related to Xiongnu. It is 1 km from each other 8 tombs were registered in the cemetery located in the west of the mountain. Mongolian-American joint archaeological expedition revealed this tombs during its excavation in 2003. The archaeologists excavated 1 tomb from each cemetery in 2003. (G.E.)
DUULGA UUL tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery of common people in Mongolia. The small mountain named Duulga Uul consisted of dark rock and with dome shaped top, located 27 km south east from the center of Jargaltkhaan soum of Khentii aimag. There is a big cemetery with around 130 Xiongnu tombs located on a plain part in front of this mountain heading from the south west to the north east. There are 15 stone fences for offerings related to the Turkic period among the tombs. Out of them, 8 are placed in series in the North East heading from the South west to the north east. Later, 10 Xiongnu tombs that had been at the beginning of the short valley of the mountain, in front of the Xiongnu cemetery were revealed and recorded. The tombs were divided into several groups. The biggest tomb’s diameter is 15 m. The smallest tomb’s diameter is 4-5 m. Most tombs are middle-sized which had the diameter of 6-7 m.
Duulga Uul site
Dureny settlement



D
71
The Duulga Uul tombs was first discovered by the Gurvan Gol expedition of the Institute of History of the Mongolian Academy of Science in
1968. 5 tombs were excavated in the following year. After that, Mongolian-Hungarian joint expedition excavated 5 tombs (out of them, 4 were Xiongnu
Duulga Uul site
Plan of the Duulga Uul site



D
72
tombs and 1 was erection of offerings related to Turkic periods) in 1971. The expedition to study Xiongnu relics excavated 5 tombs in 1988. (G.E.)
DUURLIG NARS tombs. Xiongnu period elite and common people mixed cemetery in Mongolia. There are identified about 299 burials located in
the middle of the pine forest stretched over 3500 m south of Onon River, south of the center of BayanAdarga soum, Khentii aimag. The cemetery of Duurlig Nars was firstly discovered by Kh.Perlee and D.Tseveendorj in 1974. In 1991, researchers of the Mongolian-Japanese joint project Gurvan Gol, conducted the survey and made plan of the site.
Xiongnu elite tomb of Duurlig nars



D
73
Xiongnu elite tomb of Duurlig nars



D
74
Mongolian-Korean joint team of “Monsol” project working in this site since 2006 and excavated one elite tomb and two small burials. Tomb No.2 of Duurlig Nars delivered many valuable items such as cup for holding sacrificial grains, platter, pot, mirror made of bronze, lacquer vessel, bow case, jasper objects, ceramics, horse equipment, chariot, and sacrificed animal bones. The TLV shaped bronze mirror (1st century BCE – 1st century CE) was an important object to clarify the date of the burial. During excavation looters hole enforced by wood was found in Tomb No.2. In 2009, the team excavated 4 small burials and one elite tomb. (Ch.Ye.)
DYRESTYU CEMETERY. Monographe on Xiongnu tomb studies of Dyrestuy in Transbaikalia.
Author S.S.Minyaev. Published in St. Petersburg in 1998 at the volume No.3 of Xiongnu archaeology study series edited by the Institute of the Material Culture Study of the Russian Academy of Sciences. In this book the author presented and analyzed the materials from his own excavations held in Derestyu or Derestuyskiy Kultuk cemetery in the territory of Buriatia during 1984-1996. It contains Introduction, 4 Chapters, description of tombs, human and animal bones measurement charts and an English summary, in total of 113 page texts, drawings and sketches of 130 tombs. Main topics covered in this books are Xiongnu tomb structure and artifacts, cemetery organization and structure, major issues of the Xiongnu archaeology studies. (G.E.)



E
75
E
ASTERN TURKIC KHANATE (603-630 CE). The history of the Eastern Turkic state began to be discussed after the Turkic state was split into two. At the time when Sui dynasty collapsed because of domestic riots in China, Turkic power getting more influent during the Xibi kagan rule. During his rule the Turkic khanate became a dominant power in whole East Asia. It is considered that Turkic military forces attacks played an important role in the Sui state collapse. When Tang state was founded, Turkic chefs supported it, but soon after that it began to feud with it. During the periods of Chulu kagan the successor of the Xibi and Hieli kagan, Tang state became more powerful on one hand and there was the outbreak of domestic riots and Tele tribes broke out in revolt in the back of Turks. All these resulted in the East Turkic empire’s collapse in 630. Hieli kagan was captured and taken prisoners in Tang state. Some of Turkic people fled in panic into the Great wall, some of them submitted to Tele tribe. Turkic people who were oppressed by Tang state
more than 50 years broke out in revolt in 680 and re-established its state. It is named in the history as Second Turkic Empire. (Ts.T.)
EDUI tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Buriatia. Located in the Bichur region, in 2 km from the village Edui. 45 burials were identified here: the biggest is 20 m in diameter, medium sized are 8-10 m and the smallest is 5 m in diameter. The cemetery in Edui was discovered by the Russian anthropologist Yu.D.Tal’ko-Gryntsevich, who excavated 2 tombs. In 1964 P.B.Konovalov excavated one tomb. At 0.2-0.5m from surface they found choarcal and cow bones. The tombs had a stone construction on the surface and the internal construction consisted of a wooden coffin, in some cases there were wooden coffin and internal chamber. The finds of pottery and other artifacts are now in the Regional Museum in Kyachta. (Ts.E.)
EEZGIITIIN KHUJIRT tombs. Xiongnu period burial site. The cemetery located on the west side of the Khudgiin Tolgoi Xiongnu period burial site, about 35 km northwest of Battsengel soum, Arkhangai aimag. Actual site is on the south slope of a small hill where there is a small spring trickling among young forest. Burials at this site are appearing in groups and most of burials are considerably small by their size; there are 72 burials in total. Average diameter of burials is
Plan of the Eezgytyn khujirt site



E
76
about 5-7 meters. Eezgiitiin Khujirt cemetery has been discovered by Bronze and Early Iron age study team of the Mongol-Soviet Joint Historical and Cultural Expedition in 1981. The same year the survey team excavated and documented 4 (actually 6 burials has excavated and 2 of them were empty without any findings) burials. Later Mongolian archaeologists made complete mapping of the burial site in 1987. Burials excavated in 1981 were located west and south west side of the site. Excavation report says that all excavated burials were looted. However, position of the human skeleton indicates the burials were positioned with their head to south east. (G.E.)
“ENCYCLOPEDIA OF THE XIONGNU”. Author, Lin Gan. Huhhot, 1986. The monograph written in a systematic way on the Xiongnu history by using ancient historical sources. It predominantly gives detailed information about the Xiongnu origine, politics, economy, language, custom, anthropology, and relations with Han and the Xiongnu states. (Ch.S.)
ENGERIIN BUUTS tombs. Xiongnu period necropolis located in Engeriin buuts, the first plateau of the south slope of Delgerkhaan Mountain. in 17 km to south east from former Burentsogt cooperative, Tuvshinshiree soum, Sukhbaatar aimag. Some more archaeological monuments, such as a slab burials of Bronze Age, petroglyphs, burials with stone structure (date is not defined) and many more were discovered. There are 174 burials with stone structure visible on the surface, most of which are Xiongnu burials. Stone structure of these Xiongnu burials have unique form, there is no stone in the middle, and flat stones planted vertically in circle to make enclosure around this stoneless area in the center. “Dornod Mongol” Expedition of the Institute of History, Mongolian Academy of Sciences discovered this site and excavated one burial in 1991. Later some more burials were excavated by students from Anthropology-Archeology department of Mongolian National University. (G.E.)
Engeryn buuts site plan



E
77
ERDELYI, Istvan. Hungarian archeologist. Born in 1931 in Transylvania. Graduated in Museology from Lorand Ötvös University, Budapest in 1955. He did his Ph.D studies in Department of History, University of Leningrad in 1955-1959. He was awarded Ph.D. in History for his dissertation “Magyars in Levedia” in 1959 and became full professor for his work “The Avars and the Orient from the Perspective of the Archeological Sources” in 1976. He has worked as an archeologist in the Archeological Institute of the Academy of Sciences of Hungary since 1959. He worked as a head of Mongolian-Hungarian Archaeological joint expedition in 1961-1990, as a head of Soviet-Hungarian Archaeological joint expedition in 1975-1982. He awarded the scholarship of Arnold Stein in 1990 and was awarded Korosi Csoma prize in 1997. His work “The Art of the Avar” (1966), “The Peoples Living in the Carpathian Basin before the Hungarian Conquest” (1980), “Hungarian Conquest and the Events Leading Up to It” (1986), “Introduction to the Hungarian Prehistory” (1994), “From Baikal to Balaton: Archeological Data for the History of Turkish Language Peoples” (1997) are well-known. Since 1961 he has worked closely with Mongolian archaeologists, namely in Xiongnu studies. Together with archaeologists, D.Navaan and D.Tseveendorj excavated many Xiongnu burials of ordinary people in Naimaa Tolgoi, Duulga Uul and Tevsh Uul and published reports of their research.
In 2000 in his work “Archaeological Expeditions in Mongolia” he reported the main result of archaeological research on Bronze Age, Xiongnu and Turkic period. (D.Ts.)
EREGZEN, Gelegdorj. Mongolian archaeologist, Xiongnu scholar. Born in 1977. Working in the Department of Xiongnu studies of the Institute of Archaeology, MAS. Carries out research focusing on the studies of Xiongnu burials and tombs. Completed his Ph.D. work on “Studies of Mongolian Xiongnu burials” in 2009 in Seoul University, Korea. Apart from reports, catalogues of joint authoring, such as “Xiongnu burials of Morin Tolgoi”(2001), “Xiongnu burials of Hudgiin Tolgoi”(2003), “Xiongnu burials of Duurlig Nars”(2009), “Xiongnu burials and burial sites in Mongolian territory”(2010), he wrote more than 10 scientific articles on Xiongnu theme and his works were published in Korea, USA, Russia and China. He put presentations on 20 national and international conferences on Xiongnu studies (Ch.Е.)
“EXCAVATION OF BURIAL SITES OF XIONGNU CULTURE OF XICHAGOU”. Brief report published in Wenwu, 1960, No. 8. Author Song Shoudao. The article based on an excavation report of burial sites from Han dynasty, Xiongnu and Khidan period, located near Xichagou Village of Xifeng District, Liaoning province. In 1956 researchers from Northeastern Museum carried out excavation of an area of 8500 sq m, excavated 63 burials and documented 13850 artifacts from Xiongnu and Han dynasty period. (Ch.S.)



F
78



F
79
F
ANFUCHENG City. The name of this city means “City of Fan family wife”. After the death of Fan general of Han dynasty, his widow was ruled this city and named as Fan Fuzheng. Location is uncertain. When Er Xi general moved from Wuyuan, Xiongnu warriors held them near the Goushan mountain of Fu Yang in 90 BCE and fought. Er Xi general sent 2000 warriors from dependent states to Xiongnu to Fan Fuzheng city. After that Han soldiers attacked to the north and reached to the Zhiqiu river. (B.B.)
“FEATURE OF XIONGNU CULTURE IN THE PERIOD OF HAN”. A scientific paper published “Nei Menggu wenwu yu kaogu”, No.1, 1981. The author is Guo Suxin. He drew a conclusion that the culture of Xiongnu was originated from the culture of the Bronze Age like Ordos, on the basis of the comparative analysis on the graves dated to the Bronze Age in the periods of Spring-Autumn State, found in Ordos and the Warring State, Xiongnu graves found in Mongolia and Transbaikalia. (Ch.S.)
FELT CARPET OF THE NOYON UUL. In 1924 Mongolian-Tibet expedition leaded by P.K.Kozlov excavated in Noyon Uul site and found felt carpets from several elite tombs of Xiongnu. Since 2005 parts of felt carpets were found from several elite tombs and those are: excavation of the Tomb No.20 in Gol Mod site by Mongolian-French expedition, in 2006 also found from excavation of the Tomb No.20
in Noyon Uul site by Mongolian-Russian expedition and in 2007 it was found from the Tomb No.2 in Duurlig Nars site by Mongolian-Korean Mon-Sol expedition. Most of the felt carpets found from Xiongnu tombs are small parts and the conservation of them was relatively bad than felt carpets found from Noyon Uul, which were stored well and it was nearly complete.
Length of this felt carpet is 2.6 m, width is 1.95 m and it was made by well processed white felt. It was covered by fawn colored cotton and stitched by rope warn. In the centre of the carpet there’s rectangle part which has 3 lines of 8 patterns and in total 24 water patterns stitched by red yarn sewing.
Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Noyon Uul, Tomb 20
Noyon Uul, Tomb 20



F
80
Felt carpet, Noyon Uul



F
81
In the outside of water patterns there is 5 cm of frame and this frame is shaped red, green and yellow squares, circles and half circles. Outside of the rug is welted by fawn colored silk which has oval square patterns and the space between welt and inside frame was stitched by yellow sewing and patterns of 9 pair animals fighting and 9 trees was embroidered. From 9 animals 5 of them look like fighting animals similar to yak and tiger and 4 of them look like griffon attacking on the back of running elk. These mythic patterns are famous in nomadic art and it is one of the important example of Xiongnu animal style art development. However felt carpet found from the Tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul was complete it was divided into two parts later according to the contract of leaving some findings in Mongolia by Russian explorers from P.K.Kozlov expedition. So today one part is stored in Mongolian National museum and another part is in Hermitage Museum of Russia. One part kept in Mongolia was recognized as “National Heritage of History and Culture” by Mongolian government. (G.E.)
FICO SHAPED STONE NECKLACE. Discovered from the tomb No.1 of Ikheriin Am site in Baga Gazryn Chuluu mountain, Adaatsag soum of Dundgobi aimag. The exact date of the tomb was 140-380 CE by radiocarbon dating. Length of the arm is 18.7 mm, thickness is 7.9 mm, lower part diameter is 6.9 mm, weight is 1.3 gr. Most earliest fico shaped necklace found from Egypt. German archaeologist F.Petri found a necklace similar of above from the ruin of Naucratis, one of ancient town of Egypt during the excavation in the second half of 19th century. The chronology of that finding could be around 4th c. BCE. The artists of Naucratis initiated of fabrication of faience from 6-5th c. BCE, which was interrupted due to Persian invasion in the 4th c. BCE, and revived only in the 3er c. BCE. The form, design of early and later periods of Naucratis faience were quite different and easy to distinguish their chronological relevance. The faience of Ikheriin Am site is more similar to 1st c. BCE1st c. CE period Egyptian production. This design of fico shaped necklace were also used in ancient Rome, Tup-Khon of Southern Tajikstan, Black Sea, Spain and France. (Ch.A.)
FINE ART. Though the Xiongnu fine art is based on the animal style art, which is the main type of fine art of the steppe of that time, it is distinguishes with its unique style and features. A felt rug that have been recovered from the elite tomb of the Xiongnu at Noyon Uul is one the great examples of that art style. Many kinds of patterns, animals, beasts and winged creatures are skillfully depicted on that felt rug. Also, images of horse, yak, cattle and deer are masterfully embossed, casted, or carved on thin gold, silver and bronze that have been found in tombs excavated so far. According to these materials we can see that the fine art of the Xiongnu had been highly developed. (D.Ts.)
FISHING. The fishing is considered as a part of Xiongnu food subsistence. Particularly, findings from Ivolga settlement clearly indicate it, where thick stratum of fish bones and spine was unearthed around the fireplace of the dwellings. Bone of taimen, lenok, bream, grayling and pike fish were found from the river Selenge which flows along the village. Bones of omul, sturgeon were found from the remaining of the village. It is impossible to determine what kinds of fishing utensils people from the Ivolga village used when fishing due to the fact that most of fishing utensils can not be retained under the soil or on the ground surface. No matter what the situation was, they used fishing hook, knitted net and a spear which were found in the settlement. Although a sinker made of clay was found left in the fishing knitted net, a piece of string found from the village indicates that they were able to plait (to knit) a net. Several types of fishing hooks were found. They include big iron hooks, more complex structured, smaller in size hooks made of bone and iron. In addition, a spear made of bone with 3 teeth in both sides was found. Despite the fact that a real boat was not found from the Ivolga settlement, the slotting deck indicate the ability to process wood, as an indirect proof of the presence of boats in Ivolga. As written in Hou Han Shu, “Northern barbars sent 2000 soldiers to Shoufan area as spies and they were on the boat made of horse skin”. This record also demonstrates that they had acquired the knowledge and skills of making boat. (Ts.T.)
FOUR HORNED WANGS. Xiongnu high elite and close relatives of Chanyu by its status ranking just under the authority of Chanyu were named Horned wang or princes. Namely East and West wing Tuqi wang, East and West wing Guli wang were considered as most venerable aristocrats. Only Chanyu had the rights to appoint and dethrone a



F
82
Horned wang. In extreme rare cases Chanyu had implemented capital inculpation against a Horned wang in matter of the State treachery. Horned wang had the rights to exercise the justice keeping in his domain and obliged to inform orally to the Chanyu about only larger deals. (No.B.)
FRONTIER FORTIFICATIONS. There are some names of cities and settlements of Xiongnu in Chinese sources. Zhao Xin cheng and Fanfuzhen cheng cities were marked in historical sources. On the basis of the comparative research of historic geography and archeological findings, Zhao Xin cheng city is proved to be the ruin in Bayanbulag by Mongolian archeologists, but no facts or archeological findings about Fanfuzhen cheng city have been found yet. Recently, Mongolian archeologists have discovered a city ruin called Mangasiin Khuree which is located not too far from Bayanbulag, and has delivered findings similar to one of the Bayanbulag in form and structure, in Tsogttsetsii soum, Umnugobi aimag. It will be an interesting clue for the studies. From the both monuments, some Chinese style weapons and objects have been found but Xiongnu style objects are also present. So from this, it is concluded that they may have been a city or settlement, built for Chinese soldiers who joined Xiongnu and these wall fortified port has the aim to protect Xiongnu Southern border. So far today, the ruin of Bayanbulag has inevitably been confirmed to be a big settlement of Xiongnu period. There was found shards of several kinds of pottery, many kinds of bronze arrow head with iron pivot, metal rings, wushu coins, ankle bones, bronze mirror and bronze or metal objects with unknown purpose. (Ts.T.)
FUNERAL ACCOMPANIED OBJECTS. Funeral objects in Xiongnu tombs are the not ordinary items of everyday life, these are the symbolic and religious itens for the deceased one. This is the complex understanding of cultural level, worshipping, economic character and environmental situation of Xiongnu, however these artifacts cannot represent the everyday usage objects. At the chosen site, we can see the social position, ethnic origin, and wealth of deceased person. Artifacts of common burials of Xiongnu divided into 5 classes, 5 types and 25 items. Weapons. Divided into 3 types as: 1.1. Long distance attacking weapon, 1.2. Short distance attacking weapon, 1.3. Defence weapon. Long distance attacking weapons divided into 2 items: a) Arch, b) Arrowhead. a) Xiongnu arch made by composite design of bone gluing parts. Wooden parts of the arch
decomposed in the tomb and usually only bone and horn gluing parts remained. Bone gluing part of the arch is the one of the main artifacts of Xiongnu tomb. Usually it accomapnied for the man, in few cases for women and children. b) arrowheads made by iron, bone and bronze and specific for the design. Usually, 1-10 arrowheads accompanied in commoner burials of Xiongnu. Arrowheads are the massive artifacts of the Xiongnu tomb. Short distance attacking weapon. Only the sword includes in this classification discovered in the tomb No.1 of Baruun Khairkhan site. We have also some information about sword artifact in Transbaikalia and Khovd aimag. Sword is barely used for the funeral and it may be related to its value.
Defence weapon. Only plate-armour includes in this classification. Discovered 7 plates of the bronze plate-armour from the tomb No32 of Gol Mod site. Horse equipment. Divided into 2 types as 2.1. Bridle, 2.2. Saddle. Bridle. Divided into 5 items as a). bit, b) quarter, c) knoll, d) bridle girdle, e). bell. As for the cattle-breeding nation, horse played important role in Xiongnu life. Xiongnu horse equipments were specific and developed than other nations of that time. For the moment the only remain of the saddle found from tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul. We could suggest that in that time horse equipment as we know today was almost find their classical forms. a). However the bridle-bit made in general by iron, but there discovered bone bit from the tomb No19 of Burkhan Tolgoy site. In some cases, 7 burials without horse bones and uncertain 4 burials contained bridle bits. b). The quarter of Xiongnu made by iron or horn. Iron quarter is long and have double holes, but horn quarter also have double holes and beak end like a fang. The quarter usually found with the bit, and discovered from 11 burials (6%). 8 of these quarters made by horn (3 of them couple)
Duurlig nars, Tomb 2



F
83
and 3 are made by iron. For example, 3 layers of 6 gold plated bronze quarters found from tomb No10 of Burkhan Tolgoi site, and this kind of bronze quarter wide spread in China during Han dynasty. However, there was no bit and quarter in this tomb, but found with bronze bell. c). There found numerous and various sized bridle girdles from the sacrifice part of the tomb. However we cannot distinguish the practice of these various sized girdles, but it is clear for the bridle straps` allocation. d). We have found 2 bronze bells made by Han style used for horse adornment from the tomb No.3 of Khudgiin Tolgoi site, and tomb No.10 of Burkhan Tolgoi site. Saddle. a). Saddle. However no saddle found from common burials of Xiongnu, but the saddle discovered from the tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul site confirms that Xiongnu were used wooden design of saddle. b). Bellybend buckle. There found various configuration of iron and bone bellybend buckles from Xiongnu burials.
Clothes and adornments. This class divided into 3 types as a). Belt, b). Adornments, c). Shoes. a). Xiongnu were carried out important contribution on the nomads belt wearing practices. Their metal equipped belts were superior in design and composition than Bronze Age simple belts consisted of buckle and knobbles. This tradition had been developed until the Medieval Age. b).
Xiongnu adornments include various shape of glass, stone, amber beads, earring and ring, which found commonly from Xiongnu burials. According to Ts.Turbats` study, belt adornments found in 31 times (18% of all tombs), other adornments found in 29 times (17%) from Xiongnu commoner tombs. If we consider in mind that the tomb looters had special interests for that precious object and almost totality of Xiongnu tombs were looted in ancient times, we could made conclusion that nearly all deceased were equipped with belts. Xiongnu were used metal equipped belt and its adornments for the funeral, in some cased for women, but usually concerned for men. Xiongnu belt was the special object of religious and custom meaning and it was one of the most differentiating item of Xiongnu nationality, c). The only example of the shoes found from the tomb of Noyon Uul. But very rare data of shoes found from the common burial and D.Tseveendorj determined the bronze knoll and shout adornments of the Dund Shandyn Am tombs are the adornment of shoes of Xiongnu.
Food utensils. Food utensils divided into 7 types as a). Earthenware, b). Metal pot, c). Wooden utensil, d). Birch-bark utensil, e). Lacquer utensil, f). Chopsticks, g). Knife. a). Earthenware is the common artifact of Xiongnu burial. Xiongnu were used widely for the funeral custom with no differentiation of age or sex, after the analyses of 41 earthenware data of Burkhan Tolgoi site. b). Metallic pots divided into bronze and iron pot. The bronze pot found from Xiongnu burial on sacrificial part of the funeral with animal bones. In some cases there were found animal bones in the pot. Iron pot only found from tomb No73 of Burkhan Tolgoi site. This iron pot has vertical handles and bowl type bottom, and no different in design from similar bronze pots. c). Only few pieces of wooden
d dbi l l
Iron bit and cheek piece, Noyon Uul, Tomb 9
Silver and iron spoon, iron filter, bone chopsticks of the Xiongnu elite tombs
Golden part of a belt, Duurlig nars



F
84
utensil were found in graves due to perishable character of this item under the ground, d). Birchbark utensils used commonly in everyday life of Xiongnu. Especially, much of these objects found in the tombs from Khangai region, where birch trees are abundant in the nature. Bark utensils used for containing diary product or keep woman cosmetic items and mostly accompanied to women in the graves, e). Lacquer utensils might be brought from China and also habitually followed to women in the graves, f). Chopsticks have found many times in Xiongnu burials. Most of them found in pair and straight form. Meantime there are also 2 different types of bone sticks called as “spoon headed” and “pipe headed”. Probably they were kind of hair sticks in Chinese manner. Chopsticks found in all
35 Xiongnu tombs and 6 “spoon headed” chopsticks have found from 6 tombs. g). Several types of iron knives found in Xiongnu tombs. In total 26 iron knifes found in Xiongnu commoner burials, but not well preserved all.
Items of religious or ritual purpose. These kind of special objects divided into 3 types as: a) Bronze mirror, b) “Sun and Moon”, c) Flower shaped knoll. These objects are not household using, these tools have religious content and they
Bronze mirror and wooden comb, Duurlig nars



F
85
have significant importance for the study of funeral custom. Only the bronze mirror might been used also for everyday life. The other two item have been made especially for the tomb. a) By the concept of next life and funeral custom for Xiongnu, the broken bronze mirror put in the burial. More than 30 bronze mirrors found in commoner tombs. Mainly in women graves we found this item. b). The object called “Sun and Moon” is the ritual object usually found on the head interior wall of the coffin made of sheet gold or bronze, iron or even in birch-bark. The form of sun and moon played ethno distinctive symbol of Xiongnu nation. Also it played important role in religious belief of Xiongnu. A number of iron made “Sun and Moon” have found from Burkhan Tolgoi site, and take interests of archaeologists. c). The flower shaped iron knoll nailed up on the coffin outside wall to fix coffin textile or silk cover presented also as an adornment. This may present the symbol of the four cardinal directions. Common objects discovered from the tombs as follows:
Types of tools For men
For women
For children
Bow 77% 17% 6% Arrow 87% 10% 3% Bit and bit quarter 73% 27% Belt 74% 16% 10% Adornments 47% 53% Lacquer utensil 18% 82% Bone chopstick 43% 50% 7% Birch-bark utensil 13% 87% Knife 45% 45% 10% Bronze mirror 22% 67% 11%
By above showed table some objects as the bow, arrow, bit and belt were accompanied for men, lacquer or birch-bark utensil and bronze mirror were accompanied for women, and adornment, chopsticks and knives were accompanied for both sex with no difference of age. (Ts.T.)
FUNERAL MEAL. We have clear indices of funeral meals accompanied in the tomb since Bronze Age period monuments. It confirmed by the recent excavation that the tradition of offering funeral meals of Pazyryk, and then Xiongnu elite and commoner burials, Turkic, Uighur and Medieval graves was inherited and renewed in some manner.
Noyon Uul, Tomb
Bronze mirror, Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu, Tomb 100



F
86
Horse, ox, sheep and goat head, neck, rib, radius, legs and pastern bones found widely in Xiongnu tombs. In most cases there is a special room for the offering with animal meats at the top side of coffin. For example, in the tomb No.11 of Burkhan Tolgoi, there were found rump, back, rib and radius bones put on the right and left wall of top side of burial, and chopsticks were put on these bones. In same time, horse were sacrificed with the bit in the mouth also found in special niche on the head side of pit. It seems to have two different part in the Xiongnu burials, such as offering meals or animal bones and sacrificial animals for after life presented by some distinct bones presenting complete animal. However it is sometimes different to distinguish between funeral meal and sacrificing animals, funeral cups, chopsticks, and pots followed to the funeral meal. Sometimes non domestic animals were sacrificed for the funeral. In that cases domestic animals for funeral meal and non domestic animals as wild horse, antelope, deer and various birds were sacrified for after life and in some cases the dog used for this purpose as well. (Ts.T.; No.B.)
FUNERAL PRACTICES OF CIRCULAR GRAVES OF THE XIONGNU. The Xiongnu circular graves were predominantly grouped and average of 100 grouped graves made up a cemetery. Majority of the Xiongnu cemetery are distributed in Mongolia and in the territory of Buriatia, Russian Federation. Relatively few cemeteries
were registered in Inner Mongolia and neighboring provinces of PRC. The Xiongnu cemeteries of Transbaikalia were located in a big triangle by Selenge river, Dzida river, Chikoi and Hiloka rivers. Over 30 Xiongnu cemeteries were registered here. Among them Ivolga site is located on the northern limit, Sharagol cemetery is in the eastern limit, Sosnovka is in the western limit and Bayankhar is in the eastern limit. The Xiongnu cemeteries discovered across Mongolia counted more than 100. These are distributed in major regions of Altai, Khangai, Gobi and Steppes. These cemeteries have 5 following geographical characteristics: - Rich pastureland where people are still existent; - Big and small river banks; - Elevated locations (mountain peak, steppe); - Mountain with rocks situated nearby; - Sandy or muddy territories. The Xiongnu grave could be divided into 2 types; with external construction and without it. Although graves with external construction make up most of it, graves without any stone mounds are encountered. Most of the Xiongnu circular graves are with surface stone mound. The shapes of the external structures were divided into 2 types: 1) with square mound pointing to the cardinal directions with its corners (56% of the graves) and 2) with multi-angular stone mound (44%).



F
87
In terms of external construction of the grave, an extraordinary finding that should be emphasized is the square fence in the center of the mound observed from the grave No.38 and 38a of Burkhan Tolgoi. This twin grave had common stone mound aligned and a depression with few stones on the center of the cover makes it look like one grave. However, as to look at it deeply, aligned two grave pits were found with 2 separate fences having excavated. The grave is concerned one of the most unique graves as it had twin funerals under unique surface cover and direction of the grave was pointed toward the east.
