as the settlements of Bayan-Undur and Dureny in Buriatia and monuments of Nijnii Mangirtui have been researched. The cities and settlements of Xiongnu in Mongolia have been discovered and researched since 1920’s. In 1925, B.Ya.Vladimirtsov and a Buriat scholar Bazar Baradin explored near the Kherlen and Tuul Rivers, explored the ruin of an ancient city in a place named Tereljiin Durvuljin, on the east bank of the Terelj River, a west outfall of the Kherlen River and handed over the findings to the National Museum. They could not define the date of the ruin but proposed it would be a military fortification dated to 13th century. In 1949, S.V.Kiselev reviewed the construction materials found from the Tereljiin Durvuljin, found by B.Y.Vladimrtsov in 1925, and estimated it was dated back to the Xiongnu period. In 1952-1957, one of the Mongolian first archeologists, Kh.Perlee discovered and excavated some ruins of cities including Gua Dov, Undur Dov, Shuvuutain Gol, Baruun Duruu, and the ruin of Bayanbulag. In 1966, Ts.Dorjsuren discovered the Boroo settlement, in Mandal sun, Selenge aimag and defined its date to the Xiongnu period on the basis of the surface collected findings. In 1967, D.Navaan excavated the ruin of Bayanbulag in Nomgon soum, Umnugobi aimag and found some interesting findings. In 1990, the expedition for Xiongnu monuments, of the Institute of History, Mongolian Academy of Sciences led by D.Tseveendorj and I.Erdelyi excavated the ruin of Bayanbulag and found many interesting findings like different kinds of iron arrow head, some kinds of pottery and wushu coins. In the same year, the expedition excavated small parts of the settlement of Boroo, found some remnants of dwellings, ceramic sherds and a tool made of sheep’s shoulderblade and proved that this settlement was likely to be a big center of manufacturing and handycrafts of Xiongnu period. Approximately 20 ruins of Xiongnu settlements or cities have been discovered in Mongolia and Inner Mongolia and researched. In order to determine the function and significance of the monuments, we need to make a classification. They could be classificied into following: 1. Temporary fortifications (used for military and administration); 2. Religious centers; 3. Frontier fortifications; 4. Settlement for craftsmen and agriculture. Of them, the fourth one that is to say the settlement for craftsmen and argiculture keeps plentiful materials to study the handycraft, social
system and culture of Xiongnu. Only monument of this type in Mongolia is the settlement of Boroo. (Ts.T.)
SHANG SUNJIAZHAI VILLAGE tombs. Located in Shang Sunjiazhai village, Datong county, Qinghai province, China. Total of 182 burials were excavated from 1973-1981, but 12 burials forming a distant group of others in B section of the cemetery could be belong to Xiongnu people. Some interesting artifacts such as a stamp of Xiongnu noblemen (from Tomb No.1) submitted to Han, pottery with wavy pattern, wushu coin, bronze mirror, it could be dated to the Xiongnu of 2nd c. CE. Most of these burials have entrance facing to east, brick under soil tomb with spherical dome. Burials have had earthen mound visible on ground surface. But there are few burials with entrance facing to east and west. In most of the burials there is one person buried. (Ch.S.)
SHELUN (402-410 CE). Rouran Emperor or Khan, also known as Jarun. When his father Wengedi (in some records, it says he was son of Hedohan) died, he got support from his uncle Pihouba, but broke his trust and attacked him when he was off guard and became the khan. Since then Rouran pastured north of Gobi desert, nowadays Mongolian territory. They conquered remaining tribes of Xiongnu near Orkhon River and became more powerful. Also Rouran brought under their command small and big neighboring states that was their flourishing period. He made himself Shelun khan with “Hutagtai” title. Then he enacted laws and organized his army in groups of ten. He was defeated by great army Tabgachi and was killed in 410. (Ts.Т.)
SHI LE (274-333 CE). South Xiongnu political and military figure and founder of Xiongnu Zhao dynasty. Jiehu tribesman. Was sold as a slave in his childhood. During this youth he emerged as a military general of Han-Zhao state, South Xiongnu. He made the land conquered with help of his army for Xiongnu-Han Empire under his governance and became a powerful military lord. In 319 his Zhao dynasty separated from Xiongnu Han dynasty. In 329 he defeated Han-Zhao of Xiongnu and afterwards conquered entire North West of China. He was a talented general but used to act extremely cruel after battle as noted in historical records.
(Ts.Т.)
SHIRT, PANTS, BOOTS. S.I.Rudenko assumed that Xiongnu did not wear shirt since no shirt was



S
163
found in the burials from Noyon Uul. Grey textile baggy pants were found from the Tomb No.6. The density of the textile is 14×22 fiber per sq cm. The length of pants is 114 cm; the waist is 116 cm wide. There is 0.4 cm thick woolen string runs around the waist. The waist is trimmed to create a waistband and the string runs through it to make it possible to be tied. There is woolen material sewn on the edge of the cuffs. On thighs of the pants there are three stripes created with different color thread. Another pant which was found in the same burial had the same shape, but made from thinner, silky material. Researchers conclude that this type of baggy pants were worn by Turks (Kazaks, Kyrgyz) and Mongols up to beginning of 20th century. One artifact that illustrates baggy pants of Xiongnu is a belt buckle found in one of the Xiongnu burials near Chinese Xi’an city at the tomb No.140 of Keshengzhunag. On the belt buckle there is an image of two Xiongnu men wrestling in forest. Men tied their horses with decorated saddle from a tree, had their long hair tied in loops behind ears and wrestling. Two wrestlers are topless, but have wide baggy pants, short boots. Chinese archeologists date this artifact back to Warring States period, i.e. 3rd century BCE. Until recently researchers were concluding that this image of topless wrestlers in baggy pants are similar to the traditional wrestler’s outfit of
ethnic groups of western Mongolia, such as Torgut, Zahchin, Oold, Uryiankhai, Bayat and Dorvod. Also silk booty with separate thigh parts, which as extremely wide on top and narrow down were found from the tomb No.6 of Noyon Uul. This thigh pants were attached to waist with a peg. It is 109 cm long up to the tip of the booty and 50 cm wide. Silk sole was made to wear inside felt booty. On top of felt booty, Xiongnu wore leather boots of the same shape made from leather and sewn with silk thread. The sole of boots is decorated with embroidery since Xiongnu would sit leg crossed and soles of boots were visible to others.
Pants, Noyon Uul, Tomb 6
Boots, Noyon Uul



S
164
The main part of silk boots was found in the Tomb No.12 of Noyon Uul. Original red color used to make the boot toe turned into yellow. There is horn pattern embroidery made with orange, burgundy, blue thread. Heel side was made with the same materials as toe, but inside there is leather to create heel grip as in modern shoes. Boot top is made from burgundy silk with cloud pattern. There are cows between clouds. Boot shape is very similar to the modern buriat boots. The toe, heel side and top had red silk lining. Researchers think that these boots found in the burials are for the nobles to wear during ceremonies and during everyday boots were of similar shape and made of leather. (U.E.)
SHIYIJIAN (320-376). Shiyijian was the last prince of the Tuoba Dai state and ruled from 338 to 376, when Dai was conquered by the Former Qin. He was the son of Tuoba Yulü and the younger brother of Tuoba Yihuai, whom he succeeded in 338. He was estimated as the honest and brave ruler, choosing righteous officials to high posts. In 340 he moved the capital to Shengle (near modern Horinger county of Hohhot, Inner Mongolia). When he was poisoned by enemies Tuoba people lost their state for a period and only his grandson Tuoba Gui would re-establish the Tuoba state and go on to found the Northern Wei Dynasty. (Ts.T.)
SHUNWEI. According to an ancient Chinese legend, last king of Xia state died in exile and his son Shunwei took his family, children, relatives and people and run towards north, then settled in actual Mongolian steppe, got used to nomadic husbandry and remained there, saying that he became an ancient ancestor of Xiongnu and Mongols. Shunwei and some of his people came to Mongolia and mixed among Xianyun, Xunyu and Guifang people, but position of few Chinese immigrated to warrior nomadic tribes was not powerful. Nomadic tribal states of Mongolia mentioned repeatedly in Chinese records had their own nobility and leaders and it is impossible for migrant from other countries to become their leader and even ancestors of rulers of Xiongnu. (Ts.Т.)
SHUVUUTAIN GOL Fortress. Xiongnu period fortified settlement in Mongolia. This fortress ruin is located in the valley of Orkhon river tributary - Shuvuutai River on the north coast, Buregkhangai soum, Bulgan aimag. Kh.Perlee made measurements and sketch plan in 1954. Fortress wall was built with rammed earth.
There is in the center of the fortress a remain of a mounded big construction. From this mound to the north gate identified paved ground
Plan of the Shuvuutain gol fortress
isthmus likely remains of some buildings. Size of fortress: the south side is 225 m, north side is 265 m, west side is 193 m, and east side is 187 m long. The big mound located in the center is 30×40 m and ground isthmus is 8×19 m. There are four gate remains in four sides of the fortress. The structure and the internal organization of this fortress are signs like Tereljiin Durvuljin attributable to Xiongnu period. (Ts.Т.)
SILK ROAD. It is obvious that the nomadic empires and settled states of Eastern Asia had been fought regularly for all their history to control the main commercial route of historical times the Silk Road. The control of the Silk Road delivered not only trading profit, but also commercial tax and custom revenues. Despite of these economic profits the evident political and strategic were emphasized by the counterparts from the control of Silk road. This major trade road was important for Xiongnu and they were conducted wide range of military and diplomatic policy to control it. During the struggle between Han Wudi and Xiongnu at the end of 2nd c. BCE to the first quarter of 1st c. BCE, Han court paid special attention to the investigating Western region small states, and elaborated the strategy how to engage with these countries and force to Xiongnu from the west side. As a result of these diplomatic and military activities, the Silk Road was opened in his proper sense. General Chang Qian of Han Dynasty played an important role to this objective.



S
165
He discovered the route into Middle Asia with the purpose of investigating this region and find famous battle horses. While Han Dynasty broke down in 3rd c. CE, the role of Silk Road falling much, but revived quickly and became more intensively than before. There were 2 basic branches of the Silk Road. The north road started from Chang’an capital city of Han Dynasty, pass through Gangsu corridor to the northwest and divided into 3 branches. Two branches gone along with north and south edges of Taklimakan desert and meet near Kashgar, another branch was passed in the north of Tianshan mountain along with Hami and Turfan and reach in Fergana valley. These 3 branches were continued to the west by the north and south road and meet in Merv city. The south road was along with Karakorum range, passed through present Pakistan territory and reach to Iran and Iraq (see map). The one branch in the north of Tianshan mountain was important road for Xiongnu, because it was the source to find agricultural and handicraft products. Indeed, it was the only possible branch of Silk road to control it easily. Once Mongolian historian G.Sukhbaatar said: “One of the major foreign policy of Xiongnu was to keep their authority on Silk road and take taxes
and tributes from these western region countries”. The aim of this policy not only limited by the tribute, it was proposed take a shares from the Silk Road transportation incomes. It is common to consider that this northernmost branch of the road had lesser natural barrier than others, but Chinese tradesmen opened more side-way south two branches in order to be safe from nomads or Xiongnu influence. The key strategy of the Xiongnu policy for western region countries was to consent and control own authority, without changing local governors. Xiongnu were struggled since Maodun Chanyu with Yuezhi and achieved in success during Laoshang Chanyu period. During this period, Yuezhi pushed away to the south and their native land in actual Gansu, Qinghai, came into Xiongnu control. Moreover the major nomadic nations of Western region, as Kangju and Wusun were under the Xiongnu rule. According to historical sources, General Cheng Tang of Han Dynasty was said that “Western region countries indigenously ruled by Xiongnu”. After major competitor Yuezhi migrated to the far western lands, and most influential Kangju and Wusun followed Xiongnu, all small oasis cities or countries came into the Xiongnu control in 2nd c. BCE, and dominated until 1st c. CE. After that, interest and aggression of Han Dynasty to the
Map of Silk road



S
166
west continuously increased, and these 2 biggest nations struggle for this strategic important region continued for a long time. For example, in 151 BCE, Huyang wang Yiwu of Xiongnu attacked to Kumul, General Sima Maokai of Han Dynasty battled against him and defeated near Pulei lake. The western and southern goods entered through Silk Road transportation in Mongolia have been discovered in Xiongnu elite tombs. Moreover, rare goods from Egypt and GreekBactrian style have been still found from Xiongnu common burial were probably entered by Silk Road. (Ts.T.)
SIMA QIAN (145/135 BCE- 86 CE). Historian and astrologer of Chinese Han dynasty. Compiler of Chinese early history and called as the “Father of Chinese history”. Was born in Hancheng, near present day Shanghai. He employed his fathers’ collected data and wrote Shiji (Records of the Grand Historian), which contains historical periods from mythic Yellow Huangdi to Han Wudi Emperor. His son Yan Yun continued writing the Shiji, during the Xuandi Emperor reigning and finished it in 73 BCE. Later, Zhu Xiaosun, the historian of Eastern Han edited the Shiji and Pei Ying, the historian of Song period made an explanation. The 110th chapter “Account of Xiongnu” of Shiji is the first report about Xiongnu and contains major records for the study of Xiongnu history. (Ch.Ye.)
SIX HORNED WANGS. Xiongnu high elevated title name. The six wang or princes are occupying the secondary position only after the most prominent Four Horned wang, in Xiongnu hierarchy. These including Left and West Danghu wang, Left and West Douwei wang, Left and West Jichu wang. (No.B.)
SNAFFLE BIT. The snaffle and bit are a kind of findings, which are usually found from the Xiongnu graves and are several in their number and type. According to Z.Batsaikhan research, the Xiongnu snaffle is 19-24 cm in length and its shaft is 21 cm, those are made of a beaten iron with a large ring at each end. The shaft is straight but one half is longer and another is shorter. The ring of
the part, that is put in horse mouth, is little smaller and the ring, the bit is slotted into, is little bigger. The Xiongnu used horn and iron bit. Of them, the iron bit is more common and several types have been found. There are some bits, which can be mentioned, as example: one of them is two types of iron bits, found from the Xiongnu graves in Buriatia, those are curve like hook mark and have two sharp ends; another one is iron bits found from the Xiongnu graves in Mongolia, those are straight and have two ends like ax or have ball ends. The horn and iron bits, above mentioned, have a couple of hole to fasten the leather strap of bridle up the holes through the snaffle hook. It is good for controlling the horse not to get the snaffle away. In addition, his research tells that there were three types of bits in shapes: ring, cross and hook in ancient time and they have noticeably not been changed yet today. The Mongols have used several types of bits like ring, cross and bow for livestock’s behavior. For instance, the cross and bow bits are commonly used for fast horses or ones swerved, and trained to be ridden for catching others. (Ts.T.)
“SOCIALSTRUCTUREOFTRANSBAIKALIA XIONGNU” Authors N.N.Kradin, S.V.Danilov and P.B.Konovalov. Published in Vladivostok, 2004. The issues about social structure of Transbaikalian Xiongnu based on archaeological datas of Ivolga, Derestuy, Cheremukhovaya Pad’ and Ilmovaya Pad’ sites. Authors assuming that, however Transbaikalia was the periphery land of Xiongnu Empire, but it is possible to clarify social structure of Xiongnu using archaeological data from this region. Authors consider that “it was a classic example of nomadic empire and left deep path in the social structure of the Eurasian steppe societies”. (Na.B.)
SOLBI UUL tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Mongolia. Situated at the south slope of a high mountain, in 5 km from Battsengel soum of Arkhangai aimag. Total of 200 tombs located in 2 distinct groups in the right and left side of Solbi Uul mountain, concretely 141 tombs in the right side, 57 tombs in the left side. Moreover these 2 groups of tombs divided into several small parts. The external structure of tombs is rather circular stone ring with stone absent central depression. The bigger tomb diameter is 20-25 m and the smaller is 4-6 m, most of tombs have average diameter of 8-12 m. The site was discovered by Bronze and Iron Age Study team of MongolianSoviet Joint Expedition of History and Culture



S
167
in 1980 and excavated 3 tombs in that year. Later, 2 tombs excavated in 1987 by Xiongnu study team and 4 tombs excavated by researchers of the Department of Archaeology and Anthropology, National University of Mongolia in 2000. (G.E.)
“SOME ISSUES OF THE XIONGNU ARCHAEOLOGICAL RESEARCH”. Article published in Kaogu, 1990, Vol. 4. Author Unen-Yosotu (Wu En). It introduced over 930 graves and tombs of the Xiongnu period found in the territory of Transbaikalia, Mongolia and PRC, while, the author divided them into 8 types and stated about cultural similarity characteristics of the findings found from different graves which had different structure and organization. Also part of the Xiongnu ancestors is present nomads who recently moved to the north having lived in Ordos, Inner Mongolia, and the other part could be the owners of slab burials. Furthermore, he included information about crop farming of the Xiongnu who built settlements which was the basis for urbanization. (Ch.S.)
SOSNOVSKY, Georgy Petrovich (1899-1941). Studied at University of Commerce of Petrograd in 1909-1918, and worked as a researcher of the Archaeological section of Museum of Anthropology and Ethnography. In 1919-1920, worked as a research fellow of the Museum of Yenisei province (present day Krasnoyarsk) and since 1920 entered to the Department of History, Irkutsk University and after graduation became a Ph.D student in the Institute of Anthropology, Moscow University. In 1927-1941, worked as a research fellow in the Institute of Material Culture, and Institute of Material Culture History of State Academy and since 1934, simultaneously was worked in the Hermitage and Ethnographic Museums. In 1925-1929, he was headed the first fieldwork team of Buriat-Mongolian Archaeological Expedition, excavated in Ilmovaya Pad’ site and started the professional study for Lower Ivolga site. Received Ph.D in 1938, and his most
renowned works are: “Derestuy Cemetery”. (1935); “Early nomads of Transbaikalia” (1940). (M.Ts.)
SOUTHERN XIONGNU. Xiongnu were divided into two group during the fighting of Huhanye and Zhizhi Chanyus and re-unified in 54 BCE. Final separation was happened in 48 CE. Southern Xiongnu of Bi Chanyu capitulated to Han dynasty in 50 CE. They were divided into 8 border regiments and leaded by Chanyu. Northern Xiongnu state fallen down in 93 CE and Southern Xiongnu executed the foremost role to this with Han dynasty and Xianbei tribes. After that Southern Xiongnu begun to exist under the rule of Han dynasty in south of the Great Wall, present day Shanxi and Shaanxi provinces and Ordos region. They tied to Han politically and economically. But Southern Xiongnu had been competed for their independence and keeping the cultural diversity. For example, Southern Xiongnu revolted in 94 CE and joined with rebels during the Eastern Han falling. In 188 CE, Southern Chanyu killed by own warriors for helping to Han army. In 216 CE, Cao Cao divided Southern Xiongnu by 5 tribes in order to join with rebels and pledged the Chanyu. In 304 CE, Lui Yuan established the Han state of Southern Xiongnu. (Ts.T.)
SPEAR. A spear is definitely one the weapons of the Xiongnu weaponry set and it is mentioned in the historical sources. An iron spear head have been
Plan of the Solbi Uul site



S
168
recovered from the tomb No.29 of Chandmani Uul and from the tomb No.22 at Nukhtiin Am, Galt soum, Khuvsgul aimag, excavated by the team of Bronze and Early Iron Age studies of MSHCJE. Moreover iron spear heads have been unearthed from the tomb No.2 of Duulga Uul, Jargaltkhaan soum, Khentii aimag, and from tombs No.2 and 5 at Engeriin Buuts of Delgerkhaan Uul, Tuvshinshiree soum of Sukhbaatar aimag, and from the tomb No.20 at Gol Mod, Khairkhan soum, Arkhangai aimag and from the tomb No.1 at Uguumur Uul, Sant soum, Selege aimag. The iron spear heads that have been found so far are referred to the typology of iron weaponry and they are all have sockets for shafts. Also these spear heads can be divided into triangular (Chandmani, Nukhtiin Am, Engeriin Buuts, Baga Gazryn Chuluu), right-angled trapeze (Gol Mod, Khuren Khond), and oblong shaped (Tasheba River, Duulga Uul, Uguumur Uul). A long shaft is attached to the socket of the head. The Chinese designed a spear with a long shaft and with hook on one side, which purposed to thrust, slash and pull down from the horseback, against the Xiongnu cavalry. This caused the Xiongnu to use same weapon against their enemies. (N.E.)
STATE SEAL. There are some historical records about the existence of Xiongnu state seal. Han Xuandi Emperor was presented the golden seal to Huhanye Chanyu of Xiongnu in 51 BCE. Later Wang Mang tried to change this golden seal with the inscription of “State seal of Xiongnu Chanyu” to simple stamp. Chanyu of Northern Xiongnu had jade seals. Wei Dynasty founder Cao Cao presented the state seal to Huchuquan Chanyu of Southern Xiongnu in 220 BCE. In most of the Chinese historical sources Xiongnu were recorded as inferior nation, and these records concerning the seals of Xiongnu Chanyu has the goals to present dependent situation from Han. But we have no direct facts about state seals before Hunanye Chanyu, but by indirect fact as Zhonghan Yue eunuch, who submitted to Xiongnu was advised to Chanyu to show his greatness by the many sealed letter on the bigger board. Other Chanyus as Laoshang Chanyu letter contains overconfident and arrogant words are might be written by eunuchs` advice. Thus, there were several seals during Xiongnu period. (G.E.)
STIRRUP. Stirrup is considered as the third important discovery after the cart and saddle, among other horse accessories. Before the firearms were invented the stirrup played a very important role in the war affaires. Specially, during the cavalry attack,
it is obvious that a stirrup played an irreplaceably important role when cavalry attacked enemies by standing on saddle and wedging with a spear in their hands. In general history it is considered that an item consisted of skin of leather similar to a stirrup was first used in India in the 2nd c. BCE. It was also impossible to use a stirrup before the hard wooden saddle was invented. Without a stirrup, a horseman’s weight puts too much pressure on the back of the horse which causes ulcer. Nomads likely made and began to use a pair of stirrups in every sense of the word in 3-4th c. CE. The first stirrup to be dated certainly is from the earthen figure of a horseman found in the tomb of Changsha, Hunan province of China related to 302 CE. That a stirrup with Avar people of Rouran origine reached Europe in 6th c. CE has grounds. A stirrup was first mentioned in documentary and books in Europe in 580, when the emperor Maurius Tiberius of Bizantium included a stirrup in the list of military equipment and accessories to buy newly and use in the book of strategy written by him. Xiongnu culture of horse rising was highly developed. Their main military force was light cavalry. Thus, it is possible that Xiongnu knew and used stirrup. A part of the iron stirrup with 5 cms sole discovered by D.Navaan and I.Erdelyi from the Xiongnu tomb of the Duulga Uul in 1970 is the most attractive artifact. Whether it is a part of a real stirrup or a part of something else is a scholarly valuable issue. A part of the ironware similar to a handle of the stirrup unearthed from the Tomb No.2 of the Delgerkhaan mountain of Tuvshinshiree soum, Sukhbaatar province by Z.Batsaikhan is also finding of a great significance to make a close analysis. Enough evidences to determine the origins and dateof stirrup have not been found yet. (Ts.T.)
Iron stirrup



S
169
STONE HUMAN FIGURE. Found from the tomb No.1 of the Ikheriin Am, Baga Gazriin Chuluu, Delgertsogt soum of Dundgobi aimag. A naked man is placed on a oval platform, the back is straight, two stubby legs are bent in knees, two arms are on the knees in little bending. His sexual organ, breast and muscle of belly, arms and legs are artistictly depicted. There is a trace of a ring in the middle of his shoulder blades, but the head part above the shoulders was broken and not found. The height to the shoulder is 22.7 mm, broad of the shoulder is 15.6 mm, the base is 12.2×6.6×4.3mm and weight is 1.9 gram. This stone human figure is a portrait of Egyptian god Bes. This god was hanged down from bed, pillow, chair and mirror and drawn on some kinds of tools, wall
and pottery. This god is displayed as a long haired and lion muzzled head wearing a diadem, sometimes a tail was shown. The arms and legs are displayed similarly, but sometimes it is portrayed like raising hand with a weapon in it. The god Bes was one of the important gods at that time as the protector of children and mothers, symbol of giving birth and reproduction. (Ch.A.)
SUKHBAATAR, Gunjiin (1928-1992). Wellknown Mongolian historian. Specialst on ancient history of Mongolia. Was born in 1928 in Asgat soum of Sukhbaatar aimag. Graduated National University of Mongolia in 1952, and completed the 2 years language preparation course of Beijing University. He was one of the rare scholars who specialized in Mongolia ancient history. He used Chinese sources and Chinese scholars` view in Mongolian historical study. His major works are:
“Xianbei” (1972) and “Ancestors of the Mongols” (Social structure and economy of Xiongnu) (1980) and he wrote about 20 scientific articles on Xiongnu thematic. (Ch.Ye.)
SUL TOLGOI tombs. Xiongnu period tombs in Mongolia. Situated at the junction of Ikh Uul and Tosontsengel soums of Khuvsgul aimag, in 32 km from Ikh Uul soum, near the Selenge river. There are 3 Xiongnu tombs, which are circle shaped and with stone configuration and discovered by the Paleolithic study team of Mongolian-Russian Joint Expedition of History and Culture in 1982. 1 tomb was excavated in this site. (G.E.)
SUN AND MOON WORSHIP. Since Neolithic time to Bronze and Early Iron age ancient peoples believed in sun, moon and heaven and worshipped them. There are various images which are shaped men with sun and moon head on the Javkhlant Khairkhan petroglyphs and in some closely located sites as Dalain Duulga, Khanbogd soum of Omnogobi aimag. Also there spread widely figure of sun, moon and stars, what’s more deer, ox, horse, and moose with sun head or sun horns (circle with many rays), or sun imagined on the figure or end of the tail at the Tsagaan Salaa, Baga Oigur sites in Ulaankhus soum of Bayan-Ulgii aimag, and Shiveet Khairkhan site of Tsengel soum.
The sun and moon usually depicted at the top of deer stones of Mongolian and Central Asian region. There found a very extraordinary figure, a man who connecting with the sun, moon and the star at Lovongiin Nuruu, in Bayal-Uul soum of GobiAltai aimag. Archaeological and historical sources tell us about this tradition. For example, according to Shiji, “Xiongnu Chanyu pray for the sun in the morning, pray for the moon in the evening...when moving to attack observe sun and moon locations,
Stone human figure, Tomb No.1, Ikheriin Am, Baga gazryn chuluu site
Iron Sun and moon,



S
170
attacking when moon appearing and depart when moon disappearing”. The golden sun and moon images discovered from Gol Mod and Noyon Uul tombs, and iron sun and moon images found from Burkhan Tolgoi and Shombuuziin Belchir common burials. These images were all on the top of the coffin and it confirms that Xiongnu had a sun and moon worshipping. (D.Ts.)
SU WU (140-60 BCE). Statesman and diplomat of Chinese Han dynasty. Famous for faithful in Chinese history, and renowned by the policy for Xiongnu. In 100 BCE, Wudi Emperor appointed him as a second general of a palace safety and a officer of Xiongnu affairs. Wudi sent messengers headed by Su Wu to Xiongnu in 101 BCE and asked the peace treaty as the vassal state of Han dynasty. During his stay at the court Su Wu tried to organize a coup-d’etat but it was revealed and sentenced to hard punishment or to surrender to Xiongnu. Su Wu did not accepted the surrender and sent for exile far away to Baikal lake. However the state barely forgotten him for 19 years, but took back him by the especial contract. (Ch.Ye.)
SWORD. Although the Xiongnu preferred to avoid close-range fighting, they were equipped for it, as is shown by occasional archaeological finds of the appropriate weapon types. There are two swords
have been found in the territory of Mongolia. Sword-1: It is has been found in the tomb No.5 at Baruunkhairkhan Uul near Ulaanbaatar city. Length 67.7 cm, thickness 0.5 cm, length at handle 1.9 cm, thickness 1 cm, and weight 160 gr. It is double-edged and has a spiky and thin point. The sword was placed in a wooden sheath but due to lack of preservation the sheath was damaged very much. Even though the surface of the sword is corroded considerably it has been kept very good. This sword is being kept at the Institute of Archaeology, MAS. Sword-2: It has been found in a burial excavated in Khovd province. Length 69 cm, shoulder width 1.3 cm, grip length 16 cm; it has a 3.3 diameter ring on the point. The sword is considerably corroded and damaged even though it was initially placed in a wooden sheath covered with leather and fabric. According to these swords, these have straight blade with two edges and a hilt without guard. Though some authors have referred to them as “sabers”, this is a misleading description therefore the right use of term will make further research easier. As noted in Mongolian dictionary a sword is a double-edged, straight weapon made of steel that places in a sheath/scabbard. One Chinese source described the Xiongnu as “using bows and arrows from a distance, but using spears with iron handles when closer in”. An iron sword decorated with the Chinese characters “Zhong Dao” or “A Soldier’s Knife” has been found in the Baikal region, suggesting that the Xiongnu may sometimes have used Chinese-made weapons. Other Eurasian steppe warriors of the time, for example the Sarmatians, were skillful swordsmen who, on at least one occasion, defeated the Xiongnu. When fighting the Xiongnu the Sarmatians unleashed a hail storm of whistling arrows than closed to fight them with swords (N.E).
Iron sword-2, Khovd province
Artefacts of the Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu



T
171
T
ALKO-GRYNTSEVITCH, Yulian Dominikovich (18501936). Russian-Polish scholar. Physician, anthropologist, professor and statesman. When he was working in the Troitskosavoko-Kiakhta branch of the Russian Geographical Society in 1894-1908, he interested the archaeology by the influence of A.A.Bobrinsky, head of Archaeological Commission, and furthermore made some archaeological and anthropological surveys. He made an anthropological study among Mongol, Buriat, Yakut, Tungus and Russians and his scientific work “Mongolian and Transbaikalian Anthropological data” awarded by A.P.Rastsvetov grant and golden medal in 1902. His determinations to find the cultural and anthropological data of early human persuaded him to study burial funeral custom. Y.D.Talko-Gryntsevitch excavated the burials with stone mounds systematically, paid the attention to funeral custom, artifacts and bone remains and was published the result of the study. He divided the tombs excavated by him in Transbaikalia into 2 types, as the tombs with coffin and tombs with log chambers. After him the tombs with log chambers belong to Xiongnu period. He excavated about 100 tombs and published his report regularly. He departed to Poland in 1908 and became the professor of Krakow University. He wrote about 300 scientific articles on various subjects including
Xiongnu study. (M.Ts.)
TAMIRYN ULAAN KHOSHUU tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Mongolia. Located in the Tamir river valley, at 24 km distant from Ugii-Nuur soum of Arkhangai aimag. The red colored promontory on the north bank of the Tamir river is called Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu. This large Xiongnu cemetery situated in the east part of the promontory in both sides of a large ravine. There are about 287 burials identified. In some 8 km from the cemetery located 3 remains of earthen walled settlement. The bigger tomb diameter is 18 m, smaller one is 5-6 m, and average diameter is 8-10 m in this cemetery. The site discovered by Z.Batsaikhan in 2000, and 5 burials excavated in that year, more 9 burials excavated in 2001. Moreover, students of Department of History, Mongolian University of Pedagogy excavated 2 burials in 2001. Since 2005, the Mongolia-American joint expedition financed by Silk Road Foundation continued the excavations. (G.E.)
TANSHIHUAI (141-181 CE). Xianbei political and military leader. Was born in the family of a Xianbei general from East wing. Take leadership of Xianbei tribes when he was 15. During his reign Xianbei defeated Dingling in the north, Fuyu in the east, Wusun in the west and occupied whole ancient territory of Xiongnu. Founded the capital in Darkhan mountain, simultaneously stabilize his state attacked continuously to the Han. His son Huoliang succeeded him. (Ts.T.)
TAKHILTYN KHOTGOR tombs. Xiongnu period elite and common people mixed cemetery in Mongolia. Located at sandy valley of Takhiltyn Khotgor in Mankhan soum of Khovd province. This is the biggest Xiongnu site in western Mongolia. Discovered by Ts.Dorjsuren and V.V.Volkov in 1961, who were excavated 2 small tombs and published
Xiongnu tomb of the Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu



T
172
the results. There are all 60 burials in this site, where 3 elite tomb with 9 satellite burials and 2 sacrifial structures were are excavated. Mongolian archaeologist D.Navaan excavated 2 elite tombs in 1988-1990 and published a booklet o his research results. Stone mound of Tomb No.1 have had 12×16 m, and the funeral wooden construction appeared from the depth of 12 m, where discovered a part of hub of a wheel, bronze pieces of an umbrella, bronze pot, bronze plate, iron ring, spike, iron bit, iron knife and 2 earthen wares. D.Navaan concluded that south eastern group of tombs could belong the most early period in the cemetery consider the size, direction and state of deterioration stone mound. In 1990 he excavated another big elite tomb in Takhiltyn khotgor. During the excavation of the Tomb No.2, he started from the level ground of tomb in whole surface of stone mound, and built iron fence, and wooden closure in the 5m depth to avoid the stone collapsing in area of dromos. In the depth of 11 m the undamaged wooden log chamber with no trace of looting was appeared. He named the tomb as the “Tomb of the princess”, after determination of the sex and age of the deceased based on the artifacts and anthropological research. Number of artifacts, including wooden hub of a wheel, wooden cap of an umbrella, bronze teapot, bronze handle of the coffin, golden ring, iron bit are discovered. Since 2007,
the joint team of National Museum of Mongolia and Pennsylvania University, excavated 1 elite tomb, 7 satellite burial, dozens of sacrificing structures and published the result of the study. (Ch.Ye.)
TARVAGATAI tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Mongolia. Located at 20 km from the center of Zuil soum, of Uvurkhangai aimag. There is a total of 64 tombs which divided into 2 groups (north group with 20 tombs and south group with 44 tombs), 100 m distant from each other. All tombs have circular stone mound without any depressions in the center. Average diameter of tomb is 3-7 m. This site
Takhiltyn khotgor tomb excavation, Khovd province
Plan of the Tarvagatai site



T
173
discovered by Xiongnu study expedition in 1989 and there were excavated 2 tombs, which are located in the center of north group. (G.E.)
TASKIN, Vsevolod Sergeevich (1917-1995). Soviet historian and sinologist. Completed the gymnasium of Institute of Pedagogy, Harbin in 1933 and then studied at the University of Law in Harbin. When he takes a part in the lecture of N.K.Roerih about Central Asian nomads and from this his future work dedicated to the study of nomadic history had determined. Since 1957, he worked as a research fellow of Institute of Chinese Studies and his major work was the translation of Shiji of Sima Qian with R.V.Viyatkina. In 1968, he received Ph.D by the thesis of “Historical Data of Xiongnu (by Chinese sources)”. First parts of his dissertation focused to since establishing Xiongnu nomadic state in 209 BCE the struggle between Xiongnu and Han, and fall of the Xiongnu state. Second part contains history of Xiongnu since 209 BCE based by Chinese source data. These 2 parts of works related to his work “Historical source of Donghu group of nomads”, which is published in 1984. Also his translation of “History of Khidan state” of E Longli, which has appendix of Khidan and Mongolian sources. (M.Ts.)
TECHNOLOGY FOR CATTLE-BREEDING. According to the archaeological data there are two basic systems for Xiongnu animal husbandry which were nomadic and settled. There is no point to doubt that in Ivolga site there was practices settled animal husbandry because it was presented by solid archaeological materials. According to A.V.Davydova, in Ivolga settlement they used to pasture animals in nearby pastures for whole year but it seems that they used to cut hay for young animals. It is sure that barrow and hillside near the settlement was used for pasture. But it is hard to define where the fences for animals were and Ivolga “small settlement” may was used to be for that purpose. This small settlement was used to be protected by short ground wall as same as the main settlement. No dwellings were found by excavation but very few animal bones were found. According to the Xiongnu animal husbandry as it was described in Shiji, that “Xiongnu move about in search of water and pasture and have no walled cities or fixed dwellings, nor do they engage in any kind of agriculture”. Additionally we have information that Chinese eunuch Zhonghang Yue who submitted to Xiongnu in 174 BCE said “the people eat the flesh of their domestic animals, drink their milk, and wear their hides, while the animals graze from
place to place, searching for pasture and water”. But there is no historical records about what type of migration they practiced. According to ethnographical data Mongolian nomads generally moved quiet near distances. Mongolia historian G.Sukhbaatar assumed that migration type of Xiongnu was nearly same to Mongolian nomad’s migration type and there are following reasons to approve that. This are: 1) their main place was same in Mongolia; 2) herd structure is almost same to Mongolian herd structure; 3) they had many carts and few camels; 4) it doesn’t says in Chinese historical source that they move far. The main types of nomads unit of Xiongnu was defense administrative unit. In “Hou Han Shu” it was mentioned that 58 “bu” (literally “tribe”), such as Qiulan, Chubi and Huduxui came with totally 200.000 people and 8000 best soldiers in 87 CE. “Bu” doesn’t mean any ethnic unity such clans and tribes and it means pasture land and seasonal migration parcels. It was an administrative unit similar to Chinggis khan period and had approximately 3500 people which are not group of nomads or families, which was so-called “khuree” (literally- surrounded group). Ancient Mongolian nomads used to use the pasture appropriately for cattle breeding and in other words they had an appropriate process of animal husbandry which is a main materialistic production. Xiongnu were used to dig well and water the land to extend the pasture size. For example, there is information that in 80 BCE they dug hundreds of wells and also in 79 BCE they built bridge in river Yuwu. (Ts.T.)
TEMPORARY DEFENSE FORTRESSES. One of the major subject of the Xiongnu study is the identification of Chanyu palaces, which were the center of administrative policy. However there are some sources about Longting or Longcheng palace of Xiongnu rulers, but not discovered yet by archaeological investigation. Mongolian archaeologist Kh.Perlee suggested that Chanyu palace consisted of palace complex and ritual structures and, moreover there were existed south, north and summer palaces of Chanyu. According to Shiji: “In the First month of the new year, big lords of Xiongnu come together in a Small meeting at the Chanyu palace to perform sacrifices, and in the Fifth month a Great meeting is held at Longcheng at which sacrifices are conducted to the Xiongnu ancestors, Heaven and Earth, and Gods and Spirits. In the Autumn, when the horses are fat, another Great meeting is held at the Dailin, when a reckoning is made of the number of persons



T
174
and animals”. This source says that there were more than two or three palaces of Chanyu. Mongolian scholars have been considered that it is clear the there might existed most significant and biggest city of Xiongnu the Longcheng (Dragon city) palace near Karakorum, but no source about the character of this palace or city and no archaeological evidence is yet revealed. Most of archaeologists adhere the Orkhon valley as the political center of Xiongnu, but archaeologist Z.Batsaikhan locates the Xiongnu political center in the region of Khentii mountain range and Tuul and Kherlen river basin. He assumes that “all consider that Chanyu palace had been in Khangay mountain range and Orkhon river valley but no one analyzed historical and archaeological data accurately, simply have been used Chinese interpreters view on Longcheng city as situated in Orkhon valley, but it is clear that Longcheng is not a particular city in particular region. Actually in Mongolia have been discovered several earthen walled fortresses with similar structures undoubtedly related to Xiongnu period as follows: • Undur Dov fortress in Erdene soum of Tuv aimag; • Khureen Dov fortress in Mungunmorit soum of Tuv aimag; • Tsenkheryn Gol fortress, located in the margin of Delgerkhaan and Jargaltkhaan soum of Khentii aimag; • Bars Khot –II or Baruun Duruu fortress in Tsagaan-Ovoo soum of Dornod aimag; • Khermen tal fortresses in Ugii-Nuur soum of Arkhangai aimag. None of them is identified as Longcheng or Dailin city. The common feature of these fortresses are the four sided walls built by tramped; each side of walls in different sizes; and one or more construction traces in the center or corner of the fortress. Moreover, there are traces of 1-4 entrances of side walls. It is interesting that there are not enough building materials found from the excavation of these fortresses, thus these are considered as provisional defense fortresses. The core of the ramparts was the balks, and most of the buildings were constructed also in wood which explains the absence of construction materials inside the ramparts. Archaeological materials are scanty from excavations made it difficult to study, but these defense walls considered as a seasonal camps or warfare protection for ruling elite of Xiongnu state. (Ts.T.)
TERELJIIN DURVULJIN site. Xiongnu
period settlement in Mongolia. Located at Terelj river valley, right tributary of Kherlen river, in Mungunmorit soum of Tuv aimag. Discovered by B.Y.Vladimirtsov in 1925, who conducted small scale excavation and discovered ceramic rooftiles and other construction materials. In 1949 S.V.Kiselev examined the materials collected in the Tereljiin Durvuljin and dated the site to Xiongnu period. Mongolian archaeologist Kh.Perlee conducted another small scale excavation in 1952, and found roof-tiles and earthen bricks similar to
Gua Dov site. The fortress has four sided earthen ramparts, surrounded by water ditch and has four gates. Rampart size is 235×235m, actual height of rampart is 50-80cm, width is 10-15 m, ditch width is 3-6 m, depth is 30-70 cm. There are 2 remains of building sized 20×30 m and 30×55 m. Height of them is 2.5-3.0 m and 2-2.5 m. Another two smaller building remains found in connection with bigger buildings, measured 15×17 m and 20×25 m. In total Kh.Perlee excavated 9 trenches of different sizes. Recently a Mongol-Russian joint archaeological expedition conducted large scale excavation at the central mound of the fortress and discovered interesting architectural features and nimber of archaeological findings. (Ts.E.)
TEVSH UUL tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in Mongolia. Located in the south slope terrace of Duutiin Zuun Buuz hill of Tevsh mountain, in the north-east of Bogd soum of Uvurkhangai aimag. There are identified about 200 burials and kereksurs and some rock art sites dated back from Bronze Age to Medieval period. 22 Xiongnu tombs are situated in 2 groups in 150 m from each other, and 8 tombs in the north group, 14 tombs in the group. Several times held excavations in this
Pottery piece of the Tereljiin durvuljin



T
175
site in 1971, 1972, 1977. In 1971 V.V.Volkov and D.Navaan excavated 3 tombs in the North-East and North-West sides of South group of tombs, later D.Tseveendorj excavated 4 tombs in 1972 and 4 tombs in 1977. (G.E.)
TIANSHAN Mountain. Mountain mentioned in history in relation with Xiongnu history. According to the historical facts, Tainshan mountain is the mountain system, which consisted as the Karliktag of Hami city, Machin mountain, Barkul range, Sertseg, Bor, Khar and Majinshan mountains. Moreover, Qilinshan mountain (present Richthofen range) of Xiongnu considered as Tianshan mountain and West wing aristocrats of Xiongnu were settled down in Tianshan mountain. (B.B.)
TIANYAN Mountain. Mountain mentioned in history in relation with Xiongnu history. Some Mongolian scholars believe that Khurkh range in Nomgon soum of Umnugobi aimag is the historic Tianyan Mountain, although previous scholars view it in the south of Khangai range. Most recent outlook says that is one of small mountain near the border of Mongolia and Inner Mongolia. See also Zhaoxin city and Zhiqiu river. (B.B.)
TIAN GUANGJING. Chinese archaeologist and Xiongnu scholar. Was born in Liaoning province, China in 1938. Completed the Beijing University majored in archaeology, and was the Director of the Institute of Archaeology and Cultural Heritage of Inner Mongolia. He was the head of the expedition to the study of Taohongbala, Xigoupan, Maoqingou and Zhukaigou Xiongnu tombs and wrote several works as ”Recent Archaeological Study of Inner
Mongolia” (Kaogu, 1986, Vol. 1), “Zhukaigou site in Inner Mongolia” (Kaogu, 1988, No. 3), “Xiongnu tombs in Taohongbala site” (Kaogu, 1976, Vol. 1), “Origin of Ordos Bronze Tools” (Kaogu, 1988, Vol. 3), “Bronze Tools of Ordos” (Beijing, 1986), “Early Cultural Study of Southeast Region of Inner Mongolia” (Beijing, 1991). (B.A.)
TIELE. Name of the ancientTurkic speaking tribes confederation. Recorded first time in 4th century in Chinese sources. Original name is Oguz or Tokuz (nine)-Oguz. In 391, defeated by Tabgach, and by Rouran in 403. Turks considered Oguz as their people. But Oguz had been tried to establish their own state continuously and in 630 Xueyanto (Syr-Tardush) founded their own empire. Later, in 657 one of bigger tribe of Oguz established the state under the leadership of Tumidu Elbeter of Uighur. (Ts.T.)
TOKHOIN BALAR (TAOHONGBALA) tombs. Xiongnu period cemetery in China. Discovered by chance in Khiangan banner of Ikh Zuu aimag, Inner Mongolia in 1973. Inner Mongolia Cultural heritage study team excavated here 6 burials. The site comprises six single burials, oriented to the north, located on a plateau with the elevation of 1500 m. All graves are rectangular pits, only tomb has a niche at wall. The deceased were lied down in his back and oriented to the north. Average tomb size: 1.7-3 meters in length, 0,9-1.2 m wide on the surface, 0.45-0,65 m wide on the bottom. Animal bones were placed above the head of the deceased; bits were found between the horses’ teeth, bridle ornaments on either side of the horses’ skulls. Only tomb contained horse gear. Distribution of animal bones is described as: Tomb No.1 - 9 horse skulls, 4 cattle skulls, 2 sheep skulls; Tomb No.2 - 42 sheep skulls, 3 horse skulls, 4 cattle skulls, 16 cattle hooves, all placed on the niche. Nomadic nature of artifacts as bronze dagger, battle pick, bronze belt adornments are discovered. Likewise some objects indicating Chinese influences, such as iron knife and silk fabrics are unearthed also in this site. Tian Guangjin and Guo Suxin dated the tombs to the Late Springs-Autumns or Early Warring States (early 5th c. BCE). Later the tombs are dated to 6-5th and 5-4th c. BCE by radiocarbon analyze. (Ch.S.)
Plan of the Tevsh Uul site



T
176
“TOMB OF A XIONGNU PRINCE IN SUDZI”. Author P.B.Konovalov. Ulan-Ude, 2008. Written by the data of tomb No.54 of Ilmovaya Pad’ site, which excavated by archaeological expedition of Institute if Social Science, Buriatia in 1970-1975. The tomb No. 54 size is 21-22x18-19 m, entrance-dromus is 15-17 m, and 2 small satellite burials found at the right and left side. Author consider that Xiongnu funeral custom influenced by Chinese and connected to their Chinese origin. (Na.B.)
TONGWANGCHENG city. The ruin of this city located at Jingbian xian, Shaangxi province. It was the capital of Great Xia dynasty, founded by South Xiongnu ruler Heliang Bobo in 5th c. CE. Consisted of Right and Left walled city. Walls have 4 gates and 4 watch towers. The ruin of the city kept bad, only remained earthen configuration in the center. Museum of Shaanxi and Society for Cultural Heritage Protection of Shaanxi Province made a survey around the city and drawn the general map in 1956. In 2002, Institute of Archaeology of Shaangxi province excavated first time and studied structures of the city and building distribution accurately and discovered some artifacts related to Han and Xiongnu culture. (Ch.S.)
TUOBA GUI (371-409). Founder of Wei dynasty, honor name is Daowu emperor. Birth name is Shegui. When Jin defeating Dai dynasty of Tuoba in 376, he was chosen as a khan. In 383, when falling down Jin, he re-established his state and changed the state name as Wei. In 397 he defeated Yan dynasty and reigned himself as an emperor. However he was a talented general, but was a cruel emperor. He killed in 409 by his son Tuoba Xiao. His son Tuoba Xi succeeded him. (Ts.T.)
TUOBA TAO (408-452). Honor name is Taiwu emperor of Wei. Succeeded his father Tuoba Xi. During his reign the territory expanded two times more. His reign was the last period of 16 States period. He was a devout Taoist, under the influence of his prime minister Cui Hao, and in 444, at Cui Hao’s suggestion and believing that Buddhists had supported the rebellion against government, he ordered the abolition of Buddhism, at the penalty of death. This was the first of the Three Disasters of Wu for Chinese Buddhism. Late in his reign, his reign began to be cruel, and his people were also worn out by his incessant external wars. In 452, he was assassinated by his eunuch, who put his son Tuoba Yu on the throne but then assassinated Tuoba Yu as well. His grandson Tuoba Jun succeeded him. (Ts.T.)
TUOBA YILU (?-316). He was the ruler of west side of Tuoba/Tabgach in 295-307, chef of whole Tabgach in 307-316, Wang of Dai state in 310315, and Emperor of Tuoba Dai Dynasty in 315316. His father Tuoba Shamohang, brothers Lui Guang, Tuoba Yito and Tuoba Fu were also influent chieftains. Tuoba Lui Guang divided his state into 3 parts in 295, west wing for Yilu, east wing for Lui Guang and central part for Yito. Yilu expanded his land for the gain of West wing and won several battles against Xiongnu and Wuhuan. Moreover he defeated Liu Han dynasty with his brother Tuoba Yito in 304. After passed away Yito in 305 and Lui Guang in 307, he remained only ruler of Tabgach. His son Tuoba Pugen succeeded him. (Ts.T.)
TUOBA WEI EMPIRE (386-534 CE). XianbeiChines empire in North of China. Also called as Great Wei, Northern Wei or Yuan Wei. The state founded by ancient Tuoba-Tabgach tribes in Northern China, and famous for wide spread of Buddhism and unification of the Northern China. Famous sites as Yonggan, Longmen, Dunhuang cave, very famous for their Buddhist content have kept until today. The Tuoba leader Yilu get the Dai wang rank in 315, and his descendant Tuoba Gui changed his rank as Wei wang and founded the new state. Tuoba Gui won the Rouran leader Hedohang and took control on the south of Gobi in 391. In 399, he reigned himself as an emperor. Wei developed much, but Tabgach and Xianbei were sinicized more by Chinese type of attitude. Also they supported to get Yuan clans` name and living among Chinese and Tabgach clothes and language were prohibited. Xiao Wendi emperors` decision to move the capital to Luoyang influenced much to sinicizing. Since that time Buddhist temples and lamas increased and persuaded more. At the beginning of Wei state paid an attention to protection of north border, but more sinicized Wei state underestimated the traditional nomadic army compliment. Consequently, the conflict arisen between army staffs and palace sinicized bureaucrats. The rebellion originated in 6 main garrisons in 523, and broaden to all borders for 10 years. General Erzhu Rong seized the throne and executed the queen and her puppet emperors and their 2000 palace functionaries in February 13, 528. However general Erzhu Rong suppressed the rebellion but killed by new emperor Xiao Zhuang in 520. During this time, general Gao Huan and general Yuwen Tai took control in the west and east part of the state and as a result the state divided by 2 independent states in 534. (Ts.T.)



T
177
TUFA Clan. The name of a tribe releted to Xiongnu and Xianbei. Mongolian historian G.Sukhbaatar consider that the words “Tuoba”, “Tefu” and “Tufa” are similar names and originated from kinship relationship between Xiongnu and Xianbei. Tufa is the clans` name and later formed as tribe name. (S.U.)
TRADE. The biggest market of the foreign trade in the Xiongnu period was Han Dynasty. But the real demand or inquire to make the trade between both countries was unequal. All researchers do not agree with each other on two different reasons to limit the normal relationship for trade between them. The first reason is quite objective. Because the Chinese did not have much demand to consume the nomads’ goods and they did not have any trouble especially for the existence without the nomads’ goods. On the other hand, the nomads had a big demand to consume the Chinese goods. This caused an aggravation of the appropriate ratio of demands and supply for the nomads as well as some big conflicts then. The second reason is the Chinese limited the trade and intended to use it as a mean for their political purpose. So the conflicts above mentioned, got more acute. The nomads tried to run agriculture and to make the products from the craft in several times in history. But naturally, the continental severe climate did not give a chance to develop the agriculture in the wide scale. So they needed to import some kinds of agricultural products from the agriculture neighbors. Actually, the nomads wanted to make the trade, while the merchants wanted much profit from the trade. G.Sukhbaatar mentioned that the trade between Xiongnu and Han in 80s BCE recorded in a book Yan Tie Lun (Discources on Salt and Iron) that the “ordinary people bought many precise products of Xiongnu which cost gold, with Chinese simple silk. This was the way to reduce the resource of the enemy. In this way, donkey, mule and camel caravans went in file to the border; wild horse and piebald horse got owned by us, sable, marmot, fox, badger, felt and quilted mattress were transformed
into the state funds and fade, jade, coral and crystal were belonged to us. This was the fact that foreign products flow into the state funds and the domestic profit not to slip out of the dynasty”. It can be said that as for the nomads, the trade with Han Dynasty did not make an economical profit but merely demand and supply of them. On the contrary, it gave a big profit and sometimes huge for China. So Chinese merchants looked for more ways to make the trade with the nomads, although being officially prohibited. G.Sukhbaatar appropriately formulated the official trade made under the Chinese state control as “The Palace Trade”. But the Chinese contrabands made an “Unseen trade” widely. As an example, 500 Chinese contrabands were executed in Chang’an city, the capital of Han Dynasty in 121 BCE, due to their unseen trade with the Xiongnu. In addition, it was impossible to prohibit the unseen trade along the border which extends thousands miles. Setsen Jagchid demonstrated that large number
Lacquered wooden bowl, Noyon Uul, Tomb 5
Glass bowl, Satellite 30, Gol Mod-II site
Bronze cauldron with tripod stand, Duulga Uul, Tomb 9



T
178
of contraband-merchants crossed the frontier illegally and made a trade frequently. Bilateral trade was profitable for not only the nomads but also for China. Even though Chinese government prohibited the trade and tried to deny the objective demand, China needed some goods of the nomads, particularly horses. H.J.Creel wrote about the Chinese demand of the
horses in the period of Han Dynasty as “China needed horses indeed. In early period of Han, the horses are reduced in its numbers caused by internal wars. According to Nancy LeSwan, supplying the army with horse was a big depress for the State funds of Han Dynasty. Since China had no adequate horses, they were not able to launch war against Xiongnu”. In the note written by Ying Shiyu, who did research work on conquer and trade during Han Dynasty, conquer and trade were theatrically not the same, but not distinguished from each other during this period of time by Chinese court. Also I.Ya.Zlatkin and O. Lattimore emphasize that the trade made with the nomads was resulted from political ambitions to conquer or control. Setsen Jagchid proved the government controlled official trade with foreigners and used it to carry out the political policies. The Chinese government intended to give a permission to developing neighbors to import Chinese culture and goods on only case that they accepted the China superiority. But “Hou Han Shu” compiled by Ban
Gu, says the Chinese government restricted the relationship between the nomadic and sedentary people although the first one accepted the Chinese dominance. However the Chinese government tried to control the trade with the nomads, they opened a market along the border under the pression from the nomads indeed. In accordance with Shiji, after the incident of Mayi city in 133 BCE, the Xiongnu “broke off friendly relations with the Han, and began to attack the border defences wherever they happened to be. Time and again they crossed the frontier and carried out innumerable plundering raids. At the same time they continued to be as greedy as ever, delighting in the border markets and longing for Han goods, and the Han for its part continued to allow them to trade in the markets in order to sap their resources”. In the pleasant situation, XiongnuChina cross border trade was likely in wide scale. In 84 CE, 10.000 heads of cows and horses were driven from Xiongnu to the frontier market. Some archeological materials also prove it. For example, S.I.Rudenko reveals the quantity and quality of the Chinese made luxurious properties, found from the Xiongnu
graves of elite in his work on Noyon Uul tombs. Also some Chinese made silk, lacquer objects and bronze mirror are found from the grave of common people. The fact that the consumption of Chinese goods became very common in the Xiongnu is confirmed by the graves of ordinary men which were covered with the precious fabrics like silk. The Xiongnu were over-affected by the importing goods that
Piece of bronze mirror
Silver decoration with Greece gods Piece of bronze mirror
Noyon Uul, Tomb 20



T
179
caused endanger of losing national culture and particularity. The trade between Xiongnu and China was mostly barter exchanges, but it is used some coins as trading equivalent in some cases. There are some facts that Han Dynasty gave hundreds of thousands of bronze coins to the Xiongnu as gift. In 52 BCE, 20 jin (meas.) of gold and 200.000 bronze coins were sent from China to Xiongnu. In regarding with this fact, a silk purse found from the 6th grave of the Noyon Uul is very interesting. The Xiongnu established the relationship of trade with the neighbors except for China, as instance, there is a fact that they made a trade with Wuhuan. (Ts.T.)
TSARAM tombs. Xiongnu period elite and common people mixed burial site in Buriatia. Located 1.5 km to the east of Naushki, Khyakhta town of Buriat Republic, Russian Federation. In 1899 local explorer Yu.D.Talko-Grinchevich discovered this burial site. Out of total 20 burials, 5 were excavated by Ya.E.Smolev in 1903. All excavated tombs were looted in ancient times. The burials were defined as Xiongnu burial with wooden coffin based on their inner structure. In 1997-2005, Transbaikalia Expedition headed by S.S.Minyaev excavated the biggest burial, numbered as No.7, located in the north side within the framework of their study on Tsaram. The burial had 5 accompanying tombs on the east and west side and the excavation were carried out on these as well. The total area of excavation was 7.700 sq m. Based on the materials of the study on skeleton remain from the accompanying burials, it is concluded that these were the burials of the sacrificial characters for the tomb No.7. The structure and the findings from the tomb No.7 were identical to the most of the elite burials excavated in Mongolia. The exterior structure of the tomb has rectangular shape and measured by 32×32 м, 1,5 m high, in the middle was a depression of 2,5 m deep, in the south there is 20 m long entrance-dromos. Wooden funeral chamber was found in 17 m depth and was 170-180 cm high. Chinese style bronze mirror, chariot with booth, decorated with bronze and silver pieces of ibex pattern, lacquer ware with
hieroglyphs, birch bark vessel and “funeral doll” were discovered from this burial. Researchers date this burial back to 1st c. BCE – 1st c. CE, based on the study of the Chinese Han period bronze mirror and other artifacts and conclude that this must be a burial of one of the Xiongnu high elite. This is the biggest Xiongnu tomb excavated actually in Transbaikalia. (Ch.Ye.)
TSENKHERIIN GOL settlement. Xiongnu period walled fortress in Mongolia. Located between Jargaltkhaan and Delgerkhaan soums of Khentii aimag. Kh.Perlee measured its size and structure in 1955. The fortress rampart is 300×300 m. An earthen mound measured 25×25 m is situated in the center of the fortress. It was defined as belonging to Xiongnu period based on the observations on the structure of the fortress. Soviet scholar K.V.Vyatkina also defined it to be of Xiongnu period. (Ts.E.)
TSEVEENDORJ, Damdinsurengiin. Mongolian archaeologist, Xiongnu scholar. Honored scientific fellow of Mongolia, Sc.D and Professor. Professor of the Arizona University of USA, received Honorary Doctorate from the European Institute of Chinese studies of the Kingdom of Belgium, Professor of the University of Aktau, Kazakstan, Correspondent member of Archaeological Institute of Federal Republic of Germany, Professor of the College de France. Born in 1949 in Undursant soum, Arkhangai aimag. Graduated from the M.V.Lomonosov State University of the USSR as an archaeologist in 1972. Prof. D.Tseveendorj started working in the Institute of Archaeology since 1972, as a assistant research fellow and gradually being promoted to senior fellow and leading research fellow posts; then in 1989, he became the head of an Archaeological sector and a project manager; since 1999 he worked



T
180
as a Director of the Archaeological Research Center; and in 2002 he founded the Institute of Archaeology and working as the Director of this institute since then. He was awarded Ph.D. in History for his dissertation “Chandmani burials” in 1983 and defended Sc.D degree for his work “History of Ancient Art of Mongolia” in 1998. The publications written as well as coauthored by him, such as “Mongolian Paleolithic” (UB.,1978), “Culture of Chandmani” (UB., 1980), “Prehistoric Art of Mongolia” (UB.,1983), “History of Ancient Art of Mongolia” (UB.,1999), “Historical and Cultural Heritages of Mongolia” (UB.,1999), “Paleolithic and Neolithic period of Northern part of the Lake valley” (Novosibirsk, 2000), “Mongolie du Nord-Ouest. Tsagaan Salaa/ Baga Oigor” (Paris, 2001), “Petroglyphs of Aral Tolgoi” (UB.,2005), “Mongolie du Nord-Ouest. Haut Tsagaan Gol” (Paris, 2006) were published in Mongolia and in many other countries and contributed significantly to the development of archeological study in Mongolia. He has written 30 monographs, around 400 scientific articles and co-authored 26 publications. Since 1980, D.Tseveendorj excavated by himself and also led archaeological excavation on the burial site and settlement ruins from Xiongnu period in Chandmani Uul, Tevsh Uul, Onitolt, Solbi Uul, Khuushiin Khutul, Khudgiin Tolgoi, Naimaa Tolgoi, Khirgist Khooloi, Baruun Khairhan, Sul Tolgoi, Morin Tolgoi, Bayanbulag and Boroo settlements with archaeologists from Hungary and Russia. Numerous scientific articles and work were published. These archaeological excavations played considerable role in the development of Xiongnu studies in Mongolia. Furthermore, his theoretical work on the origin of Xiongnu, Xiongnu culture is well-known among scholars. His discovery of the oldest jews harp from the burial of Morin Tolgoi Mountain was not only valuable to define the Xiongnu culture and art, but this discovery is noteworthy for the world history of art and culture. (Ts.Т.)
TUQI WANG. Tuqi or Tutsi and “Eastern Tuci wang” is the high rank among Xiongnu elite, only after the Chanyu himself. Generally attributed to the eldest son of Chanyu. There are some opinion for the meaning of this title, but most spread explanation is to tie with a title “Tushee”. This word has been used commonly for later period Mongols, as for example “Tusheet khan”, one of the most influent lord in Mongolia during 15-20th centuries. (No.B)
TUMEN CHANYU (?-209 BCE). First Xiongnu Chanyu mentioned in the history. Qin general Meng Tian defeated Xiongnu and occupied some part of Ordos and south part of Yingshan mountain by 300.000 warriors in 215-214 BC. This defeat was effected much to social and economic situation of Xiongnu, and the strong and determinate leader was required in that time. Maodun, the eldest son of Tumen emerged by this social demand. According to Maodun Chanyus legend, his father imagined as recreant one, and during his reign Xiongnu was lost their father land the Ordos. Killed by his first son Maodun Chanyu. (Ts.T.)
TURBAT Tsagaan. Mongolian archaeologist and Xiongnu scholar. Was born in 1973 in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia. Completed the National University of Mongolia in 1995. Since 1995 working as research fellow in the Institute of History, Mongolian Academy of Sciences, since 2000 lecturer in the Department of History, Mongolian University of Education and since 2007, senior researcher and then the head of the Bronze and Early Iron Age Department of the Institute of Archaeology, Mongolian Academy of Sciences. Received Ph.D by the thesis of “Burial Custom of the Xiongnu common people” in 2004, and associate professor of the Mongolian University of Education in 2006. He is the author and the co-author of scientific works as “Archaeological Sites of the Egyin Gol Valley” (UB,.2003), “Common Burials of the Xiongnu” (UB., 2004), “Archaeological sites of Mongol Altai-1, Bayan-Ulgii province” (UB., 2009), “Deer stones of Jargalantyn Am” (UB., 2011) and published 6 monographs, 4 collective works, 2 historical sources, 2 teaching manuals, 70 scientific articles and 40 conference presentations. Moreover, his articles about Burkhan Tolgoi site of Egiin Gol, funerals of Tamiryn Ulaan Khoshuu and some articles, and the dissertation which is studied the common funeral custom of Xiongnu systematically are connected directly to Xiongnu study. He published the bibliography of Xiongnu studies containing about 700 works in 2009. In 2005-2008, he was the head



T
181
of the Mongolia-Swiss joint expedition and made the excavation at Boroo settlement, and collected huge data on Xiongnu settlement, agriculture and culture of manufacture. (Ch.Ye.)
TURKIC EMPIRE (552-745). Nomadic empire in Central Asia founded by ancient Turkic tribes. Some scholars believe that the ancient Turks were the direct descendants of Xiongnu, but the ethnic affiliation of Xiongnu and Turks is still under discussion. The core of the Turk tribe was the Ashina clan, which consisted by the mis of Xiongnu and Xianbei people in 2nd c. CE, defeated by Rourans when moving around the Turfan in 460 CE, and settled in the Altai. After that they united Tele tribes and founded powerful tribal confederation. In 552, Turkic tribes ruler Buman declared himself as a kagan and upraised the rebellion against their former suzerain Rouran khanate. They united all Inner Asian nomads until 555 and conquered Central Asia successfully and reached to Black sea. Since that time Zhou and Qi dynasties from North China became the subjects
of the Turkic empire. In 603, Turkic Empire separated by Western and Eastern Turkic, and Eastern Turkic Empire exterminated by Tang dynasty of China. Turkic rebels, who headed by Kutlug founded own independent state in 682. After Bilge khans reigning Turkic Empire exterminated by Uighurs rebellion in 745. Turkic Empire played important role in historical and cultural development of Eurasian nomadic nations and Turkic name and language spread widely. Different global religions as Shamanism, Buddhism, Manichaeism and Nestorianism spread in Turkic Empire, and many merchants, missionaries and religious leaders frequented the Turkic imperial court. They created their own alphabet called Runic inscription. The main source of living was nomadic cattle-breeding among the ancient Turks. They leaved outstanding archaeological culture, including anthropomorphic stone statues, tombs and ritual monuments in Mongolian territory. (Ts.T.)



U
182



U
183
U
IGHURS. Ancient Turkic speaking tribe in Central Asia. In traditional historiography the origin of Uighur people related to Xiongnu or Dingling. They were included in the tribal confederation of Gaoche in the 3-4th centuries. Since beginning of 5-6th centuries followed Rouran domination and named as Tele. But they were called themselves Oguz. During the Turkic Empire majority of its population consisted of Oguz tribes. They made the uprising against Turkic hegemony and won its independence in 742. During the reign of Moyunchur kagan, Uighur territory expanded from Manchuria to Altai Mountains in 750-760s. His southern neighbor the Tang dynasty paid important quantity of tribute to Uighur empire. In 840, Kirgiz attacked unexpectedly and occupied Ordubalyk city, most of Uighurs moved to the south and re-established the Khanate near Turfan. Their descendants still living present day Xinjiang, China. There are many ruin of cities, burials and tombs, stone statues, steles with inscription of Uighur Empire period remained in Mongolia. They are preeminent from previous nomadic nations by their high developed urban culture and well established cultural and economic relationship to Sogdia and China. (Ts.T.)
UMEHARA, Sueji. Japanese archaeologist. Specialist of early history of Far-East and East Asia. Was born in 1893, in Osaka. Since 1939, worked as a professor in Kyoto University. Made archaeological surveys in Korea and China. He excavated An’yan site of Yin capital, Tongpei site and Nannan site in Korea. His major research work concerning the Xiongnu culture is “Studies of Noin-Ula finds in North Mongolia” (Tokyo, 1960). During his research trip to Russian Academy of Sciences in 1928, Russian scholars proposed him to study Noyon Uul artifacts. At the end of 1930’s he made detailed description of objects and many years later he had the opportunity to published his research results in Japan with 100 pages text, 18 pages of English abstract, 83 photos and 69 hand drawings. (G.E.)
UNDUR DOV Settlement. Xiongnu period walled fortress in Mongolia. Located at the south of a place named Ayga Shanaga, between the Ikh Gun and Baga Gun lakes near Kherlen river, Erdene soum of Tuv aimag. B.Y.Vladimirtsov discovered this site and made small scale excavation in 1925, but could not determine the chronology of the site. In 1950s`, Mongolian archaeologist Kh.Perlee made a map of this site and conducted also a small scale excavation. This site encircled by 4 sided earth wall, the west wall is 285 m, east wall is 292 m, north wall is 255 m, south wall is 285 m long, in average 30-35 cm high, 2-3 m width. On the ramparts remained donjons, 3 in the west, 6 in the west, 8 in the north, 5 in the south and in 4 corners of the rampart were bigger donjons. The easrthen mound named Undur Dov by local herders situated in the south of above mentioned rampart and sized 18×20 m in area and 1.35 m in height. (Ts.E.)
UNICORN. Scholars believe that the animal style in precious metals ornamentation first appeared circa 2nd millennium BCE as a result of internal developments among the peoples of Central Asia. Artifacts crafted in the animal style continue to be found throughout Bronze Age contexts as well as flourished in graves of the Xiongnu and developed in periods thereafter. Eight sites containing the monumental tombs of Xiongnu nobility have been discovered across Mongolia and South Siberia, and investigations of these sites have intensified since the year 2000. As an example of animal style



U
184
in Xiongnu art, this article presents the decoration on silver ornaments unearthed from Tomb 20 at the aristocratic cemetery of Gol Mod located in Arkhangai aimag of central Mongolia. In 2005, the Mongolian-French expedition unearthed several pieces of silver decorated ornaments featuring bas-relief images of unicorns, in both round and elongated shapes, found on the eastern side of the burial chamber of Tomb 20. Six elongated silver ornaments, in the form of edged ovals, were unearthed, each weighing 92 g and measuring 14.3 cm long, being 0.4 cm thick, and 7 cm wide at the lower portion. Ornaments with silver and gold plating were made by bending the thin metal foil around the edges of an iron backing. On the obverse side, within 0.5 cm-wide frames, are depicted in bas-relief the profiles of an artiodactyla, with the front right and rear left legs rampant. Although the torso of the beast resembles
a deer, it has an equine head. From the forehead grows a huge horn with crooked tip. Hairs are depicted protruding along the mane as well as from the chin and below the chest of the animal. Of the six discovered silver ornaments, three of the beasts are depicted facing right and three are depicted facing left. Eight silver ornaments of rounded shape were unearthed, featuring the same unicorn beast, each weighs 9–12 g, measures 4.3 cm in diameter and is 0.4 cm thick. The ornaments are crafted in the same silverwrapped iron plate method as the elongated ones. On these round ornaments the unicorn is depicted as lying with legs crossed under the torso and the tail rampant. Unlike the other depictions of the unicorn, the beast on the round ornaments has winglets on its chest and hips. Of the eight discovered round silver ornaments, four of the beasts are depicted facing right and four are depicted facing left. Clouds
Unicorn, Gol mod, Tomb 20
Unicorn, Gol mod, Tomb 20



U
185
are also shown on either side of the beast, beside the horn and beneath the legs, perhaps with the purpose of conveying the other-worldly, celestial nature of this creature. Like the other unicorn depictions, the very realistically illustrated torso of the beast resembles an antelope (capra sp.), mountain gazelle (gazella), or deer, but has an equine head. Most likely this is the depiction of a “belget göröös” (“hallowed gazella”), a nonexistent mythical unicorn creature. The presence of an equal number of beasts facing in opposing directions (right and left), clearly show that these decorative silver ornaments were paired as parts of a single pendant chain. The decorated silver ornaments unearthed from numerous Xiongnu noble tombs are of two shapes the elongated and the rounded. Some are plated with gold, and one was discovered as a single piece crafted from bronze. Scholars have paid attention to the form of their decorative motifs, but have not addressed the specific purpose, utility, or meaning of these artifacts. This article attempts to clarify the function of these items and some issues of the significance related to the “belget guroos” unicorn. Silver ornaments identical to those found at Gol Mod were discovered in large quantities from the tombs of Xiongnu nobility across Mongolia and Buriatia, including tomb 6 and tomb 20 at Noyon Uul necropolis as well as tomb 7 in the Tsaram necropolis. Similar artifacts have also been unearthed from tombs other than the royal Xiongnu burials. Chalcedonyinserted elongated artifacts discovered in graves tombs of Nangnang culture in the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea were identified by Korean scholars as decorations of horse crupper. In addition, Han dynasty tombs have yielded
similar fan-shaped decorative pieces, often called “silver petals”, which Chinese scholars have identified as the items of a horse harness. These were found in princely tombs of far south China in Shizhaishan, Yunnan province and Putuo, Guangxi as well as a tomb in central China at Xiaomintun, Henan. Also, rounded-shaped decorations were depicted on the porcelain figures of horses dating back to the period of the Northern Qi dynasty.
